Katalog Berita (Halaman 28/57)

#5977. The New Congress Can Repair Civil-Military Relations

Since its founding in 1922, Foreign Affairs has been the leading forum for serious discussion of American foreign policy and global affairs. The magazine has featured contributions from many leading international affairs experts.

PETER D. FEAVER is Professor of Political Science and Public Policy at Duke University and the author of Thanks for Your Service: The Causes and Consequences of Public Confidence in the U.S. Military.

HEIDI URBEN is Professor of the Practice and Associate Director of Georgetown University’s Center for Security Studies in the Walsh School of Foreign Service.

How It Can Reclaim Its Constitutional Powers

Article I of the Constitution gives Congress sweeping powers to raise, maintain, and equip the armed forces. But the current Congress has done little to use these powers. The intense, frenetic activity of the past two years has undone established norms for military professionalism and may shape civil-military relations for a generation. Yet it has been led almost entirely by the executive branch with little meaningful involvement or pushback from Capitol Hill.

The next Congress will face great pressure to reverse course and in doing so could shore up the historical norms of American civil-military relations. The concern today is not a military that is defying civilians—the traditional bogeyman of American political leaders since the founding of the Republic. Rather, the fear is that the thousands of changes, large and small, that the executive branch has so rapidly imposed on the military may end up undermining the professional ethic on which the U.S. form of civilian control depends.

The incoming Congress can push U.S. civil-military relations in a healthier direction by overseeing the executive branch more actively than its predecessor. It can also help restore trust with both the military and the American public if it focuses on providing greater transparency, takes a more assertive role in conducting defense policy, reinforces the military’s nonpartisan professional ethic, and establishes a new bipartisan commission to reinvigorate the military profession. Congress has the power and authority to improve civil-military relations. The new session, starting in January, is its chance to use them before the damage becomes irreversible.

When Pete Hegseth was sworn in as defense secretary, in 2025, he had strong views on the changes he wanted to make, alleging the military had lost its warfighting focus because of misguided priorities and “wokeism.” He has pursued those changes with a zeal not seen since Robert McNamara held the office from 1961 to 1968. Hegseth has reshaped the senior officer ranks: he has fired or removed nearly two dozen senior general and flag officers, and he has gotten rid of more than one-quarter of all four-star generals, the chairman of the Joint Chiefs of Staff, and the chiefs of the U.S. Air Force, Army, and Navy. He has fired, removed, or pulled from promotion lists approximately 80 general and flag officers, a disproportionate number of whom are women or racial minorities. Hegseth has justified those removals in the vaguest of terms, which has convinced most observers that the real reason amounts to little more than because these officers followed the lawful orders of the previous administration. No previous defense secretary has removed such large swaths of the officer corps who honored their oaths to the Constitution and faithfully implemented policies decided by their civilian superiors.

Hegseth has moved quickly, largely ignoring traditional bureaucratic practices, including rigorous staff reviews and close involvement of congressional stakeholders in changing the trajectory of the Pentagon’s policymaking and in conducting military operations. Amid ongoing questions about the legality of the boat strikes the Trump administration ordered against suspected drug traffickers last fall as well as civilian casualties in America’s war with Iran, it is critical to note that Hegseth had already fired the senior, most experienced military legal advisers—sending a warning shot across the bow of younger judge advocates—and begun a significant overhaul of the Judge Advocate General Corps. Ever since the My Lai massacre during the Vietnam War, when U.S. soldiers committed war crimes by killing hundreds of unarmed Vietnamese civilians, the JAG Corps had been empowered to help U.S. personnel abide by the laws of armed conflict. By demoting senior legal advisers and selecting less qualified personnel in those positions, however, the military’s legal voice is more marginalized today than it has been since the Vietnam War. The Trump administration has also centralized decision-making, including by placing routine actions in highly classified channels that minimize opportunities for input and deliberation.

Hegseth has done all of this—and much more—with essentially no pushback from the uniformed military or Pentagon bureaucracy. On the one hand, this shows just how strongly officers accept the principle of civilian control—a posture reinforced precisely because Hegseth has proved willing to punish even unsupported allegations of military insubordination. On the other hand, his actions have had a chilling effect across the force, creating an environment in which the military is reluctant to offer candid advice or uphold its professional standards and values when they are at odds with administration priorities. Indeed, in a highly unusual move, former government officials have publicly called for military leaders to do more to stand up to the Trump administration, even at the cost of undermining civilian control. As we argued in Foreign Affairs in 2024, however, the military cannot save democracy; only Congress and the courts can and should check presidential power.

Hegseth has enjoyed the unwavering support of President Donald Trump. But the real secret to Hegseth’s success in imposing his agenda on the Pentagon has been the unwillingness of the current Congress to exercise its Article I responsibilities or defend its traditional prerogatives. The incoming Congress can take a different approach by using its power to build trust, transparency, and accountability.

Most pressing, Congress should exercise its ability to appropriately scrutinize defense policymaking, much of which has been shrouded in secrecy under the Trump administration. The current Congress has held very few oversight hearings, and the ones that have taken place have been exchanges of insults rather than of information. When secrecy is genuinely needed, of course, Congress can issue classified briefings and hold closed hearings. But open hearings, when properly conducted, are necessary in cases in which Congress is entitled to answers, for example, about military personnel policy.

In the case of the boat strikes in the Caribbean and the eastern Pacific, Congress needs to know what advice senior military commanders and their JAGs offered, and it must understand how JAG firings affect the process by which commanders discern the lawfulness of military operations. Thus far, the Defense Department has provided only superficial explanations of its actions that basically amount to “we did this because we thought it was necessary.” Such justifications fall far short of what the Constitution envisions as the best way to hold the armed forces accountable: open debate that subjects policies to rigorous scrutiny. Greater transparency would also bolster public confidence in defense policy and remove pressure on the military to have to act as a potential savior of the Constitution.

Congress should also faithfully fulfill its “advice and consent” function established in Article II, which authorizes the Senate to review and confirm presidential nominations and promotions. Hegseth’s unprecedented intervention in the meritocratic promotion process has dramatically altered the military’s upper echelons. Congress has normalized this interference, perhaps unwittingly, by approving Hegseth’s altered promotion lists with voice votes and little inquiry.

The president and the defense secretary must retain the right to remove officers in whom they have lost confidence. But Congress has the right to know why a change was made, and it needs to ensure that promotions and removals follow established law and procedures rather than ideological or partisan considerations. Because Congress created the process for meritocratic promotions, it can push the executive branch to adhere to that process. It should mandate that the defense secretary provide a written explanation, supported by documented evidence of misconduct or derogatory information, for any off-cycle removals of senior officers and restore senior officers’ promotions, which would limit unjustified removals and uphold the merit-based selection process. Improving transparency in personnel policy will have one further salutary effect: it will help retain those qualified military personnel who are wondering whether they should give up on a military career that could be derailed by politicized and capricious promotion decisions.

In addition, Congress has a responsibility to police the well-established principle and practice that the uniformed military must stay away from partisan politics. The military has largely tried to uphold this norm, even as civilian political leaders have sought to draw it into partisan debates. Each time, for instance, Hegseth attacks the Biden administration before military audiences, most of whom served during the Biden era, he unwittingly further degrades the nonpartisan ethic on which service members’ oaths of office—and thus their subordination to his own policies—depend. It sends a message that embracing partisan rhetoric is key to career advancement and signals to the public that the military is not a neutral constituency. Congress can police the executive branch’s politicization of the military through inquiries, hearings, and even measures that prohibit the use of appropriated funds for events that treat military audiences like campaign rallies. Members of Congress can also call out for censure uniformed military personnel who behave in a blatantly partisan fashion regardless of whether they support or oppose the party the member supports.

Most ambitiously, Congress should reclaim its role in making defense policy rather than only providing oversight. Legislative deference to the executive in times of war is not unique to Trump’s second term, but Congress should close loopholes that allow presidents to expand their power unchecked. If Congress wants to provide a legal justification to allow the use of force in Iran or against drug traffickers in the Caribbean or the eastern Pacific Ocean, it should do so. If it believes these actions are unlawful, however, it must use its legislative powers to constrain them. Similarly, Congress should amend the Insurrection Act to clarify and restrict the domestic use of military force. The president must retain the ability to respond to true national emergencies, but Congress should narrow the criteria for deployment of troops on U.S. soil to avoid potential abuse.

Even as it becomes more active, Congress must strenuously avoid involving the military in a partisan war. Members of Congress should not press the military to criticize the president or the defense secretary in the made-for-social-media-outrage hearings that have become commonplace in Washington. It is right and proper for Congress to ask members of the military to share their personal and professional opinions on key strategic questions. But too often, politicians try to turn officers into combatants in their partisan battles, hiding behind the armed forces to defend partisan agendas or attacking military leaders who have simply carried out the lawful orders of a president from another party. Above all, Congress should not ask the military to be a check on civilian power. Only other civilians have the authority to do that.

Congress cannot solve longer-term issues through the day-to-day legislative process. But it has other tools to more holistically improve civil-military relations and identify the most essential reforms. The time-honored strategy is to set up a nonpartisan blue-ribbon commission to analyze problems, draw attention to their importance, and recommend solutions. A national commission on the state of the professional military ethic, co-chaired by three respected individuals—one with experience in a Republican administration, one with experience in a Democratic administration, and one retired senior military leader—would have the freedom to investigate how recent developments, including attempts to politicize the military and sustain a high tempo of operations, have affected the force. At a minimum, the commission should explore impacts on recruiting and retention; professional military education, fellowships in civilian institutions, and general and flag officer development; the JAG Corps’ ability to provide commanders with candid, independent legal advice; and the state of the National Guard.

The current Congress has not shown enough interest in exercising its own powers. The next one can make a clean break and rediscover the body’s ability to carry out the functions that are necessary to improve civil-military relations. The new Congress can demonstrate its commitment to fulfilling its responsibilities by providing ordinary legislative oversight in the raising, maintaining, and equipping of the armed forces. And on thorny issues that require a bigger response, Congress can start the reform process by creating a national commission to lay out an agenda for action. Even after two years of enormous strain that has eroded best practices in civil-military relations, the next Congress is well positioned to reverse the slide and shore up Americans’ faith in a nonpartisan, professional military that is essential to democracy.

#5975. Indonesia’s Wiretap Scandal Heralds a Political Showdown

This issue is preventing our website from loading properly. Please review the following troubleshooting tips or contact us at [email protected].

Southeast Asia Brief: Indonesia’s Wiretap Scandal Heralds a Political Showdown

Create an FP account to save articles to read later.

Downloadable PDFs are a benefit of an FP subscription.

Contact us at [email protected] to learn about upgrade options, unlocking the ability to gift this article.

Welcome to Foreign Policy’s Southeast Asia Brief.

The highlights this week: Indonesia’s former chief of police is arrested on suspicion of wiretapping the president, a Florida cruise ship abandons passengers in Cambodia, and we explore the Southeast Asian dimension of Jensen Huang’s affair with Alice Huang.

Welcome to Foreign Policy’s Southeast Asia Brief.

The highlights this week: Indonesia’s former chief of police is arrested on suspicion of wiretapping the president, a Florida cruise ship abandons passengers in Cambodia, and we explore the Southeast Asian dimension of Jensen Huang’s affair with Alice Huang.

Former police chief charged with spying on president

Shortly after this newsletter was finalized, it was announced vice-president Gibran Rakabuming Raka had tendered his resignation. The dramatic development comes amidst a growing swell to impeach him, and a dramatic wiretap scandal covered below.

Last Tuesday, Indonesia’s former chief of police and Presidential Chief of Staff Gen. Listyo Sigit Prabowo and two other police generals were arrested on suspicion of wiretapping President Prabowo Subianto. That same day, the government flooded Jakarta with more than 20,000 troops.

The development is widely seen as heralding a showdown between the president and his rival-turned-ally, former President Joko Widodo, whose son Gibran Rakabuming Raka serves as vice president.

Since the initial revelations, the story has grown more extraordinary. The presidential palace has accused the men, as well as a fourth police general, of treason, which can carry the death penalty.

The government reportedly suspects them of helping foster protests, which have rocked the government over the past two years.

Police alleged the president was one of some 6,000 people—including top politicians, businesspeople, journalists, and activists—illegally surveilled from 2025 until last month. Prabowo himself apparently suspected that he was being tapped for more than a year.

The political implications are explosive. Listyo is widely seen as Widodo’s man and is part of the “Solo Gang,” a group of officials from Widodo’s hometown of Surakarta, where the former president began his political career as mayor.

Listyo is alleged to have operated as something of a Mr. Fix-It for Widodo in delicate political situations, from intraparty disputes to the 2024 election.

Police investigators have rather pointedly announced that neither Widodo nor his son were on the list of those wiretapped.

The move against Listyo seems to herald a wider move against Widodo, whose political relationship with Prabowo has deteriorated steadily since the 2024 election.

In parliament, there is now a growing push to impeach Gibran. The Supreme Court ruled in October that he had failed to properly prove he had graduated high school, which is a requirement to run for the vice presidency, opening the door for parliament to take action.

Widodo himself is keeping schtum. When asked about the arrest of Listyo and the Supreme Court ruling against Gibran, he has limited his responses to platitudes about respecting the legal process. Gibran has also confined himself to broad statements about respecting legal processes and focusing on his job.

Still, Widodo has also been maneuvering to shore up his position. He has effectively taken over the Indonesian Solidarity Party of which his other son, Kaesang Pangarep, is the general chair. Widodo’s enormous personal popularity gives him some potentially powerful cards to play.

Another impact is that Indonesia is looking at tightening legal controls on wiretapping.

This is not the country’s first wiretapping scandal. In 2022, Reuters revealed that Pegasus spyware, created by Israeli cyber-intelligence firm NSO Group, had been used in Indonesia since 2018 to target politicians and other prominent figures. Evidence later suggested the software was being used by Indonesia’s security services.

Despite the scandal, little action was taken and regulations governing the use of wiretaps remain a mess.

More generally this will only contribute to the sense that the wheels are coming off for Indonesia.

The political elite are gearing up for a round of brutal infighting while Indonesia is facing its worst forest fires in a decade, there is growing pressure on government bonds as the energy shock rockets up the price of fuel subsidies, and the third finance minister in two years takes the helm amid protests from a fed up populace.

Abandon ship. More than 200 passengers were abandoned in Cambodia by a Florida cruise-liner company, Villa Vie Residences, last Wednesday. The ship then steamed toward Singapore with 50 crew aboard and it was later impounded.

The ship, Villa Vie Odyssey, is one of a handful of ships worldwide that offers people the opportunity to reside on it long term. The price of permanent residence ranged from $120,000 to $440,000. Rentals started at $3,500 a month.

However, in late October, the company abruptly suspended operations, saying increased fuel costs due to the Iran war had made operations unsustainable. Residents were reportedly given seven hours’ notice.

Problems seem to have been long-running. Passengers recounted two occasions in recent months where the company asked them to pool money to buy fuel for the ship.

And last year—even before the Iran war— Villa Vie Residences was sued for fraud by a plaintiff who had bought a cabin and alleged the company had misrepresented its financial stability.

According to Cambodia’s Ministry of Tourism, there are now 98 passengers left in Cambodia. Others have already left the country.

Growth falters further. Indonesia isn’t the only country where the political elites are playing games while the country struggles.

On Tuesday, the Philippines reported a dismal GDP growth of 2.1 percent. This was below the predicted 3 percent as growth slowed for the fifth consecutive quarter. Inflation also surged to a three-year high of 7.8 percent due to increased fuel prices linked to the Iran war.

Still, the Senate remains absorbed in the impeachment of vice president Sara Duterte. And politicians are pursuing their usual budget shenanigans, unabashed by the enormous scandal that such antics sparked last year.

The Philippines is extremely dependent on fuel imports, and 90 percent of those imports passed through the Strait of Hormuz prior to the Iran war. This means the war has hit hard, with food and fuel inflation eroding consumption. It has had a major impact on the country’s economy as private consumption accounts for roughly three-quarters of the Philippines’ economy.

The other key factors are a 4.8 percent contraction of the agricultural sector and 4 percent contraction in construction. Bad weather hit the former hard, with nearly all senators urging El Niño to be declared a calamity. Meanwhile, the latter has struggled after a massive scandal last year involving the embezzlement of state funds slowed public investment.

Fatal shooting stirs war memories. One civilian was killed and 23 were injured in an Indonesian Army operation in Aceh last Wednesday. Six army personnel were also injured in the incident.

The spark was the raising of a crescent and star flag during a celebration of the prophet Muhammad’s birthday at Pulo Keunari village in the province of Aceh. The flag is associated with Aceh’s former separatist movement.

Despite a 2005 peace agreement, the flag has remained a symbol of ongoing tensions between the province and the central government. In 2013, the provincial government tried to adopt it as Aceh’s official flag but the central government objected.

Accounts of the sequence of events last Wednesday differ. An aide to Aceh’s governor told The Jakarta Post that soldiers had used a loudspeaker to demand the flag be taken down. A soldier then took down the flag, angering the crowd and sparking the clash. The aide added that the resident who died was killed via a gunshot wound to the chest.

Representatives of the military claimed that their attempts to remove the flag were met with stone throwing. The soldiers and accompanying police responded with riot control equipment, including the firing of blank shots and rubber bullets.

In Cambodia, nearly 24,000 foreigners have been deported after scam center raids over the past year; and 15,000 volunteer for military service this year, which is 5,000 more than needed.

Politicians and royals in Malaysia row over a former prime minister’s house arrest.

Rebels cautiously welcome U.S. peace plans for Myanmar; The Economist reported how rebels’ Starlink terminals are being unpredictably turned off; and Myanmar migrants reject the government’s new IDs.

Philippines police find child abuse material on devices owned by Meghan Markle’s dad.

China buys influence in Thailand media; opposition leaders allege they had their phones hacked ahead of a no confidence vote; a former Miss Thailand is accused of stealing millions from a charity; two Uyghurs face deportation to China; and a book about Thailand’s king is banned on national security grounds.

Vietnam security services may have abducted an Australian and a Norwegian activist in Cambodia; and To Lam visits Japan.

In Focus: Nvidia Scandal’s Southeast Asian Background

The affair between Jensen Huang and an alleged Chinese spy, Alice Huang, is making headlines worldwide, with the scandal containing an almost filmic mix of geopolitical implications and salacity.

One aspect that bears further scrutiny is the Southeast Asian aspect. Alice Huang was a senior executive, and self-described CEO, of Megaspeed, a company based in Singapore with offices in Malaysia and Indonesia.

The company has been under scrutiny by the U.S. government since 2024 over its alleged sanctions-busting activity smuggling top-end Nvidia chips into China. Singapore also conducted its own investigation and detained Alice Huang in 2025.

The scandal reflects that Singapore, and Southeast Asia more generally, has become a hub for China’s chip sanction-busting efforts on a massive scale. Some smuggle them into China. Other companies operate in a gray zone, building data centers with top-end Nvidia chips in Southeast Asia and then leasing their processing power to China.

Culper Research, a short seller that flagged Jensen Huang’s relationship with Megaspeed and Alice Huang back in May, estimated that 20 percent of Nvidia’s compute revenues were driven by China “via illegal GPU diversion and Southeast Asian intermediaries.” (You can read more on this topic in FP’s China Brief.)

The latest scandal comes against the background of numerous others. In 2025, the United States launched another investigation into whether DeepSeek had used Nvidia chips smuggled via Singapore.

In May, Taiwan arrested three people suspected of planning to smuggle Nvidia chips into China via an unnamed Southeast Asian country.

In late October, Malaysia estimated that a single syndicate was behind at least five cases where Malaysia authorities had seized smuggled ⁠chips that were cumulatively ​worth more than 100 million ringgit ($24.6 million).

These murky happenings reflect that the region is deeply enmeshed with both China and the United States’ economies and has its own thriving tech ecosystem.

Malaysia has become a thriving data center hub, welcoming investment from both superpowers. The country of 34.4 million people now has half as much artificial intelligence compute power as the entire European Union.

But, for those in the region who have tried to not let geopolitics affect business, or at least turn tensions to their advantage, the walls may be closing in.

Busts in Malaysia reflect ongoing U.S. pressure to crack down. And, on Friday, Bloomberg reported Nvidia was stepping up its tracking of chips in Southeast Asia to crack down on smuggling.

Pressure is also coming from Taiwan. It recently canceled trade talks with Malaysia, which could threaten future investment in the latter’s thriving semiconductor space, due to anger over comments in which Malaysian Prime Minister Anwar Ibrahim appeared to accept China’s right to use force to assimilate Taiwan.

Joseph Rachman is the writer of Foreign Policy’s weekly Southeast Asia Brief. He is a freelance journalist based in Bangkok. X: @rachman_joseph

#5974. Top Dem cries ‘weaponization’ as comments warning young people not to join Trump admin land her in hot water

Sen. Elissa Slotkin, D-Mich, says her lawyer asked the Justice Department what it plans to do after President Trump called for an investigation into her comments about young Republicans.

Sen. Elissa Slotkin, D-Mich., is accusing President Donald Trump of escalating a coordinated "weaponization" campaign against her after he ordered the Justice Department to examine remarks she made warning young Republicans they could face "a ton of problems" if they joined his administration.

In a Truth Social post over the weekend, President Trump accused Democrats of political weaponization and singled out Slotkin for "go[ing] after all young people that believe in Republican values" in her October remarks. "By copy of this statement, I am ordering the Department of Justice to look into this criminal act, immediately!" Trump wrote.

In a video response posted Monday, Slotkin defended her remarks, saying she was referring to people who might be asked to violate the law or misuse taxpayer dollars and citing her First Amendment rights.

"A couple weeks ago, I talked about how anyone who is violating the law or misusing taxpayer dollars should be held accountable, and if there's young people who are thinking of joining people who are doing that, they should think twice," Slotkin said. "If you're not violating the law — no problem."

DEMS PIVOT FROM 'AFFORDABILITY' PITCH TO DHS PROBE THREATS AFTER MIDTERM WINS

Trump faced criticism after posting online about Democrats allegedly urging the military to refuse orders, later clarifying he was not making threats. (Tom Williams/CQ-Roll Call, Inc via Getty Images; Andrew Harnik/Getty Images)

Slotkin said Trump began targeting her after her October remarks, first by "name-checking" her from the Oval Office before he "really ramped up his coordinated play" following Democrats’ midterm election gains. She also pointed to Attorney General Todd Blanche's Sunday television appearances discussing possible investigations. Slotkin said her attorney sent the Justice Department a letter Monday asking, "What are you going to do?"

The Justice Department has not confirmed whether it has opened an investigation into Slotkin. During an interview Sunday on CNN’s "State of the Union," Blanche declined to identify a specific crime when asked whether Democrats’ rhetoric could constitute one, saying, "It depends on what’s said. So, yes, it could be."

A DOJ spokesperson told Fox News Digital that Slotkin’s comments should be evaluated under the facts and law but did not confirm an investigation.

OBAMA-APPOINTED JUDGE AT CENTER OF TRUMP'S ANTI-WEAPONIZATION FUND CASE HIT WITH MISCONDUCT CLAIM

"Senator Slotkin said publicly that the prospect of future investigations should deter young people from joining this administration," the spokesperson said in a statement. "The Department believes public service should not be met with threats of retribution, and this Department is committed to ending the weaponization of government. We follow the facts and the law."

U.S. Attorney General Todd Blanche speaks during a news conference at the Robert F. Kennedy Department of Justice building on April 7, 2026. (Getty Images)

Slotkin's original remarks also prompted a congressional ethics complaint. Michael Bicksel, a University of Chicago student who first publicized her comments, filed the complaint with the Senate Select Committee on Ethics on Oct. 22.

Bicksel argued that Slotkin had proposed using congressional investigations and potential prosecutions to deter young people from joining a Republican administration, asking the committee to determine whether her comments amounted to "improper conduct which may reflect upon the Senate."

Fox News Digital reached out to Slotkin for comment but did not hear back in time for publication.

"It's a coordinated, manufactured response," Slotkin previously said about the backlash to her remarks concerning young Trump supporters. "They want to intimidate you to be quiet."

Attendees of a Turning Point USA event pass through a protest as they leave the event, which featured U.S. Vice President J.D. Vance in Athens, Georgia, U.S. April 14, 2026. (Elijah Nouvelage/Reuters)

Slotkin made the remarks Oct. 13 during a University of Chicago discussion about how Democrats could exercise congressional power if they regained control of Congress. Fox News Digital previously reported that Slotkin advocated pursuing "signature cases" against people who enabled Trump rather than focusing exclusively on the president. She also argued that the prospect of legal trouble could deter young Republicans from joining his administration.

The latest dispute follows an earlier Justice Department investigation involving Slotkin and five other Democratic lawmakers who posted a video reminding military personnel and intelligence officials that they were not required to follow unlawful orders. A federal grand jury declined to indict the lawmakers.

In a letter to Blanche made public Monday, Slotkin's attorney, Preet Bharara, argued that her remarks were protected by the First Amendment and the Constitution's speech or debate clause. Bharara demanded that Blanche publicly confirm the Justice Department would not investigate the senator.

"Just like last time, Senator Slotkin will not be deterred," Bharara wrote. "She will continue to call out corruption by this administration and to exercise her First Amendment rights to speak out against the President’s many abuses of power."

Alec Schemmel is a Politics Reporter for Fox News Digital who is originally from Charlotte, North Carolina but now resides in the Washington D.C.-Baltimore Metro Area.

#5972. Don’t Soak the Rich

Since its founding in 1922, Foreign Affairs has been the leading forum for serious discussion of American foreign policy and global affairs. The magazine has featured contributions from many leading international affairs experts.

Daniel WALDENSTROM is Professor of Economics at the Research Institute of Industrial Economics in Stockholm. He is the author of Richer and More Equal: A New History of Wealth in the West.

Weak Institutions—Not Private Fortunes—Deform Democracy

As private wealth continues to grow to levels never before seen in modern history, a powerful idea is taking hold in the West: the accumulation of great fortunes poses an existential threat to democracy. This idea is not novel. Over a decade ago, the political scientists Jacob Hacker and Paul Pierson asserted that the rise of plutocracy has been a defining trend of American politics since the late 1970s. The economist Paul Krugman argued in 2011 that the “extreme concentration of income is incompatible with real democracy.”

Lately, however, it has begun to gain traction in the halls of power of Western democracies. In his farewell address, former U.S. President Joe Biden argued that an “oligarchy of extreme wealth, power, and influence” was threatening “our entire democracy, our basic rights and freedoms.” Spanish Prime Minister Pedro Sánchez echoed the sentiment at a 2025 G-20 summit, warning that concentrated wealth “corrodes democratic institutions.”

Some now see confiscation as the answer. After a bitter debate and over $300 million of cumulative spending by supporters and opponents, Californians rejected a statewide “billionaires’ tax” ballot measure in this week’s midterm elections. But the proposal counted among its proponents six Nobel laureates in economics, including Daron Acemoglu, Peter Diamond, and Joseph Stiglitz, who endorsed the tax in a letter framing it as “a turning point in the battle between democracy and oligarchy.” The French economist Gabriel Zucman called the measure’s defeat “just the beginning” and vowed that though “Silicon Valley billionaires have won this battle … they will lose this war.” Several European political leaders, including the former German Green Party leader Annalena Baerbock and Danish Prime Minister Mette Frederiksen, have floated wealth tax proposals of their own in recent years.

The risk they have identified is genuine. Democratic societies are not doing enough to prevent the superrich from unduly influencing politics. But their diagnosis—not to mention their preferred solution—is incorrect. Billionaires’ participation in politics does not in itself indicate that a democracy has been captured by moneyed interests. The strength of the political and civil institutions meant to protect against the exercise of those interests is a far more important indicator.

Instead of imposing wealth taxes, countries that are serious about safeguarding their political systems should take their cues from those democracies that have successfully insulated their politics from the influence of large fortunes. They should adopt transparent election laws and restraints on private campaign contributions and encourage a strong, independent media. Such measures will help make societies more democratic. Taxing wealth will simply make them poorer.

Arguments in favor of taxing wealth in the name of democracy often hinge on an understanding of billionaires as a monolithic bloc, committed to an agenda that runs counter to the public interest. These claims contain a kernel of truth. According to the benchmark V-Dem and Freedom House indices, the United States has indeed been in a state of democratic decline since the early 2010s. But it does not follow that billionaires as a group are responsible for this decline—or that such a unified group even exists.

To empirically determine its existence, I classified the richest Americans in the 2026 Forbes 400 listing according to their traceable political donations, analyzing both their average annual giving and their total giving over the past three decades. This method is no doubt imperfect—donations can be informal or hidden and are not the only way to achieve political influence—but the pattern that emerges should nonetheless cast doubt on the idea of a single billionaire class marching on Washington.

The evidence shows that roughly 30 individuals, or eight percent of U.S. billionaires in the Forbes 400, are high-level donors, giving more than $1 million each year for political purposes. Roughly half of American billionaires give on average less than $30,000 a year, 35 percent give less than $10,000, and 15 percent, or 60 of the listed billionaires, have no visible political footprint. Just 16 donors—four percent—have given more than $50 million over the last 30 years. This small minority may well plow enough money into elections to make up for the majority of billionaires with no apparent interest in politics (although if Elon Musk’s midterm outlay is any indication, even vast sums of money are no guarantee of political outcomes). But the very fact that both these groups exist suggests that the presence of the ultrawealthy alone is not necessarily responsible for American democratic decay. The weakness of the institutions meant to check their influence, on the other hand, is.

For the American billionaires who do engage in politics, federal rules and institutions make it easy to achieve disproportionate influence through large-scale private spending. Super PACs are permitted to receive unlimited contributions from individuals, corporations, unions, and other political committees. The hollowing out of independent public-service media and the consolidation of private media make matters worse, allowing a small number of wealthy owners to shape the dominant narrative unchecked.

Much of the momentum behind a wealth tax comes from the United States. But it is hardly the only country with a high proportion of superwealthy residents. Sweden and Switzerland have among the most billionaires per capita in the world, with around 4.7 and 5.0 billionaires per million inhabitants, respectively—higher than the United States’ 2.9 and dwarfing the average of 1.3 for Organization for Economic Cooperation and Development (OECD) countries. If wealth tax proponents were correct about the relationship between extreme wealth and democratic health, one would expect both Sweden’s and Switzerland’s democracies to be in dire shape.

But in neither country has the presence of large private fortunes significantly damaged democracy. Both rank highly among liberal democracies when assessing the fairness of elections, the rule of law, checks on the executive, trust in government, and media freedom. Sweden ranks fifth in the 2026 World Press Freedom Index, and Switzerland ranks eighth. As of 2025, they were tied for sixth in Transparency International’s Corruption Perceptions Index; the United States ranked 29th. According to the OECD’s 2025 trust survey, 62 percent of Swiss respondents had confidence in their national government, the highest of all countries polled. Sweden, at 42 percent, ranked 15th among the countries polled, higher than the OECD average of 40 percent and far exceeding the United States’ 27 percent, as measured by the Partnership for Public Service.

It might be tempting to point to tax policy as an explanation. Indeed, social democratic Sweden, with its 41 percent tax-to-GDP ratio as of 2024, has one of the world’s highest tax levels. Switzerland, however, maintains a tax-to-GDP ratio of roughly 27 percent, well below the OECD average of 34 percent and about even with the U.S. level of 25 percent. That these countries can support large entrepreneurial fortunes without sacrificing their democracies is in fact not the result of draconian restrictions on how those fortunes are spent in politics. Two other shared institutional features offer more compelling answers.

In Sweden, political parties receive funding mainly through public structures. Private contributions are allowed, but individual donors represent a relatively small share of total party funding, and disclosure rules make all large private contributions visible. Sweden’s constitution grants citizens and journalists the right to access almost any official document. Ministers hold little direct influence over administrative agencies, allowing the agencies to operate independently of executive authority. These rules do not eliminate lobbying, but they largely prevent private money from discreetly entering and influencing the political process.

Switzerland’s barriers between private wealth and the levers of government, although similarly sturdy, stop money from influencing politics in slightly different ways. The country’s federal system diffuses power across 26 regional cantons and over 2,000 municipalities, and frequent regional and federal referendums give citizens veto power over legislation. Switzerland has no direct federal funding of parties and no general spending ceiling, but national disclosure rules introduced in 2022 require major campaigns and large donors to identify their financing. The country does have a wealth tax dating back to the nineteenth century, but it is administered at the canton level and its highest marginal rate is nowhere near as punitive as the California proposal. Dispersing authority and enshrining accountability over government decisions through direct democracy are far more responsible for the success of Swiss democracy.

Both countries also have long traditions of private journalism and independent public-service media. A mix of private outlets and public-service media, which has been shown to foster greater public trust in the news and resilience to online disinformation, prevents any single news provider from monopolizing the airwaves or suppressing information.

These institutions are not perfect, and private donations still play a role in Swedish and Swiss politics. That countries with nearly twice the number of billionaires per capita as the United States have thriving democracies should, however, give the lie to the idea that the superrich, by their mere existence, deform democracy.

The proposals currently in vogue are presented as a novel solution to the novel problem of concentrated wealth. But societies have attempted to tax wealth before. Throughout the twentieth century, many European countries imposed similarly designed taxes on household net wealth for redistributive purposes. Problems quickly emerged. Taxing individuals who rarely generated cash income and whose fortunes were thus not easily estimated outraged entrepreneurs, lowered investment, and hampered growth. In the 1970s and 1980s, governments on both the left and the right took note. They tried to discount certain business assets and exempt closely held firms from taxation to mitigate the distortions. But tinkering around these edges only caused new problems of unfairness. Revenues, which at that time constituted less than half a percent of GDP across Europe, compared with total taxes that often stood at 40 percent of GDP, slumped. Ultimately, most countries scrapped the tax.

Many of today’s proponents are more likely to cite the threat of unprecedented fortunes to democracy than the need for redistribution as the impetus for a wealth tax. But they offer no new solutions to the problems of old. Nor would a wealth tax stop billionaires from financing politics, since the wealthiest hardly need to throw the full weight of their fortunes around to wield political influence.

Supporters might argue that the pro-democracy case for a wealth tax is much broader in scope. After all, the superrich exert influence on politics in more ways than just their donations. A wealth tax might not stop a billionaire from buying a state election, but it might stop one from, for example, buying Paramount or The Washington Post. Imperfect a solution as it is, a billionaires’ tax referendum represents a more achievable goal than amending the Constitution to wrest power from the executive.

But a wealth tax has never been an efficient fiscal tool or a substitute for substantive democratic renewal. Whatever small democratic gains it might feasibly bring would be outweighed by the threat to business ventures, capital formation, and long-term economic development it would pose. As anything more than a symbolic measure, a tax on uncertain, often illiquid values is essentially a tax on entrepreneurial risk-taking. But the case against it should not be mistaken for a broadside against all capital taxation. Governments should still seek to raise substantial revenue from taxes on the returns on capital and should set rates and brackets how they please. They must not, however, forget the crucial distinction between taxing already realized income and taxing assets that may yet generate new returns.

A better agenda to safeguard democracy against the influence of money should prioritize reforming the institutions whose weaknesses make such influence possible. Limits on campaign and party finance and more transparent disclosure of lobbying and conflicts of interest would, at the very least, make elections more difficult to buy. Increased funding for public media and stricter antitrust rules to block media mergers would create a more diverse landscape of both private and independent public-service outlets. These measures would affect just the few American billionaires who plow significant parts of their fortunes into politics, leaving the majority of the country’s wealthiest to keep doing what they do best—creating value and driving growth.

Democracies should, of course, always fear oligarchy. They should actively work to curtail the influence of money on politics. They should not, however, confuse economic success with political capture. Countries need entrepreneurs who are willing to take risks and build wealth. Democracy will survive in an era of extraordinary fortunes not by antagonizing successful people but by making political power harder to buy.

#5971. Memory Chips Are Completely Distorting South Korea’s Economy

This issue is preventing our website from loading properly. Please review the following troubleshooting tips or contact us at [email protected].

Analysis: Memory Chips Are Completely Distorting South Korea’s Economy

Create an FP account to save articles to read later.

Downloadable PDFs are a benefit of an FP subscription.

Contact us at [email protected] to learn about upgrade options, unlocking the ability to gift this article.

In South Korea, the term byeorak-geoji means “sudden beggar.” In 2026, it has gained widespread popularity again. But its meaning is figurative. Rather than denoting sudden bankruptcy or losing one’s life savings in the stock market, the term describes a new and widespread sense of relative deprivation: The realization that a colleague or acquaintance just earned years of your salary in a single month on paper.

Such anxiety is driven by a booming domestic semiconductor industry that has a stranglehold on the global artificial intelligence industry’s supply bottleneck: memory chips. At the market’s peak this year, Samsung Electronics saw its market capitalization surge to a record high of $1.3 trillion, while SK Hynix has since reported a staggering 328 percent year-on-year increase in revenue in its latest third-quarter results. The boom had led many Koreans to believe that borrowing to leverage into these companies offered the quickest and perhaps the only route to profits large enough to pay off their mortgages and secure homes.

In South Korea, the term byeorak-geoji means “sudden beggar.” In 2026, it has gained widespread popularity again. But its meaning is figurative. Rather than denoting sudden bankruptcy or losing one’s life savings in the stock market, the term describes a new and widespread sense of relative deprivation: The realization that a colleague or acquaintance just earned years of your salary in a single month on paper.

Such anxiety is driven by a booming domestic semiconductor industry that has a stranglehold on the global artificial intelligence industry’s supply bottleneck: memory chips. At the market’s peak this year, Samsung Electronics saw its market capitalization surge to a record high of $1.3 trillion, while SK Hynix has since reported a staggering 328 percent year-on-year increase in revenue in its latest third-quarter results. The boom had led many Koreans to believe that borrowing to leverage into these companies offered the quickest and perhaps the only route to profits large enough to pay off their mortgages and secure homes.

If you were an engineer at one of these companies, you could cash out stock compensation boosted by stock rallies fueled by people’s borrowing, collect enormous bonuses, and secure a mortgage rate of 1.5 percent through company housing-loan programs, alongside support for your children’s tuition and retirement savings. Meanwhile, ordinary Koreans carrying debt and buying homes are left to shoulder the Bank of Korea’s high-interest-rate campaign, with back-to-back rate hikes in July and August, designed to curb inflation and financial risks amid the national semiconductor export boom.

Koreans are increasingly questioning that boom’s broader effect on ordinary households. Economists and scholars are voicing doubts. Even as semiconductors have accounted for nearly half of South Korea’s exports and pushed nominal GDP growth to a nearly 50-year high, the Korea Development Institute slashed its annual job growth forecast from 170,000 to 110,000 in its August report, and the OECD projected the country’s private consumption growth for the year at just 2.2 percent. And if the semiconductor boom eventually bursts, the overall effects for the Korean economy could be devastating.

If the global demand for memory falters, new capacity floods the market, or technological advances curb AI’s appetite for memory, the result would inevitably be a price fall. That would be welcome for buyers such as U.S. tech giants, AI powers, and behemoth enterprises such as Apple. Meanwhile, Samsung and SK Hynix would undoubtedly take a severe hit, although the massive net cash reserves they have amassed will shield them from the kind of bankruptcies that decimated debt-fueled South Korean chaebols during the late-1990s Asian financial crisis.

But while Korea’s memory industry sits on deep liquidity buffers, the true burden of debt in 2026 rests squarely on the shoulders of the country’s households. As an acutely capital-intensive industry, the semiconductor sector’s colossal profits are effectively locked away at the apex of the corporate hierarchy. This classic dual economy structure ensures that wealth fails to permeate adequately through trickle-down effects to the small- and medium-sized enterprises and the service sector, which account for the absolute majority of national employment.

Meanwhile, the South Korean populace has been left to bear the brunt of emerging macroeconomic overheating and inflation. According to the Bank of Korea, household credit breached 2,000 trillion won ($1.49 trillion) in June, and it had already stood at 88.6 percent of GDP at the end of 2025. The onerous mortgage and credit interest burdens induced by the rate hikes are stifling private consumption.

What’s worse, South Korea suffers from one of the highest house price-to-income ratios globally, with ordinary citizens anchoring their massive debt to the real estate market, itself strained by property project-finance troubles. Yet the world’s lowest fertility rate, coupled with rapid demographic aging, leaves a shrinking young generation to absorb these real estate assets, eroding the foundations of domestic demand. And AI, of course, cannot resolve the low fertility rate.

The current prosperity carries the seeds of so-called Dutch disease, in which a booming export sector siphons capital and talent away from other industries and erodes their competitiveness. The result is a hollowing out of the foundations of the domestic economy.

Memory export figures act as a tarp draped over the nation’s roof, temporarily covering the reality of high inflation and weak private consumption. According to OECD projections, South Korea’s potential growth rate will be at a historic low of 1.52 percent in 2027, a figure even lower than the 2.52 percent recorded in 2023, before the memory boom. Fundamentally, South Korea’s 2026 growth is jobless growth. Beneath the tarp of an export boom, the ceiling of its domestic market is caving in while the foundation of its economy continues to erode.

Nowadays, another term is circulating widely among the South Korean populace: yeongkkul, which refers to “trading one’s soul to borrow.” Just as, amid the 19th-century cotton boom, Indian farmers famously surrendered their subsistence fields to chase cotton margins, modern South Koreans are seeking leverage of any kind to outrun the debt, inflation, and housing prices inflated by the memory boom.

One 25-year-old student interviewed by a South Korean news website diverted a student loan meant for living into stocks. Then he added an emergency bank loan and his part-time earnings to pour about 22 million won borrowed money into leveraged stock-market funds. Another student, aged 24, used fivefold leverage to turn 20 million won saved during military service into nearly 300 million won, only for forced liquidations to wipe out his gains and eat into his original savings within weeks. But he planned to borrow again, still hoping one day to afford an apartment, marriage, and children.

In investing, people resort to excessive leverage because of a fear of missing out. In the context of South Korea’s post-pandemic K-shaped recovery, the anxiety runs deeper: It is a fear of failing to scale a calcified social ladder, of being discarded by the velocity of wealth redistribution. People see their hard work rewarded with a salary that must be saved in its entirety for more than 30 years, without spending a penny on living expenses, to afford to buy an apartment in Seoul. They see a future in which their bosses—whose assets have made them the major beneficiaries of the semiconductor boom—replace them with AI.

The result is not just a fear of missing out on gains, but of missing out on a normal life—a fear of falling behind. Koreans are incapable of outrunning omnipresent economic anxiety and existential dread. A constant sampling is on offer on the anonymous digital platform Blind, where it’s common to read comments such as: “In a society where class divisions are entrenched, the ladder to climb has collapsed”; or “you have to bet your life on leverage”; or “If we miss out on AI boom, is it already too late for us in this lifetime?”

Extreme economic structures are accompanied by extreme risks. Whether AI will transform the world, and whether Asia has overconcentrated its resources in it, are two distinct questions. Just as in Bombay amid the cotton boom of the mid-19th century, the macroeconomic buffers of South Korea’s single-engine semiconductor economy are wearing thin. If the solitary engine of memory stalls, its effects could compound the risks from household debt, real estate vulnerabilities, and demographic pressures to deluge the entire South Korean economy and its population.

The central question of the memory cycle is not about whether it will end, but when. On the optimistic side, if the global memory shortage persists beyond 2030, as projected by SK Hynix’s CEO, South Korea’s economy and its semiconductor companies will usher in a golden age, alleviating the government’s chronic deficit pressures.

Yet a meager trickle of prosperity coupled with persistent jobless growth could potentially spell even deeper despair for ordinary people. Waiting in vain for a trickle that may never come, the people would be left to languish in a continuing drought.

A delayed fallout could be worst of all for most Koreans. The future currently portends a household debt bubble, a weakened domestic demand buffer, and a demographic cliff—ingredients for an economic famine. A memory downturn would depress incomes and asset prices, forcing indebted households to cut spending; spending cuts would deepen a property market downturn and systemic financial distress, ensuring the protracted slump.

Like 1990s Japan, South Korea risks its own “lost decade.” History has demonstrated that for an economy overdependent on a monoculture, the longer that the prosperity overshadows the decay it breeds, the harder the prolonged landing. In boom and bust alike, ordinary Koreans pay the highest price.

Nero Huang is a Taiwanese British author and analyst. His writing covers diplomacy and economics across the Asia-Pacific.

#5968. How Netanyahu Lost His Bet on Trump

This issue is preventing our website from loading properly. Please review the following troubleshooting tips or contact us at [email protected].

Analysis: How Netanyahu Lost His Bet on Trump

Create an FP account to save articles to read later.

Downloadable PDFs are a benefit of an FP subscription.

Contact us at [email protected] to learn about upgrade options, unlocking the ability to gift this article.

Get audio access with any FP subscription.

While Israelis were still coming to terms with the uncertain outcome of their own Oct. 27 elections, U.S. President Donald Trump chose the day of the U.S. midterms to further discombobulate the Israeli public. On Fox News, he announced not only that he was ending U.S. military assistance to Israel by the end of the year but also that Israeli Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu had agreed that the assistance should end. In an interview with Fox & Friends, Trump remarked: “As far as paying Israel, Bibi Netanyahu and I … made a deal months ago that we’re stopping, and they’re fine with it.”

It is true that Netanyahu told the Economist in January that he hoped to “taper off” Israeli dependence on U.S. military aid over the next decade. But he has given no indication of any agreement for military assistance to terminate as soon as this year. Feeling the pressure, Netanyahu’s office is now spinning the end of U.S. military assistance as a “transition out from aid towards partnership and mutual investment."

While Israelis were still coming to terms with the uncertain outcome of their own Oct. 27 elections, U.S. President Donald Trump chose the day of the U.S. midterms to further discombobulate the Israeli public. On Fox News, he announced not only that he was ending U.S. military assistance to Israel by the end of the year but also that Israeli Prime Minister Benjamin Netanyahu had agreed that the assistance should end. In an interview with Fox & Friends, Trump remarked: “As far as paying Israel, Bibi Netanyahu and I … made a deal months ago that we’re stopping, and they’re fine with it.”

It is true that Netanyahu told the Economist in January that he hoped to “taper off” Israeli dependence on U.S. military aid over the next decade. But he has given no indication of any agreement for military assistance to terminate as soon as this year. Feeling the pressure, Netanyahu’s office is now spinning the end of U.S. military assistance as a “transition out from aid towards partnership and mutual investment.”

The backlash in Israel was immediate. Trump’s announcement raises critical questions regarding what political price was negotiated—and specifically, whether any agreement to end U.S. aid served as the quid pro quo for Trump’s endorsement of Netanyahu in Israel’s election.

Former Prime Minister Ehud Barak accused Netanyahu of concocting “a stinking deal” that traded U.S. military assistance for a presidential endorsement, concluding that Netanyahu had “exceeded his authority” and was “utterly unfit to lead a country.” Opposition leader Gadi Eisenkot, who may yet replace Netanyahu as prime minister, was less conspiratorial but equally scathing. “Israel’s special relationship with the United States is a central component of its national security,” he wrote on X. “The fact that the security establishment is learning, together with Israeli citizens, about a change in the strategic relationship through the media shows just how much Benjamin Netanyahu’s conduct is a real danger.”

That Trump chose to make this announcement on Election Day speaks volumes about the collapse of Israel’s standing across the U.S. political spectrum. He evidently believed that it would resonate with voters amid rising public anger over inflation and the cost of living. The question of why a high-tech powerhouse requires billions in taxpayer-funded military aid carries growing political weight, even if much of the aid is reinvested in the U.S. defense industry.

The real elephant in the room, however, is the ongoing war with Iran and anger over U.S. support for Israel. Prominent Republicans, including Vice President J.D. Vance, have openly identified the conflict as the principal catalyst for the party’s electoral thrashing. Writing on X in the immediate aftermath of the vote, Vance broke ranks with traditional White House messaging, declaring bluntly: “The reason we lost is the war. … [E]veryone knows why we lost. And any honest conversation about where we go from here must start there.” He also claimed that voters wanted to see a change in the relationship with Israel. Vance highlighted internal polling showing that 2024 Trump voters who defected to the Democrats in the midterms specifically cited an end to the war, economic relief from soaring fuel prices, and a fundamental shift in Washington’s relationship with Israel as their primary demands. In an interview with CBS’s 60 Minutes on Sunday, Trump did not disagree with Vance’s observation that the Iran war caused the Republican defeat in the midterms, adding that “there is a great dislike, incredibly, for Israel.”

While it was Trump who launched a war against Iran in February, Netanyahu never concealed his desire for a joint campaign. Even following Operation Epic Fury, he has consistently tried to persuade Trump to renew the war, treating the Iranian threat not merely as an existential regional issue but as a political prop to distract the Israeli electorate from his leadership failures in the lead-up to and aftermath of Oct. 7, 2023.

Ahead of the midterms, the New York Times published a report claiming that Israel had shared intelligence with the United States and Gulf countries about possible Iranian plans to use chemical weapons against them. U.S. officials met the intelligence with skepticism, believing it was part of a coordinated effort by Netanyahu to push Trump toward renewed strikes.

Netanyahu’s rhetoric on Iran is riddled with glaring contradictions. Having publicly trumpeted historic success in dismantling Iran’s nuclear capabilities in June 2025, he has reversed course to manufacture an immediate, existential urge for military confrontation. On Oct. 25, Netanyahu posted an AI-generated image depicting a nuclear blast over Tel Aviv, warning voters that rejecting his leadership invited a “second Holocaust.” Political opponents accused him of manufacturing a crisis with Iran as an attempt to delay the election. This triggered an unprecedented rift with Israel Defense Forces Chief of Staff Eyal Zamir, who resisted political orders that risked sparking an immediate, unwarranted regional conflagration. Yair Golan of the Democrats declared that Netanyahu lacks “the necessary public trust” and “moral legitimacy or even basic capability” to lead such a campaign, while former Israeli Ambassador to Germany Jeremy Issacharoff noted that Netanyahu’s political machinations over Iran “severely damage Israel’s credibility abroad.”

During the opening session of the new Knesset on Tuesday, Netanyahu insisted that while his government had removed the immediate existential threat, Iran was renewing its nuclear program and had not given up on its intent to destroy Israel. He declared that the only way to remove this existential threat was by toppling the regime, trumpeting for good measure that his government had “changed the face of the Middle East.”

Netanyahu’s endgame is clearly at odds with the objectives of the Trump administration, which views military force primarily as leverage to reopen the Strait of Hormuz and force Tehran to climb down over its nuclear program. In his incendiary speech, Netanyahu also asserted that only a national unity government—presumably under his leadership—would enable Israel to meet the upcoming challenges, while accusing Israel’s Arab lawmakers of seeking the nation’s destruction. Eisenkot countered immediately, stating that Netanyahu’s “hate-filled” speech demonstrated that he was unfit for office. He has clearly failed to internalize the fact that, even among his erstwhile allies in the Republican party, his political standing is becoming increasingly toxic.

Netanyahu retains power for now as a caretaker prime minister while Eisenkot seeks to form a new government. Renewed hostilities with Iran in coordination with the United States would have been highly convenient for Netanyahu, as this could have delayed the opening of the newly elected parliament and formation of a new government. His overarching goal remains to block an opposition coalition that includes or is supported by Arab parties, who emerged as pivotal kingmakers in this election.

Deploying the Trump playbook to contest the election results, Netanyahu’s loyalist Shin Bet security chief, David Zini, has targeted Arab opposition lawmakers with unfounded investigations, raising grave constitutional alarm. Even though Eisenkot has received the mandate to form a government, his narrow victory and the opposition’s lingering hesitation toward joining forces with the Arab parties make coalition-building highly uncertain.

While right-wing factions in Israel view Netanyahu’s relationship with Trump and a renewed U.S.-Israel campaign against Iran as a strategic win, the long-term fallout could be severe. In putting all his eggs into the Republican basket, Netanyahu has wrecked U.S. bipartisan support for Israel. Following a decisive Democratic shift in Congress, he finds himself dangerously exposed: He faces fierce critics on the American left and an angry Republican base looking for a political scapegoat.

Trump is now signaling that a renewal of war is “highly unlikely” if talks with Iran show progress. Yet after last week’s midterm collapse, his close ally Chris Ruddy noted on the BBC, there was an 80 percent chance that the U.S. president could still authorize military action against Tehran. Netanyahu thus retains every incentive to gamble on regional escalation in order to entrench his political survival, even at the direct expense of Israel’s democratic resilience and its international standing. It appears that he has nowhere left to run.

An unpredictable lame-duck U.S. president may feel that he has little left to lose, but for Netanyahu and an increasingly isolated Israeli public, the stakes could not be higher.

Azriel Bermant is an associate researcher at the Institute of International Relations Prague as well as the author of Margaret Thatcher and the Middle East and the Azriel’s Substack newsletter.
