#5967. The True Meaning of Power

Since its founding in 1922, Foreign Affairs has been the leading forum for serious discussion of American foreign policy and global affairs. The magazine has featured contributions from many leading international affairs experts.

What the Godfather of Realism Can Teach Trump

“International politics, like all politics, is a struggle for power.” Hans Morgenthau wrote this sentence from his desk in Chicago at the dawn of the Cold War, as the United States and the Soviet Union embarked on an open-ended competition for global dominance. Politics Among Nations, the volume that Morgenthau ultimately produced, would quickly become the most widely adopted international relations textbook in the United States and provide an intellectual framework—realism—that would guide generations of scholars and policymakers.

Although much has changed in the nearly eight decades since Morgenthau’s most influential work was published, the fundamentals remain the same. Human nature, which for Morgenthau drove the struggle for power, is unmoved. States are still the most significant actors in the international system and compete for influence. Great powers continue to drive the course of history. Much of today’s world would seem to affirm Morgenthau’s conclusions: Russia’s attempts to establish a sphere of influence in Eastern Europe, China’s quest for hegemony in East Asia, and the United States’ revival of the Monroe Doctrine in the Western Hemisphere all suggest the enduring primacy of national interests over moral aspirations and international norms.

For many, Morgenthau’s contributions begin and end with his identification of power as the key determinant of state behavior. But this reading misses one of his core observations: that even if humans tend to be power-maximizing creatures, they are also moral beings who seek to limit their own power. Morgenthau’s writings reveal a dialectic between power and morality—two seemingly incompatible concepts that he spent his life attempting to reconcile. Far from romanticizing power, Morgenthau saw the need to tame it.

An immigrant who escaped Nazi Germany and owed his life to the United States, Morgenthau had specific guidance to offer his fellow Americans. Even as he urged the United States to protect its vital interests by maintaining a favorable balance of power in key regions, he implored the country to wield its power prudently. Morgenthau saw the United States as the only country with both the capacity and the will to fight on behalf of transcendent values that he believed power had to serve. He was committed to preserving American power as a check on the violence and bigotry he experienced. Yet he feared that U.S. overreach would hollow out the foundations of that power. This, for Morgenthau, would be a tragedy for humanity.

Perhaps no government has misread Morgenthau more than the second Trump administration. Its officials revel in the exercise of military force around the world, from Iran to Venezuela and the Caribbean, while dismissing concerns of alleged war crimes and threatening long-standing allies. White House Deputy Chief of Staff Stephen Miller typified the Trumpian outlook when, in January, he invoked the “iron laws of the world,” one “governed by strength,” “governed by force,” and “governed by power.”

Some analysts have also erroneously rooted the Trump administration’s actions in realism. A lengthy essay in The New York Times Magazine, for instance, ran under the headline “The Theory That Gives Trump a Blank Check for Aggression.” This is understandable, as the Trump administration has repeatedly claimed its actions are underpinned by “flexible realism.”

But any reading of realism that does not consider the moral dimensions of Morgenthau’s theory is both mistaken and dangerous. When the United States limits its own power, abiding by international norms, consulting allies and partners, and wielding its strength in pursuit of transcendent values, countries find American influence more palatable, which helps sustain it. But if Washington’s actions become untethered from these restraints, others will no longer defer to American prerogatives, and the world will become a more dangerous place.

Returning to the foundations of realism—to the man who introduced this intellectual tradition to Americans—is critical. If Morgenthau were to witness this moment in U.S. history, he would conclude that the country had overlearned his lessons about the importance of power and erred in equating it with the use of force. He would see a need to elevate moral questions and rebuild normative constraints on the exercise of power. And he would warn against foreign entanglements that undermine American democracy, which remains the wellspring of the country’s power. These are the lessons that will help the United States navigate an era of intensified great-power competition, one in which the margin for error is thin and the cost of miscalculation could be catastrophic.

Morgenthau was born into an assimilated, middle-class Jewish family in southern Germany in the winter of 1904. His conservative and authoritarian father, Ludwig, raised his only child to revere the monarchy and take pride in their country’s achievements. Morgenthau’s childhood might have passed as that of a shy, precocious boy in a self-assured bourgeois community, but Germany’s defeat in World War I and the rise of anti-Semitism set his life on a drastically different course.

In 1922, as a top student in his high school, Morgenthau was chosen to address his classmates and neighbors in a public ceremony honoring the school’s namesake. When he began to speak, he noticed that some had turned their backs and others were holding their noses at this “stinking Jew.” Morgenthau finished his remarks, but as he left the square, his classmates hurled anti-Semitic insults and spat on him. Just months later, Hitler descended on Morgenthau’s hometown of Coburg with hundreds of storm troopers, who assaulted protesters while the police looked on. Coburg soon gained the ignominious distinction of being the first town to elect the Nazis to power.

Morgenthau left Coburg hoping to become a writer or poet but was forced to pivot to law after hyperinflation ruined his family’s finances. During his apprenticeship under Hugo Sinzheimer, a leading left-wing labor lawyer, he saw conservative judges undermine the Weimar Republic and aid those who sought its destruction. Morgenthau later reflected in his intellectual autobiography that this led him to conclude that “what was decisive was not the merits of different legal interpretations but the distribution of political power.”

As life for German Jews deteriorated, he fled to Geneva to teach law. Switzerland was unfortunately not far enough away; after Hitler took power in Germany, students began boycotting his classes because they did not want to learn German law from a Jew. He then found a teaching position in Madrid but was displaced by the Spanish Civil War and wandered through Europe before arriving in New York in 1937 as a penniless refugee. Having witnessed the rise of fascism up close, Morgenthau was shocked to find that so many Americans believed that human nature was fundamentally good, that peace was the normal condition of humanity, and that history was a story of progress.

In New York and then Kansas City, Morgenthau grew alarmed as the United States watched Nazi Germany rearm, annex Austria, and invade Czechoslovakia. He believed that a failure to understand human nature had inspired Western democracies’ blind faith in international law and the League of Nations, leading them down the path of least resistance with appeasement of Hitler, straight into the moral abyss.

Shaken by the fall of France in 1940, Morgenthau began writing his first English-language book, taking aim at Americans’ confidence that the methods of the natural sciences could make sense of the social world and that the sheer force of reason would eventually eliminate conflict. In Scientific Man vs. Power Politics, published in 1946, he charged that rationalism and liberalism had misunderstood the nature of man, the nature of the world, and the nature of reason itself. Humans, in addition to having rational needs, possessed irrational impulses that could not be reasoned away. Man’s “lust for power would be satisfied only … if he became like God.” Countries, like humans, sought to maximize their power over one another, making conflict inevitable.

Scientific Man received disastrous reviews, and Morgenthau later recalled that it “shocked people to no end.” He adjusted his approach, tailoring his message to Americans and packaging it in a textbook that would appeal to their pragmatism. He wanted this book to mark an evolution in American thinking about foreign policy, privately writing that it would differ from its predecessors “as an automobile differs from a horse and buggy.” Two years later, in Politics Among Nations, he achieved that goal. Morgenthau stressed that the struggle for power was inescapable. All states sought to keep, increase, or demonstrate their strength. Disarmament, collective security, and international law would not contain this struggle. Only a balance of power could prevent countries from attempting to overthrow the existing order. Power could be tamed only by countervailing power.

It is easy to see why many misread Morgenthau as a champion of realpolitik—an heir to the likes of Machiavelli or Bismarck. After all, he placed power at the center of his analysis. But he never romanticized it. As he wrote in a private letter, “I am an analyst of the nation state and of power and have emphasized time and again their negative moral connotations.” Morgenthau’s personal encounters with power had taught him that its unbridled pursuit led to calamity. He understood that normative and moral limitations were required to keep the struggle for power within tolerable bounds. As he wrote in another letter, “It is the task of civilized societies to temper the power drives and direct them into socially useful channels.”

Indeed, Morgenthau had less tolerance for those who failed to reconcile power with morality than for those who underestimated power’s importance. Although President Woodrow Wilson’s universalism and idealism made him one of Morgenthau’s favorite targets, he preferred him to a Machiavellian prince: “Wilson’s failure was at least the failure of greatness, of too great a vision. Nobody will say as much for the epigones of Machiavelli.” He similarly criticized the British historian and political theorist E. H. Carr, whose moral relativism had led him to advocate for appeasing Hitler. Carr, he wrote, had “no transcendent point of view from which to survey the political scene and to appraise the phenomenon of power.” While both men are seen as pivotal realist thinkers, Morgenthau witheringly concluded of Carr that “it is a dangerous thing to be a Machiavelli. It is a disastrous thing to be a Machiavelli without virtù”—that is, it is ruinous to practice power politics without skill and judgment.

In Politics Among Nations, Morgenthau took issue with leaders who both glorify the struggle for power and reject any limits on their own use of power. He argued that “philosophies and political systems which have made the lust and the struggle for power their mainstay have proved impotent and self-destructive.” And he drew attention to common moral standards that could sustain order and moderate the struggle for power. He observed that throughout history, humans insisted on subjecting political action to ethical scrutiny. When such restraints held, countries did less than their power enabled them to do; once commonplace practices, such as assassinating a rival country’s leaders or killing a large portion of its civilian population to reduce its strength, became stigmatized. Morgenthau concluded, “What has changed is the influence of civilization which makes what is desirable and feasible ethically reprehensible and, hence, normally impossible of execution.”

In the immediate aftermath of World War II, however, he believed that the limitations on the struggle for power had reached their weakest point since the advent of the modern state system. Hitler had attempted to extinguish a whole people, and both the Axis and the Allied powers had bombed cities indiscriminately. Although Morgenthau believed the Allies were morally justified in taking such actions to defeat a genocidal regime, he nonetheless feared that the struggle for power was on its way to becoming boundless. In the nuclear age, failing to reestablish these moral barriers could mean the end of humanity.

Morgenthau’s hard-nosed reputation has long obscured the moral core of his realism. He was unequivocal that power must be a means to an end—“an attempt to realize moral values through the medium of politics.” He recognized that maximizing power for its own sake was both destructive and pointless, arguing that “a nation must pursue its interests for the sake of a transcendent purpose which gives meaning to the day-to-day operations of its foreign policy.” In his view, ancient Greece and Rome are remembered because their power served a purpose that outlived their empires: Athenian democracy and philosophy, and Roman law and republican government.

As he assimilated and raised a family in Chicago, Morgenthau came to see the United States as the inheritor of this tradition. The country’s contribution to the world and the source of its power lay in its democracy and civic life. A realist approach—one that prudently wielded power abroad—was best suited to protect the American experiment at home, enabling Americans to live in freedom and security while offering an example for other countries to emulate. In other words, American power had to be bound to purpose—a purpose the country fulfilled “as a light to attract strangers, not as a flame to be spread throughout the world.” This was not merely the observation of a dispassionate scholar. The United States had accepted Morgenthau when no other country would, and it was a place where immigrants like him could rise to the top of their fields.

By the 1960s, Morgenthau saw that American power faced a threat of its own making. He began to develop deep misgivings about U.S. intervention in Vietnam, warning as early as 1961 that it could become a quagmire reminiscent of France’s war in Algeria. Two years later, as clashes between the South Vietnamese government and protesters intensified, Morgenthau urged the United States to get out. He built his opposition on realist grounds, arguing that a communist takeover of Vietnam would not affect the balance of power in Asia, thus rejecting the domino theory, which held that communism would spread inexorably outward once it gained a regional foothold. Morgenthau also recognized that communism was no longer monolithic and believed that Ho Chi Minh could lead a nonaligned country as Tito did in Yugoslavia. He saw the conflict as a civil war and worried that the United States was attempting to maintain a status quo that was itself the source of instability.

Successive administrations failed to heed Morgenthau’s warnings and instead plunged the United States deeper into the war. He came to resent American policymakers’ misappropriation of his lessons on power and entered public debate with new vigor. At a packed rally in New York’s Madison Square Garden in June 1965, he warned that a guerrilla war could be won only through the indiscriminate killing of the South Vietnamese people, which would tear at the moral fiber of the United States. Later that month, Morgenthau debated President Lyndon Johnson’s national security adviser, McGeorge Bundy, on national television. He led with his moral outrage: “I am opposed to our present policy in Vietnam on moral, military, political, and general intellectual grounds.”

The greatest risk of the war, Morgenthau discerned, was “the risk to ourselves, to our mission in the world, to our very existence as a distinct nation.” He lamented, “Here is the last best hope of the downtrodden and the enslaved, to which men of good will throughout the world have looked as a shining example, relieving its frustration in blind ideological fury and aimless destructiveness upon a helpless people.” Vietnam, in his telling, had corrupted the very purpose that gave American power its meaning.

The Vietnam War validated Morgenthau’s belief that power untethered from questions of morality would lead to overreach. It proved that the United States had an interest in constraining itself, if only to avoid disaster. More fundamentally, Vietnam showed that misadventures abroad could boomerang, weakening American democracy and societal cohesion and, ultimately, undermining the country’s international standing.

The second Trump administration’s foreign policy record has thrown these insights into sharp relief. Its officials view questions of morality as a costly sideshow, the remit of woolly-minded idealists who do not understand how the world works. The White House has time and again resorted to military force, striking boats in the Caribbean, kidnapping Venezuelan President Nicolás Maduro, blockading Cuba, and waging war on Iran. It has also threatened military action against Canada and Denmark, two of the country’s long-standing allies.

American democracy, far from serving as “a light to attract strangers,” is fast becoming a cautionary tale. The second Trump administration has undermined the foundations of American power through attacks on the courts, the press, and universities. It has gleefully bulldozed through any checks on its writ. Its assault on institutions and its aggressive foreign policy are intertwined; the administration not only failed to seek congressional authorization for the war in Iran, but it also refused to even make the case to the American people before striking.

President Donald Trump has justified his aggression on the basis of the “iron laws that have always determined global power.” Morgenthau would not have recognized this as realism. He contended that “superior power gives no right, either moral or legal, to do with that power all that it is physically capable of doing.” The Trump administration, by contrast, seems to believe that to not use the power at its disposal is to waste it. Morgenthau’s verdict on Carr applies equally to Trump: he is a “Machiavelli without virtù,” playing power politics without judgment and leading his country to disaster.

Morgenthau would argue that the Trump administration misunderstands the very concept of power, which should not be equated with military might and must instead be understood as a complex psychological relationship. Real power, Morgenthau insisted, lies in the ability to influence a country through consent, not coercion. In this view, the use of force signifies the abdication of political power rather than its culmination. The Trump administration’s belligerence betrays its weakness: its acts of aggression around the world are confessions of its inability to shape events through other means.

Although Morgenthau grew up on the statecraft of Bismarck, he came to admire Abraham Lincoln above all others, even attempting to write a book about him toward the end of his life. Morgenthau saw in Lincoln a rare leader who was never possessed by power, instead possessing it and, ultimately, subordinating it to transcendent values. Today, just as at the height of the Vietnam War, Morgenthau would see his task as restoring morality and ethics to their rightful place and highlighting the need for power to move the world toward justice.

The United States has lost its purpose, and unless it rediscovers it, discrete foreign policy successes will be irrelevant. As Morgenthau wrote, if the country loses its soul, any victories are merely “footnotes to the history of the decline and fall of a once great nation.”

DAVID SACKS is Senior Fellow for Asia Studies at the Council on Foreign Relations and the author of the forthcoming book The Realist: Hans Morgenthau and the Purpose of American Power.

#5965. Democrats accused of 'No Kings' hypocrisy over push to change voter-approved trans sports measures

The ‘Outnumbered’ panel discusses Democratic efforts to overturn voter-approved protections for girls’ sports, including measures in Washington and Colorado. They also examine divisions within the Republican Party as Reps. Steve Scalise and Jim Jordan compete for House leadership following the midterm elections.

Voters in two Democratic strongholds approved restrictions on transgender participation in girls' sports last week, delivering rare Republican victories amid a blue wave. But Democratic officials are already discussing ways to change the measures, drawing accusations of hypocrisy from Republicans who point to their support for the "No Kings" movement.

Colorado Gov.-elect Philip Weiser, who also criticized a separate ballot measure restricting gender-related surgeries for minors, and Rep. Pramila Jayapal , D-Wash., both raised objections to the measures approved in their respective states.

At his first post-election news conference, he suggested Colorado's Democratic-controlled legislature could revisit the voter-approved measures, saying that it is "statutory … and a natural and I believe appropriate role for the legislature to say ‘what are the adjustments that need to be added."

PRO-TRANS ACTIVISTS HOLD RALLY AGAINST WASHINGTON STATE BALLOT INITIATIVE TO PROTECT GIRLS SPORTS

Colorado voters approved ballot measures increasing penalties for fentanyl-related offenses, restricting transgender athletes from competing in sports designated for the opposite biological sex and prohibiting gender-related surgeries for minors. The measures passed even as Democrats swept into power across the state.

COLORADO MOM WHO SAID DAUGHTER WAS SECRETLY TRANSITIONED HELPS LEAD EFFORT TO BAN BOYS IN GIRLS' SPORTS

Colorado Gov.-elect Philip Weiser, pictured July 22, 2025, suggested changes to voter-approved ballot measures following the November 2026 election. (Hyoung Chang/The Denver Post via Getty Images)

Weiser did not respond to a request for comment.

Republicans in suburban Denver criticized the governor-elect, dubbing him "King Phil I" and characterizing his response as "know[ing] better than Colorado voters in a healthy democracy."

"But before the ink on the ballots is even dry, Governor-elect Phil Weiser is already signaling that the vote of the people is merely a ‘suggestion’. With Coloradans approving ballot measures to crack down on fentanyl and protect youth sports and children, Weiser walked up to the podium on Wednesday and essentially declared that the will of the majority needs to pass his personal vibe check first," the Adams County Republican Party said in a statement.

Republicans characterized Weiser’s position as "you voted for it, but I don’t like it…"

"No Kings," the Adams Republicans added, invoking the protest movement that Weiser has supported through participation in "No Kings" protests. The movement's demonstrations have criticized President Donald Trump over what organizers characterize as authoritarian conduct.

The Colorado House Republican caucus echoed the sentiment, adding in a statement: "If the Democrats truly believe there should be ‘No Kings’ in America, it is time they take their own advice."

'NO KINGS' FOOT SOLDIERS IGNORE ORGANIZERS' WARNING, GO ALL IN FOR DEMOCRATS

Meanwhile, in Washington State, Jayapal condemned a similar measure approved by voters.

Initiative 638 prohibits students defined as "biologically male" from competing in girls' K-12 school sports and requires students seeking to participate on girls' teams to provide verification of their biological sex from a health care provider. The measure passed with approximately 56% support in the reliably Democratic state.

Following the election results, multiple local news outlets reported that Jayapal pledged to "take our state back" after voters approved the initiative.

"We're going to start planning for how we overturn these bad initiatives and how we go back to loving everyone with gender inclusivity the way we have for decades," she said, according to several reports.

Jayapal did not respond to a request for comment.

Critics also pointed to Jayapal's past support for "No Kings" protests, accusing her of contradicting her own rhetoric about respecting the will of voters.

One critic highlighted a November 2025 social media post in which Jayapal wrote: "We have democracy for a reason. America doesn’t belong to kings it belongs to the people and we're going to keep it that way."

Jayapal also faced previous criticism over her support for the "No Kings" movement after praising remarks by Britain's King Charles III during his visit to the United States.

Washington Democratic Party Chair Shasti Conrad signaled support for a legal challenge, calling the ballot measure "unconstitutional on its face" and indicating the party would "support [for] organizations planning to challenge it in court."

Rep. Pramila Jayapal, D-Wash., speaks during a House Budget Committee hearing on Capitol Hill in Washington, D.C., on April 15, 2026. Jayapal criticized a Washington state ballot measure restricting transgender participation in girls' sports following the November election. (Al Drago/Bloomberg via Getty Images)

Under Washington's Constitution, lawmakers cannot repeal a voter-approved initiative during its first two years but can amend it with a two-thirds vote in each legislative chamber. After two years, the legislature can amend or repeal the measure with a simple majority.

The controversy leaves Democrats in both states confronting a similar political dilemma: whether to preserve the restrictions voters approved or pursue changes through the legislative and judicial processes available to them.

Born and raised in Allentown, Pennsylvania, worked in both the New York and Washington bureaus for Fox News since 2013.

#5964. How New Delhi Sees the Blue Tsunami

This issue is preventing our website from loading properly. Please review the following troubleshooting tips or contact us at [email protected].

Analysis: How New Delhi Sees the Blue Tsunami

Create an FP account to save articles to read later.

Downloadable PDFs are a benefit of an FP subscription.

Contact us at [email protected] to learn about upgrade options, unlocking the ability to gift this article.

In New Delhi, officials and analysts alike awaited the results of the U.S. midterm elections on Nov. 3 with anticipation. U.S. President Donald Trump’s mercurial and idiosyncratic policies both at home and abroad—with few constraints on his choices—have rippled through global capitals since he returned to the White House last year.

India has suffered especially from recent U.S. policy vagaries, which have upturned a bilateral relationship that was by and large steadily improving and deepening. These include Trump’s tariff wars, apparent fondness for Pakistan’s leadership, and harsh approach to immigration. So, now that almost all the midterm results are in—and the Democratic Party will control both houses of Congress—how is India’s political class and attentive public reacting to the outcome?

In New Delhi, officials and analysts alike awaited the results of the U.S. midterm elections on Nov. 3 with anticipation. U.S. President Donald Trump’s mercurial and idiosyncratic policies both at home and abroad—with few constraints on his choices—have rippled through global capitals since he returned to the White House last year.

India has suffered especially from recent U.S. policy vagaries, which have upturned a bilateral relationship that was by and large steadily improving and deepening. These include Trump’s tariff wars, apparent fondness for Pakistan’s leadership, and harsh approach to immigration. So, now that almost all the midterm results are in—and the Democratic Party will control both houses of Congress—how is India’s political class and attentive public reacting to the outcome?

At one level, the results have generated some hope for the future of bilateral ties. New Delhi’s guarded optimism stems from a belief that a Democratic Congress might place some checks on the Trump administration’s propensity to make feckless decisions with adverse consequences for New Delhi. After all, the U.S.-India partnership enjoyed a bipartisan consensus for the last few decades, until Trump’s second term.

Despite this sense of relief, Indian observers still harbor some qualms toward the United States. They are aware that any U.S. president wields substantial power in conducting foreign policy. Trump has made considerable use of this authority with a pliant Republican Congress, from his legally dubious ouster of Venezuelan President Nicolás Maduro to his unpopular and undeclared war in Iran. New Delhi will watch intently for early signs of the new Congress reining in his tendency to act with little hesitation or consultation.

A host of other issues remain on the minds of New Delhi’s foreign-policy establishment. Indian officials are aware that U.S. lawmakers on both sides of the aisle have concerns about India’s dependence on and willingness to purchase Russian oil. This poses a dilemma for New Delhi, which cannot easily dispense with these imports because of high costs and its refining facilities, which are tied to specific grades of crude oil.

If external pressures mount to dilute ties between New Delhi and Moscow, India’s pro-Russia lobby may feel more inclined to urge Prime Minister Narendra Modi’s government to adopt a tougher stance toward Washington. A more consistent approach toward Russian oil sales from the U.S. Congress, without exemptions for otherwise friendly nations, might cause India to adopt a more intransigent stance.

Furthermore, many people in New Delhi—both within and outside the government—remain very concerned about the Trump administration’s dalliance with Islamabad since India and Pakistan’s military conflict last year. At the time of the crisis, which came in the wake of a terrorist attack in Indian-administered Kashmir, Trump asserted that he had stepped in to induce a cease-fire between the two neighboring rivals. India, long averse to any external involvement in the Kashmir dispute, denies that Trump played a role in ending the conflict.

Pakistan, however, said the Trump administration was crucial in bringing about a cease-fire and nominated the U.S. president for the Nobel Peace Prize. Trump, in turn, invited Pakistani Army chief Asim Munir to lunch at the White House. The United States has since taken an interest in Pakistan’s mineral and cryptocurrency resources, and the country has offered the Trump administration several lucrative deals.

Indian observers will pay close attention to the relevant, newly constituted congressional committees to see if they evince any interest in looking closely at the U.S.-Pakistan strategic nexus. Among other matters, U.S. lawmakers may scrutinize the investments that Trump’s children have made in Pakistan, especially in cryptocurrency.

Many in New Delhi’s foreign-policy establishment will likely welcome some limits on Trump’s presidential powers. But with the victory of a few Democrats who care deeply about human rights issues both at home and abroad—such as civil liberties and minority rights—India may now confront a renewed focus on the state of its democracy under Modi. Trump, of course, has cared little about democratic backsliding and tended to give India a pass.

Historically, New Delhi’s stock responses to U.S. criticism of its human rights record and the state of its democracy have been twofold. First, that these matters fall under India’s purview, and external review impinges on its sovereignty. Second, that its own institutional mechanisms can provide suitable redress for any failures to uphold democratic principles. These retorts may not satisfy Democrats, who may not be inclined to sanction India but could pass non-binding resolutions that New Delhi might find irksome.

Other nettlesome bilateral issues—especially on trade and tariffs—may see some progress. In recent weeks, both the United States and India publicly suggested that a trade deal was nearing completion after protracted negotiations. On this front, officials in New Delhi feel somewhat sanguine; the trade accord does not depend on congressional approval. More to the point, if Trump is constrained in other arenas, he might want to score a foreign-policy victory.

Even with a finalized trade deal, the wariness that has crept into New Delhi’s view of Washington will not easily dissipate. To begin with, Trump’s fitful policies have undermined the trust so carefully nurtured by his predecessors. Restoring even a modicum of this trust will require some politically costly moves, such as offering concessions on trade or signaling a willingness to ease up on visa restrictions.

Despite the electoral rout of the Republicans, it remains an open question whether Trump will prove willing to adopt a more conciliatory approach to foreign policy.

Other differences between the United States and India on the global stage are likely to persist. Trump is reportedly weighing further major military action against Iran. Since the new Congress will not convene until January, the president may have an incentive to act soon. A renewed campaign in Iran would not be viewed favorably in New Delhi, as it would again disrupt vital oil supplies and place Indian sailors and expatriates in the Middle East at risk.

Modi’s government faces growing domestic dissent over allegations of electoral misconduct. These protests have shown few signs of abating and have energized India’s political opposition. Under the present circumstances, New Delhi cannot afford to remain silent if Trump embarks on another campaign in Iran with significant ramifications for India, including driving up the cost of fuel.

There is little question that last week’s Democratic victory amounts to a seismic shift in U.S. politics. Its effects on Trump’s foreign-policy choices, especially between now and January, remain unclear. New Delhi is likely to wait until the new Congress takes office before it tips its hand. But ultimately, from the Indian perspective, seriously repairing the relationship might be a task best deferred until after the 2028 U.S. presidential election.

Sumit Ganguly is a columnist at Foreign Policy and senior fellow at Stanford University’s Hoover Institution, where he directs the Huntington Program on Strengthening U.S.-India Relations.

#5962. Senate GOP rebellion fizzles as Thune keeps power despite historic midterm massacre

Sen. Rick Scott, R-Fla., isn’t ready to back John Thune for another term as Senate GOP leader. He and other Republicans want specifics on how Thune, or another contender, would get work done as Democrats prepare to take control.

There was neither an exodus nor a mass housecleaning in the Senate GOP leadership, despite frustration and rumblings of discontent among some lawmakers reeling from last week’s devastating midterm losses.

Senate Majority Leader John Thune, R-S.D., secured another term as the top Republican behind the closed doors of the Old Senate Chamber, where lawmakers charted their course for their few remaining weeks in power and beyond.

Thune, along with the bulk of his leadership team, which now includes Sen. Bernie Moreno, R-Ohio, were swiftly elected after a group of Republicans failed to delay the leadership elections.

It wasn't without a fight, though, with conversations behind closed doors dragging on for over seven hours.

GOP SENATOR WITHHOLDS SUPPORT FOR THUNE AS LEADERSHIP SHOWDOWN LOOMS

Senate Majority Leader John Thune, R-S.D., talks with reporters in the Capitol Visitor Center on Nov. 9, 2026. (CQ-Roll Call, Inc. via Getty Images)

Sen. Ron Johnson, R-Wis., led the charge to delay, and didn't stick around after his attempts to do so failed on a 31 to 15 vote in the Old Senate Chamber to proceed with the scheduled election.

Johnson and others wanted to have a deep conversation about what had happened during the midterm elections, and a roadmap on what Republican leadership candidates could accomplish with the little time they had left in power.

SENATE GOP REBELS MOVE TO DELAY LEADERSHIP VOTE, DEMAND RECKONING AFTER MIDTERM DISASTER

But there was no opposition to any of the lawmakers running for leadership.

"This has never been anything against John Thune," Johnson said. "I think he has been very unfairly attacked, you know, by our base, and I don't agree with that. What I didn't agree with was holding the election right away, giving nobody an opportunity to really challenge any leadership position."

On Monday, Thune was adamant that he’d still run but was unsure of the outcome.

"That's the hope," Thune said when asked if he had the Senate GOP’s support. "You never know right until they vote."

Before the vote, Johnson and a handful of others believed there should be a shakeup in leadership, but didn’t offer an alternative to Thune.

"After the stunning defeat, you just can't continue on business as usual," Johnson said. "I mean, that kind of defeat demands change."

When asked if he’d be interested in running, Johnson said, "That’s part of the problem. Would you want it?"

"I think there's a number of people with talent," Johnson said. "Again, I'm not — at a minimum, we need to see out of our leadership team what's the strategy moving forward, and you can't come off of a defeat like that and just ram through elections and oh yeah, ‘let's just elect that leadership team by acclamation.’"

Sen. Rick Scott, R-Fla., had previously run for leader twice and most recently against Thune. But the lawmaker, who joined Johnson’s crusade for change, declined to throw his hat into the arena again.

"I think we have to acknowledge the voters are not happy," Scott said. "We have to start listening to voters."

CONGRESS RETURNS TO BRUTAL LAME-DUCK STRETCH WITH SHUTDOWN DEADLINE, LEADERSHIP RACES: 'CLOSET IS FULL OF S—'

Sen. Ron Johnson speaks during a hearing of the Senate Homeland Security and Governmental Affairs Committee in the Dirksen Senate Office Building on Feb. 12, 2026, in Washington, D.C. (Chip Somodevilla/Getty Images)

Scott wanted ironclad promises on the GOP’s to-do list, including ramming through judicial nominees, government funding "without a massive omnibus," a stock trade ban, permitting reform, passing the National Defense Authorization Act, taking another crack at the SAVE America Act and balancing the budget — possibly through budget reconciliation.

Johnson and Scott were joined by Sens. Ted Budd, R-N.C., Mike Lee, R-Utah, John Kennedy, R-La., Jim Banks, R-Ind., and Dave McCormick, R-Pa., in a push to delay the elections that was not met, and the would-be coup faded as the secret ballots were cast.

Still, there were others outside that cohort who weren’t happy with last week and believed that there should be deeper soul-searching on what Republicans did over the last two years that led to a historic night for Senate Democrats.

Sen. Josh Hawley, R-Mo., contended that much of what the GOP listed as accomplishments over their time in power is what worked against them.

"And, you know, you have the kind of record that this Congress has had, I think we better have a pretty tough family conversation about what it is we're doing," Hawley said.

"When you cut Medicaid, but you fund data centers, when you cut rural hospital funding, but you fund Planned Parenthood, when you let the cost of healthcare for working people go through the roof, but you can't do anything for them on other issues of affordability, from fertilizer to gas," he continued. "Yeah you know what? The public doesn’t like that very much."

Alex Miller is a writer for Fox News Digital covering the U.S. Senate.

#5960. Democrats Shouldn’t Squander Their Chance to Launch a Foreign Policy Revolution

Since its founding in 1922, Foreign Affairs has been the leading forum for serious discussion of American foreign policy and global affairs. The magazine has featured contributions from many leading international affairs experts.

MATTHEW DUSS is Executive Vice President of the Center for International Policy. From 2017 to 2022, he served as Chief Foreign Policy Adviser to Senator Bernie Sanders.

The Imperative of Ambition in a New Congress

Tuesday’s election made clear that endless wars, political corruption, and the high cost of living are three of the American public’s top concerns. They are deeply interrelated—and the new Congress must address all three. That will require a true foreign policy revolution that grapples not just with the outrages and failures of the Trump administration’s foreign policy but also with the ways in which past Democratic or Democrat-supported foreign policies have contributed to today’s crisis. There will be a temptation among some Democratic leaders, in Congress and beyond, to limit the scope or pace of change. But to find a way out of today’s crisis and move beyond the failures that have afflicted American foreign policy for the past several decades, Democrats will need to have the courage to face a hard truth: that President Donald Trump’s abuses of power and unilateral warmaking are not so much a break from Washington’s militarist mindset as they are a particularly aggressive, predatory expression of it.

The incoming Democratic majority will face a national security environment in which the most severe challenges to U.S. safety and prosperity come from within. That’s why Congress should shut down the Iran war immediately but also recognize that it can make the United States more secure only if it pairs a foreign policy shift with a frontal assault on spiraling costs and political corruption. Political polarization; government corruption and dysfunction; increasing dominance of political and economic life by a small handful of mercurial oligarchs; and a president who recklessly and unilaterally launches unauthorized military interventions while relentlessly seeking opportunities to enrich himself, his family, and his cronies—all pose more immediate danger to the safety and prosperity of the American people than does any foreign enemy.

When Democrats take control of Congress in January, they will have considerable power to challenge the corruption and malfeasance of the Trump administration. Control of committees and of the congressional calendar will provide the opportunity for sorely needed oversight. But although the Trump administration deserves aggressive investigation, Democrats must aim higher—and use the opportunity to articulate a genuinely compelling alternative for American foreign policy.

The first and most urgent task will be ending the Iran war, a reckless and unpopular conflict that has become even less popular as Americans have felt its effects on their wallets. Congress must respond to widespread bipartisan anger among voters at Trump’s war and abuse of war powers by using all the tools it has, especially its power to cut funding. It has been encouraging to see Democrats continuing to oppose any supplemental budget request by the administration for the war. It is an area where Democrats have gotten tripped up in the past when they’ve been afraid of accusations that they don’t “support the troops” if they oppose funding for foolish, counterproductive wars. With Democrats having held firm against any additional funding for the war, those days appear to be gone. That is good.

But Democrats need to go further. They should be prepared to oppose all funding for the military, including blocking votes on the National Defense Authorization Act, the annual bill setting out the broad scope and budget for the Defense Department, in order to put an end to hostilities in Iran. In fact, not only should they be willing to withhold funding from the Pentagon, but they should also be willing to withhold funding for the entire government, and threaten another government shutdown if the bill does not prohibit any funding for Trump’s war. December 11 is the deadline for a continuing resolution to keep the government funded and operating. If ever there was a reason for Democrats to threaten to withhold their votes, stopping a blatantly illegal and globally destructive war that American voters have now resoundingly rebuked is it.

Democrats must also be willing to take a hard look at the continuity between Trump’s use of military violence and past uses of force by administrations of both parties, and the bipartisan support for that violence. The groundwork for the Iran war was laid by the Biden administration’s unstinting support for Israel’s genocide in Gaza (as even some former administration officials have acknowledged), but also by years of U.S. refusal to impose any consequences on the Israeli government despite clear evidence of ongoing violations of human rights and international law. Trump’s strikes on alleged “narcoterrorist” drug boats in the Caribbean represent the logical evolution of long-standing treatment of the United States’ drug problem as a “war,” and of casually applying the label of “terrorist” to justify any and all measures, including execution without due process. Seeking accountability for these abuses will require acknowledging the past complicity of both Republicans and Democrats.

Democrats must also take on corruption, a key node connecting the country’s domestic and foreign policy crises. In doing so, they will be walking through an open door. Polls consistently show that political corruption is high on Americans’ list of concerns. According to a recent Harris poll conducted for The Guardian, “Nearly nine out of 10 Americans said that government corruption, as defined as elected officials putting their own or their donors’ financial interests ahead of the public’s, is a problem.” A mid-October CNBC All-America Economic Survey found that voters are more concerned about government corruption than any other issue, including high grocery and gas prices. In September, Gallup reported that “Americans’ perceptions of government corruption in the U.S. are at their highest level in 20 years, with 89% of U.S. adults saying it is widespread.”

To say that the Trump administration provides a target-rich environment for a strong anticorruption push is to commit felony understatement. Exposing foreign gifts to Trump and conflicts of interest by family members, contracts awarded to Trump’s oligarch donors and new tech buddies, and the selling of pardons, as well as restoring enforcement of the Corporate Transparency Act, the Foreign Corrupt Practices Act, and related task forces, should all be part of the plan. Democrats should drill down into Trump’s wildly corrupt “Board of Peace” for Gaza, which U.S. Representative Jamie Raskin, the Maryland Democrat set to chair the House Judiciary Committee, rightly referred to as a “global slush fund.” The obvious conflicts of interests of Trump’s diplomatic envoys Steve Witkoff and Jared Kushner also demand investigation.

The good news is that effective anticorruption does not necessarily require new legislation; it merely requires that existing legislation be enforced. An August 2025 essay in Foreign Affairs, co-authored with my colleagues Casey Michel and Trevor Sutton, laid out a more expansive case for Democrats putting anticorruption at the center of their foreign policy platform. (A recent essay by Casey Michel lays out a more detailed agenda for the new Congress.)

Ultimately, the most important step toward tackling corruption will be reforming the campaign finance system. The United States is an outlier among developed democracies in that its system of campaign finance allows for what is essentially legalized bribery. Legislators speak openly about campaign contributors expecting “returns on investment” for their political donations. This is an outrage. In recent years, foreign actors have increasingly begun bankrolling American legislators, taking advantage of massive loopholes in campaign finance laws and the Federal Election Commission’s lack of enforcement.

Confronting this will be no small task. At a minimum, Congress must move to legislate a response to the Supreme Court’s 2010 Citizens United decision, which effectively greenlighted bottomless campaign funding from American corporations. It must also reform the Federal Election Commission, providing the body with much-needed resources to investigate campaign finance crimes and restoring the practice of allowing congressional appointments to the commission, which has become little more than an appendage of the executive branch.

All of the above steps must be connected to the top concern among American voters at this moment—the high cost of living. Democrats have disagreements in several policy areas, particularly in foreign policy. Their coalition is diverse. But there is consensus around addressing Americans’ material needs urgently and aggressively. Such an approach would confront Trump’s reckless use of tariffs (while recognizing that the neoliberal taboo on tariffs is a thing of the past), rising gas prices because of the Iran war, the need to restore the global minimum tax, the threat of job losses to AI, and the increased power of tech oligarchs over Americans’ economic and political lives, including on foreign policy, as Elon Musk’s restriction of Ukraine’s Starlink connection demonstrated. The approach should involve a mix of tactics, including hearings, oversight investigations, and legislative proposals.

Many of these efforts may not produce new laws in the next two years. But they could become the backbone of a winning agenda for Congress and for a Democratic administration in 2029. Most important, they would represent a step toward the forging of a new American political consensus on which a new and durable foreign policy can be built.

How Far Will He Go to Make Peace—and War?

How It Can Reclaim Its Constitutional Powers

#5959. Florida AG subpoenas DSA over potential illegal funding from Cuba and China

The AG’s office is examining whether the Democratic Socialists of America took contributions from ‘foreign sources of concern.’ (Credit: @AGJamesUthmeier).

Florida Attorney General James Uthmeier announced Tuesday that his office issued an investigative subpoena to the Democratic Socialists of America (DSA) to investigate whether the organization accepted contributions from "foreign sources of concern," including entities in Cuba and China.

"Our laws prohibit accepting contributions from foreign sources of concern," Uthmeier said in a video statement. "We will not allow foreign adversaries to influence Florida through the backdoor of non-private fundraising."

The subpoena demands documents related to recent DSA delegations to China and Cuba, where organization members met with Cuban government officials, to determine if the DSA violated Florida's Deceptive and Unfair Trade Practices Act, according to Uthmeier.

Florida Attorney General James Uthmeier issued a subpoena to the Democratic Socialists of America to determine whether the organization accepted donations from "foreign sources of concern," including entities in Cuba and China. (Getty Images)

Uthmeier did not detail specific criminal allegations or name individuals accused of violating state law.

The DSA has repeatedly praised the Cuban regime and routinely advocates for lifting American sanctions and ending the U.S. embargo against Havana.

#5957. A Recipe for Disaster in Taiwan

Since its founding in 1922, Foreign Affairs has been the leading forum for serious discussion of American foreign policy and global affairs. The magazine has featured contributions from many leading international affairs experts.

REBECCA LISSNER is Senior Fellow for U.S. Foreign Policy and Director of the Future of American Strategy Initiative at the Council on Foreign Relations. She served as Deputy Assistant to the President and Principal Deputy National Security Adviser to the Vice President during the Biden administration.

MIRA RAPP-HOOPER is a Visiting Senior Fellow at the Brookings Institution. She was Special Assistant to the President and Senior Director for East Asia and Oceania and Director for Indo-Pacific Strategy at the U.S. National Security Council during the Biden administration.

Just a few years ago, U.S. President Joe Biden caused a stir when he confirmed publicly that his administration would defend Taiwan if the island were attacked by China—a divergence from the traditional U.S. policy of strategic ambiguity, in which Washington does not commit to taking any particular action. In May, U.S. President Donald Trump made waves for taking a different position. In a television interview, he described Taiwan as “a very good negotiating chip” in dealings with Beijing and mused that the island was close to China but far from the United States, implying that U.S. interests in Taiwan were negligible. In doing so, Trump seemed to confirm something many Asia watchers had feared: that this U.S. president would be willing to loosen the United States’ commitment to Taiwan to bolster his relationship with Chinese leader Xi Jinping.

A few days later, Secretary of State Marco Rubio told the Senate Foreign Relations Committee that there had been no change in U.S. policy toward Taiwan. But Trump’s words were alarming nonetheless—in part because they reflect a shift in Washington that goes beyond the Oval Office. Since 2025, politicians from both parties have been shying away from affirming the United States’ long-standing support for Taiwan. Meanwhile, China is developing new strategies to coerce Taiwan without going to war, and Beijing will be increasingly tempted to use them if it judges that the U.S. commitment to Taiwan is waning among both politicians and the public.

Yet that would be a grave miscalculation on China’s part. In fact, the United States’ interests in Taiwan are growing, not diminishing. Whatever the nature of Washington’s rhetoric right now, the United States would have to respond to any action by Beijing that threatened those interests, and both countries would then face a great-power crisis. To defuse this dangerous situation, American leaders must update the United States’ Taiwan strategy to reflect a new reality—and they should begin immediately.

Taiwan’s unique international status has long made U.S. policy complex. The Republic of China, now known as Taiwan, was a World War II ally of the United States. When the People’s Republic of China was founded on the mainland, in 1949, the Republic of China’s president, Chiang Kai-shek, decamped to Taiwan, and the United States maintained formal diplomatic ties with Chiang’s government, not the People’s Republic of China. In 1954, as the Cold War crested in the Pacific, the United States concluded a formal mutual defense treaty with Taiwan, in part to forestall increasingly aggressive artillery barrages from the mainland. Nearly 20 years later, however, when U.S. President Richard Nixon and his national security adviser, Henry Kissinger, began to engineer a diplomatic opening to the government in Beijing, they knew it would cost Taiwan.

In part because they understood that building diplomatic ties with Beijing would be painful for Taiwan and cause controversy in Congress, where Taipei had many boosters, Nixon and Kissinger slow-walked the process. So did Nixon’s successor, President Gerald Ford. It finally fell to President Jimmy Carter to make the change. On January 1, 1979, the United States ended its alliance with Taiwan and recognized the mainland government as “the sole legal Government of China.” Congress was irate. Lawmakers from both parties quickly drafted the 1979 Taiwan Relations Act, which remains in force. The law requires Washington to provide Taipei with defensive arms, creating the trappings of an alliance with Taiwan despite the relationship’s unofficial status. In 1982, the Reagan administration made an additional set of commitments to Taiwan, known as the Six Assurances. These include an affirmation that Washington does not endorse Beijing’s sovereignty claims over Taiwan and a promise that the United States would not use arms sales to Taiwan as a bargaining chip.

In the 50 years since, U.S. Taiwan policy has been remarkably successful. Through its partnership and arms sales, the United States has helped underwrite peace and stability in the Taiwan Strait, and Taiwan has thrived as a democracy and an economy, even as Washington has maintained a relationship with Beijing. But U.S. commitments to Taiwan have lately come under increasing strain. China has proceeded with a massive military buildup designed to give Beijing the option to take Taiwan by force; used coercive economic tactics, launched cyberattacks, and interfered in elections to put pressure on Taipei; and run frequent army and coast guard exercises to demonstrate its ability to strangle the island. All of this has stoked concerns across the Pacific that the United States’ long-standing partnership might not be enough to keep the peace in the Taiwan Strait.

The fear that China could attempt to retake Taiwan by force, possibly during Xi’s tenure, has grown in recent years. Analysts have frequently cited 2027 as the beginning of the window of maximum danger, based on a 2021 statement made by Philip Davidson, the former commander of U.S. Indo-Pacific Command, about when the People’s Liberation Army might be prepared for an invasion. But this timeline was never hard and fast, and Xi’s recent purge of more than 100 officers has likely delayed the military’s readiness. U.S. intelligence assessments do not anticipate an invasion of Taiwan next year, and most scholars believe the Chinese government would consider such a move as a last resort only if unification could not be accomplished by other means.

The primary danger now is that Xi will escalate a pressure campaign designed to coerce Taiwan into negotiating a change in its status. Since 2025, China’s leaders have used their diplomatic channels to the Trump administration, including Xi’s direct line to Trump, to insist on American concessions on Taiwan in exchange for progress in trade talks. In May, China secured a pause on a planned $14 billion U.S. arms sale and other defense funding for Taiwan. Beijing may hope that additional incremental moves will further weaken Taiwanese confidence in the United States and that such actions will be difficult for Trump or a future U.S. president to reverse.

China is also clearly planning for more dramatic forms of coercion. It has a few options: since 2022, China’s military has practiced blockades of Taiwan, and its coast guard has simulated a quarantine of the island’s major ports. Beijing could also use customs enforcement or other administrative measures to attempt to assert its jurisdiction over Taiwan while minimizing the likelihood that the United States would intervene to stop it. If Taiwanese President Lai Ching-te, who favors Taiwanese independence but has not pursued it in office, is reelected in 2028 and Chinese leaders believe that the path to a peaceful unification is closing, Xi may consider using these tools against the island in late 2028 or early 2029, before a new U.S. administration takes office. Given Trump’s alienation of U.S. allies in Asia and Europe and Washington’s limited means to respond to a maritime quarantine, Beijing might hope to get away with such a large-scale coercion campaign without inviting serious pushback.

Taiwan’s growing strategic importance makes Beijing’s development of new coercive options more concerning. Washington is no longer maintaining a partnership with Taipei because of a historical commitment or, as Beijing would have the world believe, to needle China. The artificial intelligence boom has put Taiwan at the center of the global economy. Taiwan is a critical supplier for the U.S. tech industry, and the United States recognizes that it cannot be a leader in AI without Taiwan.

The scale of the United States’ economic dependence on Taiwan is revealing. Taiwan is the country’s fifth-largest trading partner; a Chinese blockade of the island would imperil more than $250 billion in trade and could shave more than three percent off U.S. GDP in a year, according to a Bloomberg report. As much as 97 percent of the global supply of the high-end logic chips used in smartphones and other advanced electronics are produced or packaged in Taiwan. Losing access to Taiwanese chips could reduce advanced electronics production globally by 60 percent, with factories forced to rely on their chip inventories. The United States’ auto and medical device industries could deplete their inventories in a matter of weeks. The U.S. firms Amazon, Apple, and Nvidia are among the Taiwan Semiconductor Manufacturing Company’s ten largest customers, which together represent more than $14 trillion in market capitalization. With AI growth buoying the U.S. stock market and the broader economy, a crisis in Taiwan would trigger a crash—potentially one similar in scale to the stock market crash at the start of the COVID-19 pandemic.

The economic effects of a Chinese effort to squeeze Taiwan would not be limited to the United States. The research firm Rhodium Group has conservatively estimated that a yearlong blockade of the island could cost the global economy between $2 trillion and $3 trillion. A full-scale war would be far worse; Bloomberg has estimated that it would cause $10 trillion in economic losses and a global recession. It is therefore increasingly likely that the United States would be forced to act if Taiwan’s supply chains were put in jeopardy. The cost of standing aside would simply be too high.

Yet just when the United States needs to be doing all it can to prevent a crisis, Washington’s support for Taiwan appears to be wobbling. Trump’s musings about Taiwan’s geographical distance from the United States and his use of arms sales as a bargaining chip are the headline examples, but they are not the only indicators of a shaky U.S. commitment. Policy analysts have argued that the United States should change its position to definitively oppose Taiwanese independence or even withdraw U.S. forces from the military bases in Japan and the Philippines that are closest to Taiwan.

Public opinion in the United States is shifting, too. Americans’ feelings toward China have warmed considerably over the past two years, according to a July 2026 Chicago Council on Global Affairs survey. A majority, 57 percent, support friendly cooperation and engagement with China, whereas 40 percent support an active effort to limit the growth of China’s power. The share of Americans who see China’s territorial ambitions as “a critical threat” has decreased by more than ten percentage points since 2022, and those who favor the use of U.S. troops if China invaded Taiwan has fallen from 52 percent to just 38 percent.

In part, the declining support for defending Taiwan reflects Americans’ growing wariness of instability and conflict. A sense that the world was descending into chaos helped fuel Trump’s 2024 campaign; as president, Trump has heightened that sense by taking assertive military action in places such as Iran and Venezuela. Americans are now suffering the economic consequences of nine months of war in the Middle East, further deepening antiwar sentiment, as the midterm election results vividly demonstrate. Candidates for office used to clamor to sound tough on China, but many now blanch at the idea of suggesting that the U.S. military should defend Taiwan against a Chinese invasion, calculating that this is not the time to be seen as a hawk.

Of course, public attitudes can change quickly during a crisis. If China actually committed aggression against Taiwan, Americans could become more supportive of a strong U.S. response. And if the early stages of a conflict saw American casualties or a Chinese attack on U.S. critical infrastructure, the U.S. public could very well rally around the flag and demand a muscular intervention.

For now, however, China may believe that the United States’ will and ability to fight are both diminishing. The war with Iran has substantially reduced U.S. military readiness for a Taiwan contingency. The United States has used up much of its missile defense stock, including roughly two-thirds of its Patriot interceptor inventory, which was already depleted by U.S. aid to Ukraine, and as much as 80 percent of THAAD interceptors against Iranian drones and missiles. Even before the Iran war, analysts had warned that the U.S. military could run out of long-range, precision-guided munitions in a fight over Taiwan; now, the munitions shortfalls have become perilously acute. The war has also drawn military assets away from the Indo-Pacific. In August, Washington moved its last aircraft carrier in Asia, the USS George Washington, out of the region. It has also relocated missile defense systems to the Middle East, including Patriot and THAAD systems that had been deployed in South Korea.

The result is an increased risk of dangerous miscalculation. Should Chinese leaders assess that the United States’ commitment to Taiwan is wavering in both political parties and that the country is ill prepared for a fight, they might conclude that they have an opportunity to pressure Taiwan without inviting an American response. Yet because the United States has a greater interest in Taiwan today than at any point in history, Beijing’s bet could turn out to be wrong. China could announce a maritime quarantine of Taiwan or another coercive measure only to have the United States intervene, potentially prompting a great-power standoff.

Avoiding such a showdown will require American leaders to recommit to deterrence. First, Washington needs to update the way it communicates its positions. In their public remarks, policymakers should clearly state that the U.S. economy’s dependence on Taiwanese exports makes peace and stability across the Taiwan Strait a vital interest of the United States. American political leaders should emphasize to Beijing that the United States has an obligation to prevent a crisis that would tank the U.S. stock market, cut critical supply chains, and fell many American industries.

Clarifying U.S. interests would not impugn 50 years of careful diplomacy with China, nor would it require any change to U.S. policy as established by the Taiwan Relations Act, the Six Assurances, or the series of joint U.S.-Chinese statements, made between 1972 and 1982, known as the Three Communiqués. The updated statements, rather, would convey to Chinese leaders that certain changes to the cross-strait status quo would be unacceptable to the United States, even if Taiwan were to acquiesce to those changes under pressure. Washington would support a peaceful unification, for example, only if the process guaranteed the security of critical supply chains.

Once the United States makes its interests clear, it will also have to back up its statements with a new playbook to deter Chinese actions that fall below the military threshold. Beijing may already be in the early stages of imposing a quarantine on the island. Washington should not tolerate additional measures that interfere with commerce and maritime traffic in and out of Taiwan. To deter China from taking such steps, the next U.S. Congress should use legislation to clarify its wholesale, bipartisan opposition to Chinese blockades, quarantines, and other restrictions. Even if the White House continued to take a softer position, this would send a message to Beijing that other political actors might force the president’s hand. Congress should also press the administration to prepare punitive diplomatic and financial measures for use in the event of an escalating Chinese quarantine of Taiwan. By planning these reprisals well ahead of any coercive action, Washington would signal that a maritime pressure campaign would entail clear costs for Beijing and carry dangerous escalation risks.

The United States should strengthen military deterrence, too. The next Congress must pressure the Trump administration to release the stalled $14 billion package of arms sales to Taiwan, as well as other security assistance the administration is slow-rolling. Congress has a legal obligation to do so under the Taiwan Relations Act. Pausing security aid violates U.S. policy, and allowing the Trump team to make this concession in its likely quixotic pursuit of rapprochement with Xi only weakens Washington’s hand.

Washington does not have to—and shouldn’t—take these steps alone. U.S. allies such as Germany, Japan, and South Korea would suffer nearly as much as the United States if China were to take control of Taiwan’s semiconductor industry. These countries have an interest in working quietly together to deter and develop joint responses to Chinese coercion while also collaborating to diversify their chip supply chains and make them more resilient. In the United States, this work does not have to wait for a new presidential administration. Congress can send delegations and use other diplomatic opportunities to start developing plans with parliamentary counterparts abroad.

As the 2028 U.S. presidential election approaches, moreover, candidates from both parties should speak clearly about the country’s strong economic interests in Taiwan and about their commitment to deterrence. This does not require embracing bellicose positions, but presidential candidates do need to state firmly that any move by Beijing to choke off vital supply chains would harm U.S. interests and demand an American response. Consistent messaging would also signal to China’s leaders that they should not expect a future administration to be bound by Trump’s policy concessions—and signal to allies that come 2029, the United States will again be a reliable partner in standing up to China’s economic aggression.

The United States needs to update its Taiwan policy, but ceding ground as Trump has done only makes the cross-strait balance less stable. Unlike other Cold War–era policies, the importance of preventing Chinese aggression against Taiwan has become more—not less—vital with the passage of time. U.S. interests in Taiwan are plainer and easier to explain than they have ever been. Only by stating those interests clearly and backing up the words with actions can the United States continue to avert a crisis or conflict that would be devastating for all.
