#5956. Hegseth labels impeachment push a policy dispute, but do lawmakers' allegations rise to high crimes?

Fox News’ Jonathan Turley and Kerri Urbahn assess articles of impeachment filed in the House against War Secretary Pete Hegseth. Jennifer Griffin reports the latest, with additional analysis from former Secretary of State Mike Pompeo.

War Secretary Pete Hegseth pushed back against an impeachment effort Tuesday, accusing lawmakers of trying to turn disagreements over President Donald Trump's military policies into impeachable offenses.

Congress is preparing to vote on eight articles alleging the Pentagon chief violated federal law and abused his authority.

"One disgruntled congressman wants to shred it over policy disagreements. That's what it is: policy disagreements, not high crimes, not misdemeanors, not treason, not bribery, not the kinds of offenses the founders intended when they wrote the impeachment clause," Hegseth said at a Pentagon press conference Tuesday morning.

"The proper remedy for a policy disagreement, it's legislation, it's debate, it's the next presidential election," he added.

But the impeachment resolution introduced Monday by Rep. Thomas Massie, R-Ky., goes beyond objections to the administration's military strategy. It alleges that Hegseth violated congressional war powers, authorized unlawful military operations and retaliated against a sitting senator for exercising his constitutional rights.

HEGSETH RIPS IMPEACHMENT PUSH FROM OUTGOING REPUBLICAN AS 'FARCE,' 'STUNT'

ARLINGTON, VIRGINIA - NOVEMBER 10: U.S. Secretary of War Pete Hegseth takes questions from reporters during a news conference at the Pentagon on November 10, 2026 in Arlington, Virginia. U.S. Rep. Thomas Massie (R-TX) introduced articles of impeachment against Hegseth on Monday, saying he violated the Constitution by waging war in Iran without congressional authorization and other charges. (Photo by Chip Somodevilla/Getty Images)

The clash puts the limits of impeachment at the center of a growing fight over Trump's national security agenda, testing whether lawmakers can make a constitutional case for removing Hegseth or are just using impeachment to challenge military and policy decisions they oppose.

Massie's eight articles center on several of the Trump administration's most controversial national security decisions, with three accusing Hegseth of violating the War Powers Resolution during the war against Iran.

The outgoing Kentucky Republican alleges that Hegseth initiated hostilities without congressional authorization, continued military operations after Congress directed their termination and disregarded statutory deadlines requiring the withdrawal of U.S. forces.

Other articles accuse Hegseth of failing to comply with laws intended to minimize civilian casualties, including in connection with a strike on an Iranian school, and conducting unlawful maritime strikes against suspected drug traffickers in the Caribbean.

7 REPUBLICANS REBEL AGAINST TRUMP, VOTE WITH DEMS TO REIN IN IRAN WAR POWERS

The resolution also alleges that Hegseth retaliated against Sen. Mark Kelly, D-Ariz., over a video urging service members to refuse unlawful orders, and unlawfully directed military operations in Yemen and the capture of former Venezuelan leader Nicolás Maduro.

Rep. Thomas Massie, R-Ky., speaks on impeaching U.S. Secretary of War Pete Hegseth outside the U.S. Capitol on Nov. 9, 2026 in Washington, D.C. (Andrew Harnik/Getty Images)

Massie has rejected the suggestion that the impeachment effort is simply a referendum on Hegseth's job performance or the administration's military policies.

"The question is whether he committed impeachable acts," Massie wrote on X Monday. "And the answer is yes, eight times."

Constitutional law scholar Jonathan Turley largely agreed that the impeachment effort was on shaky ground, arguing that six of Massie's eight articles should be dismissed outright.

"In my view, six of the eight articles should be nonstarters, including the Iranian articles," Turley wrote on X Tuesday.

Turley acknowledged that Congress has a legitimate constitutional interest in asserting its war powers, but argued that Massie was attempting to use impeachment to settle a longstanding dispute over presidential authority. Presidents of both parties, including Bill Clinton, Barack Obama and Joe Biden, have claimed broad authority to launch military operations without congressional approval, he noted.

"Massie is not going to 'litigate' the matter through impeachments," Turley wrote, arguing that Trump was exercising authority long claimed by his predecessors.

GOP REP THOMAS MASSIE INTRODUCES 8 ARTICLES OF IMPEACHMENT AGAINST WAR SECRETARY PETE HEGSETH

Secretary of Defense Pete Hegseth testifies before the Senate Armed Services Committee, on Capitol Hill, in Washington, Thursday, April 30, 2026. (Cliff Owen/AP Photo)

Vice President JD Vance, however, argued Tuesday that lawmakers were using impeachment as a substitute for exercising their legislative powers.

"Has Pete Hegseth committed impeachable crimes? The question is absurd and the answer is: obviously not," Vance wrote on X.

"Congress has so many tools for registering its policy disagreements. Rather than use them, they're going to try to impeach Pete Hegseth," he continued.

Vance suggested the effort was driven less by the war with Iran than by opposition to Hegseth's broader changes at the Pentagon, including shifting U.S. forces away from Europe and Ukraine and reorganizing the military's senior leadership.

TRUMP’S PENTAGON SHAKE-UP REVIVES A PRESIDENTIAL POWER PLAY WITH A LONG HISTORY

ARLINGTON, VIRGINIA - NOVEMBER 10: U.S. Secretary of War Pete Hegseth speaks during a news conference at the Pentagon on November 10, 2026 in Arlington, Virginia. U.S. Rep. Thomas Massie (R-TX) introduced articles of impeachment against Hegseth on Monday, saying he violated the Constitution by waging war in Iran without congressional authorization and other charges. (Photo by Chip Somodevilla/Getty Images)

"This isn't about Iran, this is about the fact that Pete took on the bureaucracy at the Pentagon," Vance wrote.

DRAMATIC FOOTAGE SHOWS ALLEGED NARCO BOAT ERUPT IN FIREBALL DURING US MILITARY STRIKE

Turley dismissed the article of impeachment concerning Maduro's capture, pointing to court precedent involving the U.S. seizure of former Panamanian leader Manuel Noriega.

But Turley drew a distinction between those disputes over executive authority and two allegations he considered more serious: the maritime strikes against suspected drug traffickers and Hegseth's treatment of Sen. Mark Kelly.

"The 'double tap' allegation would be a war crime, if true," Turley wrote, noting that Trump and Hegseth have denied issuing such orders.

The effort to punish Kelly over a video urging troops to refuse unlawful orders also raised serious questions about a sitting senator's free speech rights and potential undue command influence, Turley argued.

Still, he stopped short of concluding that either allegation met the constitutional threshold for impeachment, saying more evidence was needed.

DEMOCRATS RALLY BEHIND GOP LAWMAKER'S HEGSETH IMPEACHMENT PUSH, SIGNAL MORE TO COME IN JANUARY

"I will be looking for the underlying evidence on those two articles," he wrote, describing both as potentially problematic but difficult to establish as "high crimes and misdemeanors."

The Constitution authorizes impeachment for treason, bribery or other high crimes and misdemeanors, a standard that does not require proof of a criminal offense. Congress has historically treated serious abuses of official power as potential grounds for impeachment, though the precise boundaries remain contested.

That distinction complicates the argument that the allegations can be dismissed simply because they involve military policy. Lawmakers can disagree with a defense secretary's decisions without those disagreements constituting impeachable misconduct, but allegations that an official knowingly violated the law or abused the powers of his office present a different constitutional question.

Hegseth, meanwhile, has maintained that the operations cited in the resolution were lawful and directed by the president.

"I stand behind every one of these operations. I was proud to execute them, and I would do it again tomorrow," Hegseth said Tuesday.

U.S. Secretary of War Pete Hegseth gives a State of the Force address at Marine Corps Base Quantico in Quantico, Va., on Sept. 30, 2026. Secretary Hegseth delivered his address to junior officers, enlisted leaders, and special guests including Anduril Industries co-founder Palmer Luckey. (Getty Images)

President Trump also rallied behind his defense secretary following the press conference, praising his performance and attacking Massie, who lost his Republican primary earlier this year.

"In just under two years, Secretary Hegseth has worked side-by-side with President Trump to restore American power on the world stage, build the strongest military in generations, and instill a warrior ethos at the Pentagon. President Trump and Secretary Hegseth have taken bold, necessary action to deny the Iranian regime the ability to develop a nuclear weapon, bring narco-terrorist Nicolás Maduro to American justice, and defend our homeland from dangerous terrorists and cartels – saving American lives," White House spokesperson Olivia Wales told Fox News Digital.

"This spectacle only proves what we’ve known all along: Thomas Massie and the Democrats are far more interested in playing political games than working to improve the lives of the American people," Wales added.

Trump Cabinet officials rallied behind Hegseth, with Collins, Burgum, Rollins, Mullin and Turner posting messages of support on social media. Rubio and Blanche also defended Hegseth publicly.

Rubio said he carried out Trump’s orders "quite effectively" and Blanche rejected claims that he had done anything illegal or wrong.

The House is expected to vote Tuesday evening on whether to advance Massie's impeachment resolution. Impeachment would require a simple majority in the House, but removing Hegseth from office would require conviction by a two-thirds majority in the Senate.

The vote will test whether lawmakers view the allegations as grounds for impeachment or a dispute better addressed through Congress's legislative and oversight powers.

#5950. The New Epicenter of Global Terror

Since its founding in 1922, Foreign Affairs has been the leading forum for serious discussion of American foreign policy and global affairs. The magazine has featured contributions from many leading international affairs experts.

EGHOSA E. OSAGHAE is Director-General of the Nigerian Institute of International Affairs.

How Africa’s Weak States and Rare Earths Drive Transnational Extremism

Twenty-five years after 9/11, much of the world has moved on from the war on terrorism. Following the defeat of the Islamic State in Iraq and Syria and the U.S. withdrawal from Afghanistan in 2021, Washington has turned to other major concerns; along with many of its European allies, the United States has been more preoccupied with the growing confrontation with China, and with the wars in Ukraine and the Middle East, than with the threat of transnational extremism. Buttressing this shift has been a general decline in terrorist attacks in many parts of the world since ISIS’s peak, in the mid-2010s.

Yet there is one region of the world where terrorism not only persists but also appears to be growing. According to reports by the United Nations and the Global Terrorism Index, Africa is the overwhelming center of terrorist activity today. The African continent accounts for more than 50 percent of all global terrorism-related deaths over the past five years. It also hosts three of the four deadliest jihadi groups in the world: ISIS, which is now primarily based in Africa, Jamaat Nusrat al-Islam wal-Muslimin (JNIM), and al-Shabab. (The fourth is Tehrik-e-Taliban Pakistan, also known as the Pakistani Taliban.) Six of the ten countries that are most affected by terrorism are also in Africa: Burkina Faso, the Democratic Republic of the Congo, Mali, Niger, Nigeria, and Somalia. And whereas the Middle East has had a 93 percent decline in terrorist attacks between 2017 and 2025, the Sahel has experienced a 17 percent growth over the same period, with a record 4,043 terrorism deaths in 2023 alone.

To the extent that African terrorism has attracted global concern, much of the attention has focused on the Sahel region. The broad swath of land separating the Sahara and the Maghreb in the north from sub-Saharan Africa is a hotbed of extremism, hosting JNIM and ISIS affiliates such as Boko Haram in Nigeria, Cameroon, and Chad, as well as bandit groups such as Lakurawa in Niger and Nigeria and al-Shabab in Somalia. But terrorist activity in Africa now extends to a host of other countries in western, central, and southern Africa as well, including Benin, the Central African Republic, Mozambique, and Togo. Many of these countries have little previous history of jihadi ideology or extremist violence.

World leaders overlook this continent-wide terrorist threat at their own peril. Not only have terrorist groups become a major challenge to African stability and development; they also pose a growing danger to global security. For now, these groups may appear to have little capacity to project their power beyond Africa’s borders. Yet amid the new scramble for Africa’s mineral resources—and for influence on the continent—this may change. Global powers, especially China and Russia, have been increasing their efforts to gain economic, political, and military leverage in Africa, which has in turn undermined collective international initiatives to fight terrorism. And many African jihadi groups themselves have strong anti-Western sentiments and have been using the resource wars to help build transnational networks.

Thus far, the global response to the African threat has largely focused on military operations, especially in the Sahel. But to truly address the phenomenon, outside powers and world leaders will need to understand the underlying forces that have led to this proliferation of extremist groups. Above all, they will need to find new ways to help African countries address the erosion of state institutions and governance that has made terrorism endemic across the continent.

Africa’s entanglement with terrorist and jihadi groups is hardly new. Al-Qaeda’s 1998 attacks on U.S. embassies in Dar-es-Salaam and Nairobi were planned from Sudan, where Osama bin Laden had been based between 1991 and 1996. After 9/11, security experts viewed those attacks as a crucial precursor that should have set off alarm bells. Then, in the early years of the twenty-first century, local African groups coalesced into al-Shabab, based in Somalia, which also began to pursue transnational terrorism as an affiliate of al-Qaeda. Later, al-Qaeda in the Islamic Maghreb formed a major foothold in Mali. These developments prompted a number of Western-led antiterrorism campaigns, often supported by African forces. Most notable were the military interventions in the Sahel led by the French and other Western special forces and the initial U.S.-supported intervention force in Somalia, the so-called Somali Warlord Alliance.

But these counterterrorism operations did little to stop extremist activity in Africa. In the 2010s, countries in and close to the Sahel began to transition from being breeding grounds of terrorism to becoming the focus of terrorist attacks. Over the past few years, these countries have not only continued to be the places where terrorist groups can organize, radicalize their adherents, and spread violent extremist ideology; they also now bear the brunt of terrorist attacks and killings and the instability and breakdown in security that go with them. Worse, this activity has spread to new areas of the continent, such as Benin, the Democratic Republic of the Congo, and Mozambique, where there had not been jihadis previously. In these countries, terrorist groups have capitalized on long-standing popular grievances spurred by government neglect, exclusion, and poverty. The jihadi expansion has been led by new local groups with connections to al-Qaeda and its branch JNIM in the Sahel region of West Africa and to ISIS affiliates in central Africa, whose major motivations include generating wealth from illegal mining, smuggling, and transnational organized crime.

What the outside world has often failed to recognize—both in the earlier and more recent phases of extremism—are the underlying factors that have made so many African countries prone to this activity. In nearly every case, fragile or collapsing state institutions have been crucial enablers: states’ inability to ensure the welfare and security of their citizens, to exercise sovereign authority over the length and breadth of their territory, and to alleviate the widespread poverty, unemployment, and disease that have created openings for extremism. In many countries, these factors have been exacerbated by large numbers of internally displaced persons and refugees, a lack of social infrastructure, and perennial violent conflict.

The unique characteristics of African demography have added to the challenge. In contrast to the aging populations of most other regions of the world, people under the age of 30 make up nearly 70 percent of Africa’s population and are also most affected by state failure. Large numbers of young people are not in school—more than 270 million African children in 2026, accounting for more than 40 percent of the global total. Nigeria alone has nearly 20 million youth out of school, the highest of any country in the world. At the same time, youth unemployment has remained persistently high: the continental average is 18 percent, but in some countries, such as Djibouti, where mass employment opportunities are limited and young people generally lack the requisite education for skilled employment in the logistics and transport sector that dominates the economy, youth unemployment may reach more than 75 percent. Disaffected young Africans constitute a huge reservoir of potential recruits for extremist groups, which have grown commensurately: according to recent estimates, al-Shabab has more than 15,000 fighters and JNIM has around 6,000.

But state complicity also plays a role. It is now well documented that political leaders have been building ties to bandit and terrorism groups to secure and consolidate their power. For example, the emergence of Boko Haram and various other militant outfits in Nigeria has been abetted by state governors and influential politicians employing them to intimidate opponents during elections. It is worth noting, as well, that elites in Muslim-dominated northern Nigeria initially opposed the U.S. designation of Boko Haram as a terrorist group—based on the assumption that the group was defending the interests of mainstream Muslims and northerners. This kind of support helped legitimize the group and enabled it to extend its control beyond the northeast of the country. The adoption of sharia law in the early years of the twenty-first century by 12 predominantly Muslim states in northern Nigeria also helped incubate Boko Haram. In this sense, Nigeria followed the path of Sudan, whose long history of Islamic fundamentalism and practice of sharia law made it a safe haven for bin Laden as he began to build his ideology of global terrorism.

Yet another contributing factor has been climate change and natural resource wars. Many of these countries have difficult desert geographies and have been beset with growing food insecurity and conflicts over diminishing resources. The result has been the emergence of so-called ungoverned territories within a number of African countries—areas characterized by power vacuums, porous borders, and illicit mining of critical minerals and other natural resources by terrorist groups, bandits, and criminal gangs. Often in these places, parallel state structures have been established by nonstate actors—not only to provide public goods, including safety, protection, and social services but also to challenge state legitimacy through sectarian uprisings and war. As these extremist nonstate groups seek to root their ideologies in ethnic, religious, and communal identities, territorial control has become central to their goals. This is especially true of jihadi groups such as al-Shabab, Boko Haram, JNIM, and ISIS affiliates, which aim to create caliphates, although none has thus far demonstrated the capacity to take over an entire state.

Although the domestic sources of African terrorism are important, external factors and global interconnections have been even more crucial to its spread. Take the continent’s jihadi and radical Islamist movements, which have for decades been inspired and supported from outside. For example, the 1979 Iranian Revolution spurred the rise of Shiite movements among Muslim youths across Africa. The Iranian government actively supported the movements by sponsoring the establishment of schools and mosques, offering scholarships for students to study in Iran, and creating training and indoctrination programs. Today, Ibrahim al-Zakzaky’s radical Islamic Movement of Nigeria, whose goal is to overthrow the secular government and establish Islamic law, remains one of the more resilient Iranian affiliates in Africa and has training camps in northwestern Nigeria that are linked to Iran and Lebanon’s Hezbollah.

In the 1990s, the exploits of the Taliban in Afghanistan inspired young Muslim radicals in northern Nigeria, where the forerunners of Boko Haram initially called themselves the Taliban. In turn, terrorism researchers have identified Qatar, Saudi Arabia, Turkey, and other Middle Eastern powers as funders of Salafi extremism, which espouses the violent imposition of Islamic law on everyone, including fellow Muslims. Such is the success of these efforts that many African terrorist groups today consider themselves part of the global network of Salafist jihadism.

Among the most important external forces driving African terrorism are porous borders and the free flows of weapons, drugs, money, and human trafficking into and out of the continent. These flows have facilitated the integration of African groups into transnational terror networks. Al-Shabab was one of the earliest examples of this phenomenon: the group was founded by Somali supporters of the Afghan mujahideen in the late 1980s. Later, it became an affiliate of al-Qaeda, pledging allegiance to the group in 2012.

Other African groups followed a similar trajectory. Al-Qaeda’s African affiliates have grown to include Ansaru, a splinter faction of Boko Haram operating in northern Nigeria, as well as al-Qaeda in the Islamic Maghreb, which is based in Algeria. ISIS has added a panoply of African groups to its own network, such as Islamic State Sahel Province in Burkina Faso, Mali, and Niger, and Islamic State West Africa Province, which is active in the Lake Chad Basin. And it has developed ties with Islamic State Central Africa Province, which also operates in parts of Mozambique and the Democratic Republic of the Congo and Uganda; and Islamic State in Somalia, which is a rival of al-Shabab. Meanwhile, al-Shabab has cultivated links to the Houthis in Yemen, a development that has heightened the potential for transnational terrorism to become enmeshed in the struggle for control of the Red Sea and the Gulf of Aden between Egypt, Ethiopia, Saudi Arabia, and Turkey following conflicts and closures in the Strait of Hormuz.

These transnational affiliations have offered African groups many advantages, bringing new sources of funding, new weapons (including drones), and training in suicide bombing and IEDs, as well as indoctrination, foreign fighters, intelligence, and logistics. In return, African terrorist groups have helped their international affiliates by engaging in sea piracy and smuggling, human trafficking and migrant smuggling, money laundering, kidnapping for ransom, robbery, livestock rustling, drug trafficking, and illegal mining. African groups use many of these activities, as well as illegally taxing the areas under their control, to raise illicit funds for their international affiliates.

In addition, African jihadis provide personnel for international operations—al-Shabab members have been involved in terrorist training operations in the Philippines—and safe havens for fugitive terrorists. During the decades of the post-9/11 war on terrorism, there was a steady influx to Africa of foreign terrorists, fighters, and instructors who were fleeing Iran, Iraq, Syria and other parts of the Middle East. Others came from North Africa, especially following the civil war in Libya and unrest in Algeria. These foreign extremists brought with them massive amounts of weapons and helped turn Africa into a center of terrorist activity. As the epicenter of global terrorism today, Africa now houses numerous al-Qaeda and ISIS operatives, a fact that increases the risk that Africa-based groups may be planning operations beyond the continent.

In theory, international engagement in African security should help contain the rising terrorism threat. But the record thus far is not promising. During the war on terrorism, the United States, the EU, the United Nations, and international security organizations provided support for numerous counterterrorism campaigns on the continent. These efforts included backing the G5 Sahel—a counterterrorism initiative by Burkina Faso, Chad, Mali, Mauritania, and Niger—as well as support for regional counterterrorism initiatives, including in Mozambique and Somalia, as well as the Multinational Joint Task Force, which aimed to combat terrorism in the Lake Chad region. Most of these efforts failed or were not sustained long enough to change the trajectory, and the worsening security situations that they left in their wake fueled anti-Western sentiment. And now, with Russia and China pursuing interests of their own in many parts of Africa, involvement by outside powers is often driven by a desire for strategic minerals and a quest for geopolitical influence, interventions that have only further encouraged terrorist activity.

In the Sahel, the withdrawal of U.S. and French forces between 2022 and early 2025 created power and security vacuums. Russia has quickly exploited the situation, using bilateral defense pacts and mercenary and Africa Corps forces to gain access to minerals and natural resources that France had previously controlled. Indeed, in many parts of Africa, global competition for critical minerals has become a new driver in the spread of terrorism. In the Manono district of Congo, for instance, American, Australian, and Chinese firms are enmeshed in long-running disputes over lithium deposits, which has made counterterrorism operations in the same area more difficult: the various international players no longer want to coordinate on counterterrorism and terrorist groups are using external rivalries to strengthen their own operations. A similar scenario is playing out in other resource-rich areas.

It is now urgent that leading world powers adopt a different approach. A first step is to recognize the problem for what it is. Jihadi terrorism today is not a local or regional problem afflicting a handful of African countries; it is a worldwide threat. It can be effectively tackled only through concerted and sustained international action. Although internationally-backed counterterrorism efforts by the African Union and other regional forces are more needed than ever, the new scramble for resources has become an obstacle to international collaboration in Africa. In this regard, it will be crucial to embrace the common protocols and regulatory frameworks for critical minerals and other resources being developed by the AU and other regional African organizations—which seek to reduce the scope for illicit activities and ensure that that the countries benefit more from the resources through more inclusive and accountable governance structures.

Most important, outside powers need to engage with the root causes that have made the continent so prone to terrorist activity, even when it has been effectively countered elsewhere in the world. As the former UN Secretary-General Ban Ki-moon said in 2015, “Good governance is what will kill terrorism.” Northern Ireland after the Good Friday Agreement—whose institutional reforms such as power sharing, human rights protections, and community policing proved to be more effective than military operations—is a case in point; so is the 2016 peace agreement between the Colombian government and the FARC, which included governance reforms in previously neglected parts of the country as part of its stabilization measures.

The United States as well as governments in Europe and international bodies need to shift their focus from military operations to addressing the actual social and economic sources of state fragility that have made so many African countries fertile ground for conflict and extremist violence. This would of course involve strengthening state capacities, and it would require regional efforts as well as more targeted development assistance. None of it will be easy, but doing nothing would be far worse. Unless African countries and their international partners can begin to address the underlying dynamics that are driving Africa’s transnational terrorism problem, it is unlikely to stay in Africa for much longer.

#5949. House GOP leaders block Massie's Hegseth impeachment push

Hewitt argues that Massie's impeachment effort is politically motivated and intended to attract attention as he faces uncertainty over his political future, questioning the congressman's intentions and the timing of his actions.

Secretary of War Pete Hegseth is safe from an impeachment vote in the House of Representatives after GOP leaders successfully stopped a looming vote led by Rep. Thomas Massie, R-Ky.

A vote to refer Massie's resolution to the House Judiciary Committee for consideration — which effectively killed it in its tracks — passed on Tuesday evening by a 211-204 margin, averting a potential impeachment vote on Wednesday.

President Donald Trump celebrated the failed impeachment effort in a Truth Social post, calling Massie a "major LOSER" and praising Hegseth's record on military operations in Venezuela and Iran and recruitment.

HEGSETH IMPEACHMENT VOTE LOOMS AS HOUSE REPUBLICANS PRIVATELY WARN IT COULD PASS

U.S. Secretary of War Pete Hegseth speaks during a news conference at the Pentagon on Nov. 10, 2026 in Arlington, Virginia. (Chip Somodevilla/Getty Images)

"The Democrats’ attempt to impeach Secretary of War Pete Hegseth has miserably FAILED!" Trump wrote, calling the outcome the "EXONERATION of a Great American Patriot."

The GOP had been worried about having a significant number of absences on their side after their brutal midterm election performance last week, but it appears Speaker Mike Johnson, R-La., wrangled one more victory on his way out the leadership door.

Massie's resolution is a wide-ranging piece of legislation accusing Hegseth of misusing his office via eight impeachment articles, spanning issues including the Iran war, U.S. strikes on suspected drug boats in the Caribbean and his retaliation against Sen. Mark Kelly, D-Ariz., over a video calling on troops to disobey "unlawful orders."

Hegseth railed against the effort and criticized Massie personally during a news conference Tuesday morning.

HEGSETH LABELS IMPEACHMENT PUSH A POLICY DISPUTE, BUT DO LAWMAKERS' ALLEGATIONS RISE TO HIGH CRIMES?

"What one disgruntled and defeated congressman is attempting is not oversight, and it's not accountability. It's a political spectacle, a personal manifesto driven by ego and resentment," Hegseth said.

The outgoing Kentucky Republican was able to force Tuesday night's vote over the objections of House GOP leaders by filing a privileged resolution, a mechanism aimed at bypassing the will of the chamber's majority. A privileged resolution must be considered within two legislative days of being filed.

Rep. Thomas Massie, R-Ky., speaks on impeaching U.S. Secretary of War Pete Hegseth outside the U.S. Capitol on Nov. 9, 2026 in Washington, D.C. (Andrew Harnik/Getty Images)

"I don't know if any Republicans other than Massie will be in support of his articles of impeachment, but I don't even know how many Republicans will be here by the time of the vote. The real question is how short are we. Numerically, if they're all here and we're not, what happens then?"

#5947. The Luxury of Children

Since its founding in 1922, Foreign Affairs has been the leading forum for serious discussion of American foreign policy and global affairs. The magazine has featured contributions from many leading international affairs experts.

JESÚS FERNÁNDEZ-VILLAVERDE is Howard Marks Presidential Professor of Economics at the University of Pennsylvania. This piece is adapted from “Terra Incognita: The Economics of a Shrinking World,” co-authored with Patrick Norrick and forthcoming in the 2027 Annual Review of Economics.

Why the Baby Bust Is Making the World More Unequal

The world’s fertility crisis is more severe than is commonly understood. Last year, humanity’s total fertility level dropped below what demographers call the replacement rate—that is, the average number of children that a woman must have to keep a population constant over the long run. This has never happened before. There have been periods when the human population declined because of wars or epidemics, such as the Black Death, in the fourteenth century, or the diseases brought by Europeans to the Americas in the fifteenth and sixteenth centuries. But now, simply as a function of their choices, humans will see the world population peak around 2055 and then begin to fall.

The story of the coming decades is not simply one of dwindling and aging. There is a great deal of variation in the baby bust. The most surprising trend may be one that has overturned basic assumptions about fertility rates. Where once it was thought that people—and, by extension, countries—with higher levels of income and education would have lower fertility rates than those who were poorer and less educated, the inverse is increasingly true. Many wealthier people and countries boast higher fertility rates than many poor people and countries.

The inversion is revealing of why governments have had a hard time coming to grips with the fertility crash. The best explanation for why people are no longer having babies is that child-rearing at the scale necessary to maintain populations is fundamentally at odds with the structures of modern life and modern societies. Once an economic necessity, children are now an economic luxury. That portends a world in which economic inequality is accentuated by demographic inequality, with widening fissures within countries and among them. The whole world is grappling with a decline in fertility, but in many different ways. And those differences will stoke further divisions, tensions, and even conflict.

Humanity falling below replacement has passed largely unnoticed. The central projections in the latest release of the United Nations World Population Prospects (in 2024), known as the report’s “medium variant,” still places the world’s total fertility rate slightly above replacement in 2026 and expects the global population to peak as late as 2084. But this medium variant does not fully incorporate the recent drops in births recorded by national statistical agencies, and its projections for future fertility rest on questionable assumptions, including forecasting a strong recovery of fertility rates in countries such as China. By comparison, the report’s “low variant” (the forecast that takes a more pessimistic view of future fertility) accords nearly exactly with my own assessment, which reflects more recent national statistics and takes a less optimistic view on the possible recovery of fertility. In that reading, the world has dropped below replacement fertility levels (approximately 2.2 children per woman) and will only keep dropping.

Fertility rates are plunging so fast that they are overturning many assumptions about demography. The steepest fertility declines have occurred in unexpected countries. Consider this test. Pick the state with the highest fertility rate among the following: Argentina, Brazil, Chile, Colombia, Jamaica, Iran, Malaysia, Thailand, Tunisia, Turkey, Sri Lanka, and the United States.

The United States, the wealthiest of the 12, has the highest fertility rate. This holds true even when just assessing the fertility rate of mothers born in the United States (immigrant mothers have a slightly higher fertility rate than native mothers). Dozens of other middle-income countries now have fertility levels below that of the United States.

That trend runs against the old explanation for demographic change, which held that fertility invariably falls as a community or society gets richer. That is demonstrably no longer the case. Among the countries in the Organization for Economic Cooperation and Development (OECD), richer members tend to have higher fertility rates than do poorer ones, even after controlling for migration flows. Take Mexico. According to the most recent survey, in 2024, a Mexican woman could expect to have 1.2 children over her lifetime, well below the 1.57 average of non-Hispanic white women in the United States. Mexico’s income per capita is only about 28 percent of the U.S. level. And just two decades ago, fertility rates in Mexico were much higher than those in the United States.

This dynamic obtains not just among countries but also within them. Richer and more educated households in the United States and other advanced economies now tend to have more children than poorer and less educated ones, reversing a pattern that had held for centuries.

There is no single, simple answer for why this has happened. Most theories do not hold up against international evidence. Factors that seem to contribute to lower fertility in the United States, for instance, such as higher housing prices, don’t seem to matter elsewhere, as many countries have seen even steeper fertility declines without skyrocketing housing prices.

Or take another favorite culprit, smartphones. Some researchers have suggested that the spread of the smartphone stoked atomization and a desire for individual fulfillment, discouraging many people from having children. But fertility rates have been plunging worldwide since the late 1960s, well before smartphones appeared, and there is little evidence of an acceleration in the drop after their introduction. Instead, the device is more likely an accelerator of underlying mechanisms than a cause in itself: by my calculations, smartphones compressed into ten years a change in fertility that would have occurred anyway over 25.

Another common explanation for drops in fertility is the spread of female education and employment. Freed from the domestic function of birthing and raising babies, the argument goes, women have chosen to prioritize other goals instead of child rearing. Although that shift may indeed have played a role in the twentieth century in lowering fertility rates, it is no longer so clearly causal today. Indeed, across OECD countries, the correlation between fertility, on the one hand, and female education and employment, on the other, is positive: the countries where women are more educated and more likely to work outside the home are also the countries with higher fertility rates. And in other regions for which demographers have good vital statistics, such as Latin America, the women not having children in 2026 are the least educated and the ones most likely to not be part of the workforce.

In the absence of neat, definitive explanations, a broader explanation for the global fertility crash comes into view. As societies embrace what is often termed “modernity,” they become incapable of maintaining replacement-level fertility rates. Modernity describes how countries have organized contemporary life around formal institutions (such as schools, universities, and health systems), specialized expertise (through degree accreditation, standardized exams, and professional licensing), and large-scale systems of coordination (such as multinational corporations, governments, and global supply chains). It is not synonymous with capitalism; communist countries, too, invested heavily in mass education and bureaucracies (and saw concomitant declines in fertility). The only way to run large, complex societies that harness modern technology at scale is to standardize knowledge and detach it from local custom through mass education, systems of expertise, and bureaucracies.

Modernity raises the cost of children and lowers the cost of childlessness. Parents must equip children with formal knowledge and credentials, an endeavor that takes decades of effort and spending. Tight career schedules also conflict with parents’ biological clocks. The most productive years for a research economist, for example, are usually between 30 and 45. Those are also the years when one needs to spend the most time with children. No matter how much accommodation a university offers, the day still has only 24 hours. Even when a researcher manages to produce both a large family and strong academic output, one can always wonder how much more that researcher would have produced with a smaller family.

And modernity has also made it easier not to have children. In traditional agrarian and artisan economies, children were useful factors of production, helping in the fields and workshops. Children also provided parents and other older relatives with eldercare. But the modern organization of economic life no longer requires children to perform those roles and the advent of welfare states has given the elderly other forms of support. Lineage once determined people’s present and future; it no longer does so in such a comprehensive way.

There are some exceptions to this seemingly universal trend. Tight-knit religious communities, such as the ultra-Orthodox in Israel and the Amish in the United States, have managed to maintain high fertility rates well above replacement level. But these groups have also stayed apart from much of modern life. For example, the Amish live in rural areas, where housing is much cheaper, and they end formal education at eighth grade, which eliminates one of the main costs of raising a child in a modern society, advanced education.

Apart from these idiosyncratic pockets, modernity has spread around the planet, with fertility falling everywhere. But rates are tumbling unevenly. In 1980, the richer an OECD country, the fewer children its women had. By 2000, the opposite was beginning to become true. The same reversal shows up inside countries. In Sweden, the demographer Martin Kolk has found that in every cohort born between 1940 and 1970, men with higher lifetime incomes have more children, and that among women born since the 1960s, the poorest have the fewest children. In the United States, the economists Moshe Hazan and Hosny Zoabi have found that in the first decade of the twenty-first century, women with a graduate degree had about 2.0 children, significantly more than the 1.8 for women who stopped at a bachelor’s degree.

Many adults still want to have children and flourishing families. When Gallup asked Americans about their ideal number of children in July 2025, the average answer was 2.7, a figure that has barely changed since the 1970s. But children are costly and bring no income. They have become something of a luxury good that richer people can afford more of than poorer people can. In the same way that a richer household owns, on average, a more expensive car than a poorer one, it also has more children—at least since the 1990s. Prosperous parents can hire help at home, order prepared meals, pay for premium grocery delivery or laundry services, and afford tuition and tutoring.

Richer families (and, among them, the most educated parents) also have more stable family lives. For instance, among Americans born between 1957 and 1964 (a cohort whose adult lives can be observed almost in full), about half of men with a high school diploma but no college education had seen their first marriage end in divorce by age 55, compared with 27 percent of men with a bachelor’s degree or more. Stable families lead to higher fertility (and much better outcomes for the children). In short, income and education soften some of the worst consequences of modernity. The intuition of this result applies, as well, to countries as a whole: richer countries can, on average, afford more “luxuries,” including children, than poorer countries.

If the modernity conjecture is right, the flip in the correlation between income and fertility that is evident within OECD countries will extend to all other countries over the next few decades. This is already happening in the Americas: among countries with at least 20 million inhabitants, the United States has the highest fertility rate in the hemisphere, well ahead of Argentina, Brazil, Canada, Colombia, and Mexico, and matched only by Peru, whose fertility rate is plummeting fast. (Venezuela’s official fertility rate is pure fiction.)

The same phenomenon will occur in sub-Saharan Africa over the next few decades. It is often projected that the continent’s high fertility rates will make up for shortfalls elsewhere—but that will likely not happen. Indeed, in those sub-Saharan African countries for which demographers have slightly better statistical data, fertility rates are dropping very quickly. For example, a recent household survey in the Republic of Congo has shown a decrease in fertility from 5.1 children per woman in 2011–12 to 3.5 in 2025. Similar falls are happening elsewhere and likely to accelerate as sub-Saharan Africa succumbs to the same factors that have sped fertility declines elsewhere.

The coming years will produce deep changes in populations within countries and across countries. The already sharp divide between regions and social groups with higher average incomes and education levels and those with lower average incomes and education levels will grow more pronounced, with the added dimension of contrasting fertility rates. The school district of a wealthy suburb outside a major East Coast city may not need to grapple with the widespread school closings that urban districts are already experiencing. And since political power in a democracy follows, at least to some extent, the number of voters, these different regions and social groups will see their relative influence in American politics change: New York, for instance, has lost 19 votes in the electoral college since the 1940s.

Something similar can happen among countries. In 1972, nearly eight times as many children were born in China as in the United States. In 2025, that figure has dropped to 2.2 times as many. China’s total fertility rate in 2025 was 0.93, while the U.S. rate was 1.58. If fertility rates remain at those levels, the United States will overtake China in annual births at some point in the 2080s and in total population by around 2120. That date might arrive earlier if the gap between the fertility rates of China and the United States continues to widen.

Demography is not necessarily destiny, but a world in which the United States has a larger population than China is very different from the world today. The shrinking and aging of populations will have first-order effects on a country’s economic performance. In research with Gustavo Ventura and Wen Yao, I have documented that nearly all of Japan’s poor economic performance relative to the United States since 1990 can be explained by its worse demographics, not by financial crises or poor economic policy. Among the great powers, the United States is the least exposed to demographic headwinds. Younger populations tend to be more innovative and less reliant on public support, a fact that bodes well for American prospects in the competition to come.

But for most other countries, demographic decline will produce significant problems. Aging Japan, for instance, is likely the best-case scenario for countries reaching a demographic sunset. It boasts a capable state and a homogeneous society with a high degree of consensus. More important, Japan became rich before it got old. Countries such as Mexico and Thailand, which lack many of Japan’s advantages, may struggle to weather the same demographic headwinds. While rich economies can rely on immigration to soften some of the effects of falling fertility, countries such as Tunisia or Bolivia, which lose many people to emigration, may find it harder to deal with their own baby busts. The new dynamics of fertility augur a world in which gaps between the haves and the have-nots grow even wider, as poorer countries and communities endure strains that wealthier countries and communities manage to escape.

Short of an unlikely turn to the social mores of deeply conservative and ideological communities, such as the Amish, only transformations in material circumstances will help raise fertility rates. Governments have had a hard time determining how to tackle fertility decline. Policy can do only so much in convincing people to make the monumental decision to have children. But that does not mean that governments should not try to reverse these trends. Their policies should respect the freedom of citizens to form the families they believe are best for them. Ultralow fertility seems to be caused not by a lack of desire for children but by the absence of the conditions that would make having children easier, or even possible.

In this vein, some policies are no-brainers. Governments should make education systems more accessible and less competitive than, for example, the ones found in East Asia and in the United States, where high levels of cost and stress discourage having children. They should build more affordable housing and provide more affordable childcare. Such material changes may have only a marginal impact on rates, but even, for instance, raising the U.S. total fertility rate to 1.7 from the current 1.58 would produce a significant impact. Fertility rates behave like the rates of return on a retirement fund: small yearly differences accumulate into large differences over time. Policies that give people greater material freedom to have children can thus be very consequential in the long term.

If governments fail to enact these policies, and if, indeed, these policies fail to encourage a broader rise in fertility, the world will simply become more unequal. Richer families and richer countries will gain greater weight in the population, while poorer families and poorer countries dwindle and weaken. These shifts will create political tensions, as shrinking populations will add to their relative poverty the desperate sense that they have no demographic future. Eastern Germany offers a preview. Since reunification, its young people (above all, its young women) have left for western Germany, its towns have emptied and aged, and its voters have turned to the parties at both extremes of the political spectrum. Many countries in the world are about to become eastern Germany, but few of them will have a western Germany to pay the bills. Demographers often suggest that the world is united by the crash in fertility rates. In truth, demographic decline will likely divide the world even further.

#5943. China’s AI Diplomacy Is Working

Since its founding in 1922, Foreign Affairs has been the leading forum for serious discussion of American foreign policy and global affairs. The magazine has featured contributions from many leading international affairs experts.

DANIEL REMLER is a Senior Fellow with the Technology and National Security Program at the Center for a New American Security. He served as AI Policy Coordinator in the Office of the Special Envoy for Critical and Emerging Technology at the State Department from 2023 to 2025.

Can the United States and China simultaneously cooperate and compete on AI? That is the question driving debates in Washington about the wisdom of pursuing talks on the subject with Beijing. The Biden administration paired sweeping technology restrictions with a narrow dialogue on risk, and the Trump administration has kept a channel open even as it has downplayed safety. Some experts go further. They hope that cooperation on safety can be siloed off from competition entirely, a belief as appealing as it is fanciful. Any dialogue on AI risk takes place within the broader strategic competition between the United States and China.

Beijing has long appreciated this dynamic. Much of Washington has not. Whereas the United States has shifted its approach to AI competition and safety across and within presidential administrations, Beijing has kept its AI diplomacy focused on gaining competitive advantage over the United States. China has taken part in international AI safety discussions, but it has used them, along with talks with the United States, to paint U.S. export controls as the main obstacle to cooperation. It has cast itself as the champion of the developing world, promising a Chinese-led AI ecosystem without technological or financial barriers as an alternative to a restrictive U.S.-led AI order. The pitch is working. A growing number of states are embracing China’s message, and Chinese open-weight models are gaining users across the developing world.

If the United States wants to maintain its lead and make meaningful progress on safety, it needs to treat the dialogue as part of the competition with China. To do so, it must ramp up pressure on China outside the talks and offer countries still choosing sides a more compelling vision for AI adoption and governance. And when U.S. and Chinese officials next meet, in November, Washington should test Beijing’s seriousness about safety. In short, the United States should approach AI diplomacy with the same strategic clarity China has.

The Biden administration’s first round of advanced semiconductor export controls in October 2022 and the release of ChatGPT a month later signaled that the United States had taken an early lead in the AI competition and would act to weaken China’s AI industry. Washington and its allies also seized the diplomatic initiative, taking the first steps toward global AI governance. In May 2023, the G-7 began intense discussions on AI governance that would eventually produce a code of conduct for developers. Weeks later, the United Kingdom announced it would host the first global AI safety summit at Bletchley Park.

China responded by rolling out a diplomatic script it has been honing ever since. Its core tenets first appeared in the Global AI Governance Initiative, published in October 2023. It called for countries to “put equal emphasis on development and security” (though it left security to be defined by individual states) and contrasted China’s focus on cooperation and development with U.S. export controls, attacking Washington for “creating barriers and disrupting the global AI supply chain through technological monopolies and unilateral coercive measures.” Despite Beijing’s growing body of AI regulations at home, the initiative offered little more than slogans on the technology’s risks.

That basic formula—cooperation and development, set against U.S. restrictions, with little to say about risk—has guided China’s AI diplomacy for the last three years. In March 2024, the UN General Assembly adopted a U.S.-led resolution on safe, secure, and trustworthy AI intended to appeal to both developed and developing countries. China supported the resolution, then introduced another resolution centered on help for poorer countries and “emphasizing that member states should enjoy equal opportunities” to develop and use AI. At a UN Security Council meeting on AI chaired by the United States in December that year, China’s UN ambassador, Fu Cong, once again counterposed Beijing’s stated interest in cooperation against “discriminatory barriers based on ideological differences.” In thinly veiled terms, Fu was charging that U.S. restrictions on China harmed the developing world.

To win the support of these countries as they built out their AI infrastructure, China needed a bloc of its own. In July 2025, Premier Li Qiang proposed the World Artificial Intelligence Cooperation Organization (WAICO), which promised developing countries technology, training, and a greater say in writing global AI rules. In a speech marking its launch a year later, Chinese leader Xi Jinping called for “openness,” shorthand for access to U.S. chips and the adoption of Chinese open-weight models. After urging countries to take the risks of AI seriously, Xi added that they should, “in the meantime,” jointly oppose “overstretching the national security concept in the field of AI and placing one country’s security over that of others.” Cooperation on risks, in Xi’s telling, was subordinate to overcoming U.S. restrictions.

WAICO has quickly gained standing among its target audience. An early endorsement by UN Secretary-General António Guterres lent it immediate credibility in the eyes of developing countries, and Chinese officials have since promoted WAICO in frequent speeches and in bilateral meetings with countries as varied as Brazil, Pakistan, and Serbia. Beijing has signaled that membership will come with perks. Its draft export controls on leading models, released in October, promise to help WAICO’s 37 members build their AI capabilities, suggesting members could have easier access to models restricted for the rest of the world.

Xi personally led this diplomatic offensive in the lead-up to the Washington summit with U.S. President Donald Trump in September. During a speech in Kyrgyzstan, he pledged to expand a Chinese AI center for the Shanghai Cooperation Organization that helps members build local language models and train engineers. In Cairo, he and Egyptian President Abdel Fattah el-Sisi signed agreements on AI, data centers, and semiconductors, which have become a staple of Xi’s trips abroad. And in New Delhi, Xi proposed an open-source AI zone for BRICS members and told other leaders that a “small yard, high fences” approach would only “box oneself in and divide the world.” AI risks were nowhere on the agenda. The message instead was that U.S. restrictions would isolate Washington, not Beijing.

Much of the allure of China’s pitch rests on how the country’s developers share their technology. Chinese developers release their best models with open weights, which any government or company can download, run on its own hardware, and adapt to its own language. For governments wary of depending on American companies whose exports Washington can restrict, open-weight models promise a greater degree of control. Chinese developers rarely conduct or publish the results of any safety testing for these models, leaving others to discover after release whether, for example, they can be used to launch cyberattacks or build weapons. But their accessibility allows Beijing to argue that any effort to contain the risks of Chinese models is an attempt to deny AI to the developing world.

China’s approach to bilateral AI talks with the United States has deflected discussions of risk just as assiduously as its global AI diplomacy has. In May 2024, the United States and China held talks in Geneva, which I joined as part of the U.S. delegation. The U.S. objective of the talks was to test whether an in-depth technical exchange with China on AI risk was possible. The answer was no. Chinese diplomats steered the discussion away from risks for hours and declined to even explain China’s own AI regulations beyond a superficial overview, despite having technical experts from the relevant agencies in the room.

The Chinese position came through most clearly in the opening and closing remarks of the delegation’s most senior officials. They began with an appeal for cooperation on capacity-building projects in the developing world. They then cast U.S. technology restrictions as an attempt to contain China’s development and the U.S. emphasis on safety as a barrier preventing developing countries from accessing and using AI. The implication was clear—export controls were the primary impediment to cooperation on AI safety. The one concrete outcome, a November 2024 statement in which former President Joe Biden and Xi affirmed “the need to maintain human control over the decision to use nuclear weapons,” asked little of Beijing.

This September, after the summit between Trump and Xi in Washington, China’s Ministry of Foreign Affairs published an “eight-point consensus,” Beijing’s account of what the two sides had agreed to. It described the dialogue as an exchange on the “risks and benefits” of AI. But whereas the White House framed the dialogue as a security issue, listing it alongside nuclear arms control, China’s Ministry of Commerce stressed instead that talks had taken place, and would continue, under the two countries’ economic and trade mechanism. Folded into trade talks, a dialogue on AI risks and benefits leaves Beijing free to parry U.S. requests on risks, such as limits on openly releasing models with dangerous capabilities, by demanding that Washington relax export controls.

Xi did propose to “jointly prevent the misuse and abuse of AI,” conveniently eliding China’s own frequent use of AI against the United States in various ways, such as hacking campaigns and influence operations. He added that AI should remain under human control and be developed for good, a principle Beijing has repeated since 2023 but about which it has offered little further detail or follow-through. More pointed was his insistence that future dialogue should “draw on each other’s strengths, not guard against each other” and his request that Chinese companies be “treated fairly,” both implicit rebukes of U.S. restrictions.

This record suggests Beijing will come to future meetings, beginning with the next discussion in November, with proposals designed to put Washington in a bind. Although U.S. Trade Representative Jamieson Greer has made clear that export controls are not on the table, Beijing will raise them anyway. If Washington engages, Chinese negotiators will claim a standing channel where U.S. restrictions on AI technology are up for negotiation. If it refuses, Beijing will continue to evade U.S. entreaties on safety while accusing Washington of stifling opportunity for developing countries. It hopes that every round of the dialogue will further cement an impression among allies such as Japan and the Netherlands that Washington will trade away restrictions over their heads, and that they should accommodate China’s AI ambitions, too.

But Washington is in a stronger position than its diplomacy has let on. The world’s leading models are still American, and, together with allies, the United States controls key chokepoints in the semiconductor supply chain. Export controls on those chokepoints are not a bargaining chip and should be strengthened. Chinese AI companies regularly lament their lack of high-end chips and have resorted to smuggling chips and illicitly distilling U.S. models. U.S. developers also conduct increasingly extensive and transparent safety testing, which Chinese developers avoid. Taken together, these strengths give Washington the means to press Beijing and an attractive offer for the rest of the world. It should use them, advertising its rigorously tested models to compete for global adoption while pressuring China on safety in the dialogue.

The first chance to apply that pressure is the next bilateral meeting. Washington should set two conditions for it and future meetings. Chinese developers must commit to publishing safety evaluations at least as extensive as those that leading U.S. developers publish today on equivalent benchmarks for the most serious risks, such as cyberattacks and biological weapons, and to disclosing previous and future incidents. The United States can make the same commitments, as its companies already publish such evaluations, increasingly disclose incidents, and open their models to independent and government evaluators before release. Otherwise, the incident channel to which the two sides agreed in September will run one way, with China free to point to U.S. incidents while reporting none of its own.

These conditions would change the terms of the dialogue. Since 2023, Washington has raised risks and Beijing has steered the conversation toward development. As long as Chinese companies face no scrutiny for the risks of their models, Beijing can tout them for their openness and innovation without having to answer questions from the rest of the world about their safety. Concordia AI, which tracks China’s AI ecosystem, found that only five of China’s ten leading developers published safety test results for any model over the past year, and none did so consistently. If China agrees, governments and companies choosing which models to use will be able to judge Chinese and American developers by the same criteria. If China refuses, the onus will be on Beijing to explain why fair treatment for Chinese companies should not extend to safety testing. Regardless, the U.S. government should ramp up its own tests of new Chinese models and publish the results. It should also avoid unforced errors, such as Trump’s waving away of AI safety concerns as a “hoax,” which hand Beijing an easy retort to U.S. appeals.

More broadly, effective AI diplomacy ultimately requires convincing other countries to join the American AI ecosystem. Countries that train their engineers, build their local language models, and run their data centers on Chinese technology will come to depend on it. To counter that dependence (and prevent it from turning into political alignment), Washington needs to present a positive vision of AI governance that pairs verifiable safety commitments—the kind Chinese developers avoid—with the access and investment developing countries want.

U.S. companies already offer the best technology, but Washington has yet to put real diplomatic weight behind them. It should draw from its evolving approach at home to rally partners around shared safety measures. Under the White House Accord on Super Intelligence, signed in September, six leading U.S. companies committed themselves to internal controls, external audits, and independent oversight. The G-7 Code of Conduct, which built on voluntary commitments secured under the Biden administration, could incorporate the accord’s measures and serve as a common standard for partners. Countries that agree to its measures could receive priority for technology and financing. Without such incentives, the AI competition may be determined by price and access alone, where China has the edge.

Much of Washington has treated talks with Beijing as a place to manage the shared risks of AI apart from the competition. China has always treated them as an arena of the larger rivalry. To maintain its lead and make real progress on safety, the United States should start doing the same.

Stephen E. Biegun and Emma Chanlett-Avery

#5938. Small-town Marine veteran refuses to back down in 16-year fight for America's forgotten heroes — and wins

Scott Stump, president and CEO of the National Desert Storm War Memorial Association, tells Fox News Digital about the yearslong effort to bring the memorial to the National Mall. (Credit: Nicholas Ballasy for Fox News Digital)

A Marine veteran from a small North Carolina town spent 16 years fighting to build a national memorial honoring the Americans who served in Desert Shield and Desert Storm — overcoming congressional setbacks, years of bureaucratic hurdles and a lengthy battle over its location, all while refusing to use taxpayer money to get it done.

"I'm just an average everyday American," Scott Stump told Fox News Digital. "I just felt like something had to be done to correct what I felt was a historical oversight."

What began as one veteran's determination to ensure the sacrifices of the 1990-1991 Gulf War generation were not forgotten became a 16-year undertaking that culminated in the Oct. 24, 2026, opening of the Desert Shield and Desert Storm Memorial on the National Mall.

Stump, who spearheaded the effort with a team of veteran volunteers, said he insisted from the beginning that the project be built without U.S. taxpayer funding, even as it required years of congressional action, federal approvals and financial support from donors.

TRUMP SIGNS ACT CLEARING THE WAY FOR MEDAL OF HONOR MONUMENT ON NATIONAL MALL

"I made the decision right off the bat that [I] was not interested in encumbering the taxpayer at all," Stump said. "This should be 100% privately funded. Just felt like it was the right thing to do."

Stump said the idea first came to him in November 2010, as the country approached the 20th anniversary of Operation Desert Storm, the U.S.-led military campaign that liberated Kuwait from Iraqi occupation.

He had attended veterans events where service members from other conflicts were recognized, but those who fought in Desert Shield and Desert Storm were overlooked.

SIX AMERICAN HEROES EVERYONE SHOULD KNOW THIS MEMORIAL DAY, FROM DELTA FORCE TO A WWII BOMBER CREW

Scott Stump spent 16 years fighting to build a national memorial honoring the Americans who served in Desert Shield and Desert Storm. (Scott Stump)

"They skipped right over it, and they act like it never happened," Stump recalled.

Living in Waynesville, North Carolina, Stump had no elaborate political operation or established Washington network to carry out his vision. Instead, he assembled a team of fellow veterans who shared his belief that the conflict deserved a permanent place in the nation's history, he said.

Stump worked with then-Rep. Phil Roe, R-Tenn., and Sen. John Boozman, R-Ark., to secure the legislation necessary to authorize the memorial.

"It was kind of a ‘Mr. Smith Goes to Washington,’" Stump said, referring to the classic film about an ordinary American navigating Washington politics.

EXCAVATION SET TO BEGIN ON TRUMP ADMINISTRATION'S 250-FOOT TRIUMPHAL ARCH AMID LEGAL BATTLE

Scott Stump spent 16 years fighting to build a national memorial honoring the Americans who served in Desert Shield and Desert Storm. (Scott Stump)

The legislative effort alone took roughly three and a half to four years, and Stump said he was forced to start over after legislation introduced late in one congressional session failed to advance.

He reintroduced the effort in 2013, testified before Congress in February 2014 and watched the House approve the measure in a unanimous 370-0 vote on May 28, 2014.

Stump said the Senate effort encountered additional legislative complications that threatened to derail the memorial authorization before the congressional session ended. He fought to clear the obstacle, knowing another failure could mean starting the legislative process from scratch for a third time.

The authorization ultimately passed as part of the National Defense Authorization Act for fiscal year 2015, which President Barack Obama signed into law in December 2014.

LAWYER SAYS 250-FOOT TRUMP ARCH DISRUPTS HISTORIC SIGHTLINES CHERISHED BY VETERANS SEEKING BURIAL AT ARLINGTON

Scott Stump spent 16 years fighting to build a national memorial honoring the Americans who served in Desert Shield and Desert Storm. (Scott Stump)

Next came the challenge of securing a location worthy of the service members the memorial would honor. Stump initially believed the site-selection process would take approximately 18 months. Instead, it stretched to 39 months, he recounted.

He said much of that time was spent educating officials and members of federal commissions about the significance of Desert Shield and Desert Storm. Some questioned whether the conflict had truly ended, he said. Others questioned whether enough Americans had died to justify a prominent national memorial.

"’There weren't enough people who died to have a memorial for this,’" Stump recalled being told during the approval process.

After initially receiving approval for a different location, Stump and his team continued pushing for their preferred site near 23rd Street and Constitution Avenue.

FEDERAL JUDGE TEMPORARILY BLOCKS CONSTRUCTION OF TRUMP'S ARLINGTON TRIUMPHAL ARCH HOURS AFTER APPROVAL

Scott Stump spent 16 years fighting to build a national memorial honoring the Americans who served in Desert Shield and Desert Storm. (Scott Stump)

They secured approval on June 21, 2018, in an "absolute epic battle," Stump said.

"We are not interested in just something visible," Stump said, recalling the argument he made to commissioners. "But we wanted something that was visitable."

He wanted visitors to do more than pass a monument on their way to another destination. He wanted them to walk through the memorial, experience its design and leave inspired to learn about a war that had faded from the memories of many Americans.

Iraq invaded Kuwait in August 1990, prompting President George H.W. Bush to launch Operation Desert Shield and build an international coalition. After months of military buildup, Operation Desert Storm began Jan. 17, 1991, with weeks of airstrikes before a ground offensive launched Feb. 24 drove Iraqi forces from Kuwait in approximately 100 hours.

GOLDEN EAGLES, LIONS AND A WINGED LADY LIBERTY TOP TRUMP'S PROPOSED 250-FOOT DC TRIUMPHAL ARCH DESIGNS

Scott Stump spent 16 years fighting to build a national memorial honoring the Americans who served in Desert Shield and Desert Storm. (Scott Stump)

"You know, you have 30 some odd countries, five continents, different races, religions, creeds," Stump said. "It's a wonderful story."

Outside of the monument this month, DJ of Wisconsin told Fox News Digital his mother served in the Air Force as a nurse in the early '90s. She was never deployed overseas, but during her time, she worked with many veterans from Desert Storm.

"[The memorial] reminded me of her and her service, and just the thanks that I have both for her and for all Americans who sign a blank check for the country that can be paid for up to and including their lives. So, I'm just very thankful, and this is a good, good, humble reminder of that sacrifice," said DJ.

Desert Storm veteran Steve from Indiana told Fox News Digital he was "impressed" that the memorial was so close to the Lincoln Memorial and the Vietnam Memorial.

"In some ways, Desert Storm is somewhat forgotten. It was a relatively small war, but a very successful one, and I think there are a lot of lessons to be learned from that war," said Steve.

DAKOTA MEYER TALKS SERVICE, BLASTS CONTROVERSIAL DESIGN FOR GLOBAL WAR ON TERROR MEMORIAL

Scott Stump spent 16 years fighting to build a national memorial honoring the Americans who served in Desert Shield and Desert Storm. (Scott Stump)

The Department of the Interior and National Park Service formally welcomed the Desert Shield and Desert Storm Memorial to the National Mall in Washington, D.C., in October, honoring the Americans who served.

"The Desert Shield and Desert Storm Memorial honors the courage of the Americans who answered our nation’s call, the sacrifice of those who never returned home and the families who supported them," Interior Secretary Doug Burgum said.

Stump said he intentionally structured the organization behind the memorial as an association rather than a traditional foundation, allowing the project to maintain a singular focus on completing the monument rather than supporting a permanent payroll or other long-term institutional expenses. He added he did not want future operating costs to force compromises in the design.

"Our job, and I believe that we've achieved it through a wonderful design, a wonderful design team that worked extremely hard to come up with what we have, is to make an emotional connection with the visitor," Stump said.

He pointed to the Korean War Veterans Memorial as an example of how a monument can move visitors even when they know little about the conflict it commemorates.

Young people, he said, often encounter the memorial's statues of soldiers wearing ponchos and leave wanting to understand the war behind those figures.

That is the experience he hopes the Desert Shield and Desert Storm Memorial will create for generations to come.

Ashley J. DiMella reports on politics for Fox News Digital.
