#5822. PPL increases capital plan to $25B with proposed Kentucky gas plant

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

The company projects some 50 GW of potential load growth, mostly from data centers, across Kentucky and Pennsylvania.

PPL Corp. raised its base capital plan to $25 billion for 2027 through 2030 — $2 billion more than the prior plan, primarily due to a proposed combined-cycle gas turbine plant in Kentucky, President and CEO Vincent Sorgi said during a Friday third-quarter earnings call.

If approved by regulators, the new plant would have 645 MW of capacity, and be expected to reach commercial operations in the second quarter of 2032, according to PPL’s earnings presentation.

In Kentucky, where PPL operates Louisville Gas and Electric and Kentucky Utilities, the company’s spending is set to increase from $2.3 billion in 2026 to $3.6 billion in 2027.

“I personally think we're going to be building generation for the foreseeable future in Kentucky, just given what we see with the quarter-over-quarter movement in the pipeline,” Sorgi said during the call.

Regarding the Kentucky Public Service Commission’s previous denials of PPL’s requests to build new gas capacity, Sorgi said, “Of course, the commission is going to be diligent in their review of all of that, but conservatism is not cost-free.”

“Sometimes, being overly conservative in regulatory outcomes ends up costing our customers, and we've seen that in the past,” he continued.

“I clearly want to try to stave that off in this next [Certificate of Public Convenience and Necessity],” which is anticipated in the first quarter of 2027, he added.

Sorgi said that the next “next CPCN filing will occur once we have signed a data center [electric service agreement]” in the state.

Kentucky’s development pipeline “has expanded by 1 GW to 14.7 GW of potential load growth through 2032,” with data center-related load representing 12.8 GW, according to PPL’s earnings presentation.

PPL also continues to see a strong data center pipeline in Pennsylvania, where projects in “advanced stages” increased for the 11th consecutive quarter to 35.3 GW, up 3.5 GW from the second quarter, Sorgi said.

Sorgi said the company is seeing “significant progress” in converting that pipeline into committed projects.

“Projects with a probability of success of 50% or greater have increased to 4 GW of additional peak demand, with the average probability for these projects sitting at almost 85%,” he said.

Invitium Energy – which PPL owns 51% of in a joint venture with Blackstone – signed a letter of intent on Friday with QTS Data Centers “to be their generation solution provider in portions of [Pennsylvania],” said PPL’s earnings presentation. Blackstone also owns QTS.

“Invitium has agreed to all key commercial terms with QTS providing for ~2GW of generation capacity to supply power to QTS’s Salem Township data center project with a 20-year initial term with multiple five-year renewals permitted,” the presentation said.

The proposed generation portfolio “provides for two combined cycle natural gas plants complemented with fuel cells and battery storage,” it said.

The structure of the deal “provides a regulated-like risk profile with fixed monthly payments (with annual escalation) covering return of and on invested capital plus fixed operating costs and variable payments when the plants run, covering fuel and variable operating costs,” the presentation added.

Sorgi expressed confidence about the company’s data center-related business in response to an analyst’s question about the potential impact of the midterm election results.

“The national election results could actually support our pipelines that we have both in Pennsylvania and Kentucky,” he said. “Both of our governors have signed executive orders for developing data centers and doing it the right way.”

He suggested that restrictions on data centers in other states could be a net benefit to PPL.

“I do think some of the election results could, in some other states, potentially have a chilling impact, which could drive some of those projects or future development projects to both Pennsylvania and Kentucky,” Sorgi said.

#5819. REDD — Picking Up Our New Tesla Model Y

REDD, the name for our new Tesla Model Y (Standard Range ’26 build, ’27 model), was inspired by the movie of the same name – Retired, Extremely Dangerous Drivers (the wife and I, of course). As mentioned previously, there is nothing wrong with our beloved 2019 Model 3. It is our bodies that are aging and less able to get in and out of our beloved little sporty Tess. So it was with mixed feelings that we arrived at the Tesla delivery centre to pick up the new car.

As always, it was a hive of activity, with deliveries taking place all around us. Quite a contrast to 7 years ago when Tesla was operating out of one cramped delivery spot near the centre of Brisbane, and the handover took less than 3 minutes. This time there were plenty of sales representatives spending their time and making sure we knew what we were doing.

On arrival, we walked past a line consisting of ICE cars being surrendered as trade-ins for an electric. As we left, a car carrier was unloading half a dozen new Model Ys. All in all, a smooth operation. While Majella was getting her phone synced and lots of information from the sales rep, I had a wander around.

The delivery centre is divided in two by a screen. On our side of the screen, I could see at least 60 new cars anxiously awaiting their new owners — like puppies at the pound, I thought. I wasn’t allowed behind the curtain where cars were being detailed and prepped. I commented to our sales rep that there didn’t seem to be many Model 3s. He told me that they had just received a Model Y shipment; the 3s were on their way. Asked about what lay behind the curtain in Tesla wonderland, he said that there were hundreds of cars “back there.”

Majella drove REDD home and I drove Tess. I am having mixed feelings. I get attached to my cars; it was difficult to sell George (my ‘64 Wolseley) and Ruby (my Hyundai Accent). But they were old and tired and their time had come. Tess, at 180,000+ kilometres still feels young. It’s me that’s getting old. The letting go is being made easier because we are selling Tess to our youngest daughter. So, we will still be in contact with the car and watch the milestones as she grows.

As we sipped our celebratory champagne, I mused at the difference between buying the Model Y in 2026 and the Model 3 in 2019. Going from the Hyundai to the Tesla was a massive leap in both design and technology — the screen, the regen braking. Our Tesla was the only one in our suburb, and there was no high-speed charging. Electric vehicles were seen as a “soon to pass” fad on the way to hydrogen cars, and a stupid decision in a country as big as Australia. In 2019, less than one percent of new cars sold had a plug, Tesla was still on a deathwatch. We had taken a risk — would it work out?

It certainly has! Owning Tess and dealing with FUD gave me a cause to champion. I started exploring this new world and writing about it — driving long trips and proving that an EV can do what it has to. CleanTechnica published my ramblings — which have now reached over 1,000 articles. People contacted me and shared their stories of private ownership. CEOs of new startups (like recently introduced Thermal Dawn) gave me interviews and insights. This might sound weird, but moving from the Model 3 to the Model Y is nowhere near as exciting. Yes, I will continue to write, but the motoring world has changed. In Australia, the Model Y has been the highest selling passenger car of all drivetrains for the past few months. In October, it even beat the refreshed Toyota RAV4. My northern Brisbane suburb is full of Tesla Model Ys. The crossover has become common.

Motoring journalists are learning slowly to accept the new kids on the block, and most of their reviews seem to indicate a modicum of tolerance and truth. Of course, we still have vested interests proclaiming that Chinese cars are destroying the Australian car industry. Read: dealerships can’t sell ICE cars and aren’t making enough money. I wonder sometimes what there will be left write about as plugins take more and more of the market, and “real” motoring journalists write the articles I normally write. Relax, I am sure there will be room for all of us for some time. Especially in the USA!

Calls continue for more charging infrastructure, which is fair enough for highway and tourist travel, but seems to totally ignore home charging. “I live in a remote town and there is nowhere to charge my car!” wails one. I could scream if he would hear me: plug it into your house power socket, you moron!

Tess sits in the carport plugged into our home battery. Majella has taken REDD to her gem club. I am sure that instead of their normal lapidary, faceting, and jewellery making, they will be exploring REDD. Four other members of the club have now got Teslas or BYDs. One of them commented that they want a Model Y for Christmas — not sure if his wife will agree.

REDD will also be the guest of honour at our upcoming Coffee, Cake, and EV morning tea in a few days’ time.

Once we have done the roadworthy and registration transfer, Tess will be off to her new home. We will get to see her at family gatherings and when we pick up the grandkidlets from school. Majella is busy buying the accessories — mats, ceiling screen (no, the new heavy tint doesn’t do much in the Australian sun), rim protectors, etc. REDD came with front and back floor mats. She is buying all-weather mats for the frunk and trunk. The dog may even get to sit in the car! Shock! Horror! And we are slowly noticing subtle differences, like the reduction in noise driving on the highway — REDD has better seals and double-glazed windows.

We may have lost some of the apps that came free with Tess (Spotify, Karaoke, connectivity), but we have gained cooling seats and a heated steering wheel. And, of course, Hardware 4, which will give us access to FSD (Supervised) for our long trips. Speaking of longer trips, the range of our Model 3 (about 300 km with some degradation) pales beside the 460 km range of the LFP batteries in REDD. LFP batteries also mean less anxiety about charging to 100% when needed. REDD is longer, wider, and taller, but, thank goodness, still fits in our garage.

Majella is missing Enhanced Autopilot, for which we paid AU$5,000 just a few years ago. The Model Y’s cruise control is not as convenient. Stay tuned for updates as we enter the next leg of our electric journey.

David Waterworth is a retired teacher who divides his time between looking after his grandchildren and trying to make sure they have a planet to live on. He is long on Tesla [NASDAQ:TSLA].

#5817. Solar and storage companies voice support for federal permitting reform

Hundreds of solar and storage companies have co-signed a letter addressed to Congress in support of the Bipartisan American Affordability and Jobs Act of 2026 (BAAJA) (S. 5653).

“You can’t lower energy costs if you can’t build the energy projects, and these companies know that this bipartisan legislation will help get America building again,” said Tim Pawlenty, CEO of the Solar Energy Industries Association (SEIA), in a press release.

BAAJA was introduced in the Senate on Sept. 30 by Sen. Shelly Moore Capito (R-WV), and co-sponsored by Sen. Mike Lee (R-UT), Sen. Sheldon Whitehouse (D-RI) and Sen. Martin Heinrich (D-NM). The legislation is focused on quickening permitting for a range of projects with federal oversight, many of them related to energy production. BAAJA specifically calls out solar photovoltaic systems, energy storage and on- and offshore wind, but also includes language for fossil fuel sources, such as natural gas, oil and coal.

If passed, the Senate bill would amend sections of the National Environmental Policy Act, Clean Water Act and Endangered Species Act of 1973 with the goal of shortening project permitting windows. According to the text, it would change and reduce which projects would need to be reviewed under those acts, such as water quality certifications; shorten the length of review periods for projects; and would not require permits for oil and gas drilling on land not owned by the federal government.

BAAJA would also grant the Federal Energy Regulatory Commission the ability to issue permits for some electricity transmission projects.

According to a SEIA analysis, if BAAJA was passed, solar energy output could increase by 167 GW between 2028 and 2035. The companies that signed this letter to Congress believe that federal permitting practices are outdated, and permitting reform is a solution to lowering energy costs.

“The message from more than 300 companies comes as voters made clear in the midterm elections that lowering energy costs is a top concern,” the group stated in a press release. “Permitting reform is urgently needed to meet rising demand and get more reliable, affordable solar and storage projects built.”

BAAJA is currently under review by the Committee on Energy and Natural Resources.

Billy Ludt is managing editor of Solar Power World and currently covers topics on mounting, inverters, installation and operations.

#5815. BMW Pitches Free EV Charging And You Ain’t Seen Nothing Yet

EV sales in the US are beginning to tick up again, and BMW is determined to grab a slice of the action. The German automaker has begun offering a free EV charging package to anyone who purchases a 2027 BMW i5, BMW i7, or BMW iX3. The package includes $500 in charging credits, which seems like small potatoes compared to the cost of a BMW. Still, it’s better than nothing, and it positions the company to compete in a rapidly evolving landscape that includes free charging hours.

The “free hours” trend has already emerged in the US, where Texas (of course!) is an epicenter of activity. The idea is to smooth out peak weekday demand spikes and take advantage of spare capacity on the grid by offering ratepayers an incentive to use more electricity during nighttime hours, with the leading utility Constellation being a representative example.

“When you sign up for a free night electricity plan, a smart meter monitors your energy usage by time of day,” Constellation explains. The company offers a “Free Power Hours” plan between midnight and 4 a.m. every night.

“Constellation also offers Discounted Nights & Fair Days, with discounted electricity supply rates from 10 p.m. to 6 a.m.,” the company adds.

Constellation notes that standard service charges and other fees apply. They also advise that the free plan is not a good fit for households with heavy electricity use during the day, when higher rates apply.

Still, access to EV charging at a minimal cost is a tantalizing incentive for some households. Constellation currently offers its “FreeCharge” plan between midnight and 4 a.m. to ratepayers in Pennsylvania as well as Texas.

Against this backdrop, a mere $500 EV charging credit for a BMW is not much of an incentive, at least not in states where free hours are available. The monthly lease for the 2027 BMW i5, for example, clocks in at almost $750. Purchasing the car outright will set you back almost $70,000, including the destination fee.

Still, any incentive is better than no incentive, and the new offer provides BMW an opportunity to hitch its star to the free hours trend. “The new offer, available beginning November 5, 2026, is designed to make public charging simpler, more convenient and more affordable for BMW customers,” explains BMW.

“The package includes a $300 BMW Charging Network credit that can be used at participating charging providers1, as well as a separate $200 credit for charging at IONNA locations,” the company elaborates, with IONNA referring to a consortium of EV makers (including BMW) focused on improving the driver experience at charging stations.

In addition to the $200 IONNA credit, BMW is also offering a one-year IONNA membership plan that provides for a 25% charging discount.

For the record, the BMW Charging Network includes more than 180,000 charging stations compatible with the company’s My BMW app.

Over and above the EV charging package, BMW is also among the electrification stakeholders that did not throw in the towel after last year’s EV sales crash. Despite the premature demise of the federal EV tax credit on September 30 of 2025, the number of public EV charging stations in the US has continued to increase, and charging networks have become more sophisticated and convenient.

In addition to the hookup with IONNA, for example, the BMW Charging Network includes Shell Recharge, Electrify America, ChargePoint, and EVgo among others.

“The company also has invested in Plug & Charge, which simplifies the charging experience; and ChargeScape, a collaborative effort to help manage the flow of energy between EVs and the power grid,” BMW added in a press statement last week.

ChargeScape represents another indication that Trump’s war against EVs was over before it started, tax credits or not.

ChargeScape launched three years ago as a joint venture between BMW, Ford, and the US branch of Honda. “Benefiting both EV customers and the electric utility industry in the U.S. and Canada, ChargeScape will unlock entirely new value that EVs can provide to the electric grid, while enabling EV customers to earn financial benefits through a variety of managed charging and energy-sharing services never before possible with traditional gasoline-powered vehicles,” the three automakers enthused in a press statement dated December 29, 2023.

“ChargeScape’s platform will give electric utilities access to EV battery energy across a wide pool of EVs. Participating EV customers will have the potential to earn financial benefits by charging at ‘grid-friendly’ times through flexible and managed schedules,” the partners elaborated.

“The development of this project comes at a time when electric vehicle sales and infrastructure growth are ramping up quickly,” the partners added.

That was certainly an optimistic take on the future of EV sales in the US, but that was December of 2023. Just about one year later, then-candidate Donald Trump sailed to victory on Election Day 2024, winning a second turn around the Oval Office with a plan to stop the vehicle electrification trend in its tracks.

Still, ChargeScape soldiered on. By September of 2024 the alliance was ready for launch, to be joined by Nissan just one month later. ChargeScape also added the startup Rivian to its rolls in June of this year, with an option to add bidirectional EV charging. Other automakers, including Tesla, also use the platform.

The bidirectional charging element is where things get particularly interesting. The technology emerged in the early 2000’s, enabling EV drivers to power their home from their car battery, or send electricity to the grid. Unlocking the mass market potential of bidirectional charging is a next-level challenge, and the free hours trend could help accelerate uptake. With free hours EV charging, drivers can store electricity at minimal cost during those periods, and discharge it to trim the cost of electricity for their homes during periods when higher electricity rates apply.

Ford is particularly excited about the potential for bidirectional charging to scale up. The company’s forthcoming Ford Fathom electric pickup truck comes with an internal inverter that converts the DC charge from the battery into an AC current for home use. The built-in inverter eliminates the cost of the external, wall mounted inverter otherwise needed for bidirectional charging at home.

Photo: BMW is sweetening the EV charging pot with a $500 charging credit on its 2027 i5, i7, and iX3 electric cars in the US (courtesy of BMW).

#5812. A New $1.5 Billion Wind Farm Will Play In Peoria

It ain’t over ’til it’s over, but the proposed $1.5 billion, 500-megawatt Four Creeks wind farm in Peoria County, Illinois has just passed a key step in the approval process. The new milestone is yet another indication that the domestic wind industry will continue to play a key role in the US energy transition, regardless of the topsy-turvy energy policy articulated by US President Donald Trump.

If you’re somewhat shocked to hear word of a new wind farm proposal in the US, join the club. The President has spent considerable time, energy, and taxpayer dollars in a Herculean effort to keep new wind turbines from cropping up around the US under his watch. However, his efforts have earned only mixed success so far.

Offshore wind turbines keep slipping through the cracks, even though virtually every offshore wind farm in the US is subject to federal authority. Opportunities for wind power also persist on land, where the President’s direct authority is limited to projects on federal property.

Trump attempted to work around that obstacle in August of 2025, when he weaponized an otherwise routine wind project review process that applies to projects on non-federal property. The change in policy threw at least 155 onshore wind projects totaling 45 gigawatts into limbo, prompting a lawsuit from a coalition of clean energy organizations and wind energy developers.

In July of this year, Attorneys General in 19 states intervened in support of the lawsuit. The participants included Arizona, California, Colorado, Connecticut, Delaware, the District of Columbia, Illinois, Maine, Massachusetts, Maryland, Michigan, Minnesota, Nevada, New Jersey, New Mexico, New York, Oregon, Rhode Island, and Washington State.

Illinois Attorney General Kwame Raoul, who co-led the intervention, notes that federal law does require wind developers to submit proposals for wind turbines more than 200 feet tall to the Federal Aviation Administration, which then refers the project to DoD to check for any conflicts with military operations and related activities. Raoul further notes that the normal process involves collaborating on mitigation measures to resolve any conflicts.

That all ground to a halt in August of 2025, when DoD dropped the ball on its part of the process. “Officials ceased countersigning mitigation agreements, stopped sending completed agreements to developers for signature, and delayed or halted communications with developers about mitigation,” Raoul explained in a press statement in July.

The abrupt change in policy impacted at least nine wind projects in Illinois alone. According to Raoul’s office the nine projects represent $4.3 billion in private investment, or more.

Separately, on June 16 of this year the same coalition of state Attorneys General won the final resolution of a series of legal challenges to an Executive Order issued in January of 2025, in which Trump ordered a halt to all federal approvals for wind projects.

The DoD lawsuit is heading down the same path, at least so far. On August 6, a federal judge with the US District Court for the District of Oregon ordered DoD to resume its normal wind turbine review process while the case continues to wind its way through the courts.

DoD has been slow-walking the process since then, reportedly approving just 11 projects since August. However, the US branch of the global clean energy developer Repsol already saw the writing on the wall. On August 14, just one week after the Oregon decision came down, Repsol Renewables began submitting paperwork for the proposed Four Creeks Wind project.

“Repsol Renewables has identified northwest Peoria County as suitable for wind development because of a robust wind resource, its proximity to the existing electric transmission system with available capacity, compatibility with existing land use, and minimal anticipated environmental impacts,” Repsol summarized, with existing land use referring to provisions in the Peoria County Code that permit commercial wind farms in certain agricultural districts.

Although the wind farm involves a total of 27,000 acres on private land, each of the project’s 113 turbines will only account for less than one acre. The bulk of the land is dedicated to required setbacks as well as adequate spacing between the turbines.

Things are moving along at a relatively fast clip so far. On November 6, Peoria public radio station WCBU reported that the land use committee of the Peoria County Board recommended the Four Creeks project by a 6-3 vote. The project almost appeared to be dead in September when the Zoning Board of Appeals issued a 4-1 denial, but the Zoning Board also voted to include conditions for consideration by the full Peoria County Board at its meeting on November 5. The County Board approved the Four Creeks proposal on a split vote of 12-5 alongside a list of 36 restrictions mainly governing construction and operations.

Wind fans, don’t break out the champagne just yet. Repsol still has to apply for construction permits, and the entire process could take up to five years or so before shovels meet ground.

If and when Four Creeks materializes, Repsol is prepared to pour more than $193 million into the coffers of the Princeville, Brimfield, Williamsfield, and Stark County School Districts, with an additional $44.7 million in total property tax revenues for Peoria County and associated jurisdictions.

Repsol also draws attention to more than $69.4 million in total property tax revenues earmarked for libraries, fire districts, Illinois Central College, local road authorities, and the Greater Metro Airport Authority among other community resources.

Local property owners will also rake in millions of dollars in escalating lease payments.

As for objections to the new wind farm, WCBU reporter Joe Deacon highlighted remarks by Peoria County Board member Terry Ruhland, who acknowledged those who spoke against the  project at a public meeting.

“I don’t know how many people are in this room; I won’t even guess. But there is at least 179,000 people in Peoria County who are not in this room,” Rhuland said.

“Of that 179 [thousand], for every school kid who deserves the best education we can give them, and for every family that’s struggling to pay their utility bills, I’m going to vote yes,” Ruhland added.

Perhaps Ruhland was also thinking of Laborers Local 165, which has been working with Repsol for more than a year on developing a local workforce for the project. In a hearing before the Zoning Board in September, union representative Dane Simpson asserted that each wind turbine would account for approximately 3,000 hours of work.

Image: The global energy developer Repsol is on track to construct a 500-megawatt wind farm in Peoria County, Illinois, regardless of last year’s sharp U-turn in federal energy policy (screenshot, courtesy of Repsol).

#5808. Tesla Hasn’t Had A Successful New Product Launch Since JB Straubel Stepped Down As CTO

As has been widely reported, Tesla is about to end Cybertruck production. The Cybertruck has been a shocking failure. I’m sure there are more detailed analyses out there somewhere, but this short response on X from Ben McCombe that Tina Casey included in her article on the matter sums it up very well:

“Installed tooling to build 125k Cybertrucks a year.

“Made just $100m in gross profits from sales.

That’s pretty wild. A net project loss of around $2 billion. Now, I have to admit that I haven’t fact checked the numbers here, but what we know for certain is that Tesla planned to produce a lot of Cybertrucks per year and ended up producing a small fraction of that target. In fact, the annual sales target for the Cybertruck was higher than 125,000 — it was 250,000, with a goal of getting up to 500,000!

The Cybertruck was hyped up as having more reservations than any vehicle in history and was supposedly going to shatter records and transform the market. I warned people on many occasions that the reservation total was not a real reservation total and was definitely not a reliable indicator of demand because a) a reservation only required a $100 refundable deposit, and b) a ton of people made reservations after Elon Musk said that you could lock in the price of lifetime Full Self Driving when making a reservation (so, you’d only have to pay $6,000 for FSD no matter how much the cost of it went up in the following years). Anyway, the Cybertruck had other issues as well when it finally arrived. It cost about twice as much as it was supposed to cost and it had about half as much range as it was supposed to have. In other words, it was not even close to the vehicle it was supposed to be — its range per dollar cost was about ¼ what it was supposed to be.

The Cybertruck was supposed to be the next big thing after the Tesla Model Y. It was supposed to disrupt the gigantic US pickup truck market. The Model S was a superb and widely acclaimed car, if a bit more expensive than the mass market could afford. The Model S did in fact compete superbly well with the leaders in its segment, the BMW 7 Series and Mercedes S-Class. The Tesla Model X brought a similar offering in a larger package with some fun extra elements (falcon-wing doors, self-presenting doors, a third row). The Tesla Model 3 then completely shook the automotive world as a tremendous market phenomenon and perhaps the biggest product launch in history (in terms of total revenue). The Tesla Model Y was basically the Model 3 in a more popular crossover package and eventually became a much higher selling vehicle. In fact, it became the highest selling vehicle model of any kind in multiple years. Then JB Straubel stepped down as CTO.

Due to their EV passions, JB Straubel and Elon Musk were introduced to each other and had lunch together to talk about electric airplanes (Elon’s dream) and then electric cars (because JB talked more sense into Elon). Then the two of them were introduced to Marc Tarpenning and Martin Eberhard, who had founded Tesla Motors and just barely got started on the idea. After a while, Tarpenning and Eberhard left the company, and Musk and Straubel truly led its development and growth into an automotive superpower (as briefly described above). Since JB Straubel stepped down from his role as CTO of Tesla, however, if one thinks about it, it’s hard to identify any successful product launch from the company or much significant improvement at all.

Straubel left his CTO position in July 2019. The Model Y was unveiled in March 2019 and was about ⅔ the same as the Model 3. It would seem that getting the Model Y designed and prepared for launch was the last big project Straubel worked on. (Note that the Semi was unveiled in November 2017 but was long delayed due to battery supply, according to Elon Musk.) Since the Model Y, what significant product launch have we seen from Tesla? There was the Cybertruck, as we’ve already discussed. That was unveiled in November 2019. When you think about the timing now, did Straubel disagree strongly with Musk on the Cybertruck and decide it was time to move on when Musk pushed forward with his idea for an electric truck? It took another 4+ years for Tesla to start delivering the Cybertruck, in contrast to Model Y deliveries starting in January 2020, less than a year after the model was unveiled. More recently, the Cybercab has been developed, unveiled, and is now in production. A consumer car smaller and cheaper than the Model 3 was never developed and unveiled — just a stripped down, decontented Model 3 — and there basically haven’t been any other product unveilings since Straubel left his role as CTO. Elon Musk has published a couple of somewhat scattered, rambling “master plans,” but we haven’t gotten any significant new products aside from the Cybertruck and the just arriving Cybercab.

Even looking over on the energy storage side of things, the Tesla Megapack was launched in July 2019, the month Straubel stepped down from his role as CTO. Did Straubel just decide he had done enough once he had finished the Megapack launch and gotten the Model Y essentially ready for market? Did he have conflicts with Musk on where to take the company from there?

Yes, there’s been incremental product development and refreshed versions of Tesla cars and energy storage systems. There are still plenty of engineers working on improving things. However, there doesn’t seem to be the same leadership in new product development, and there also isn’t the same practical, moderating element that Straubel seemingly provided to Musk’s more wild, grandiose, sci-fi inspired dreams and ideas.

Perhaps this is all misunderstanding the roles of the two men to some extent. Perhaps things would not be much different if Straubel had stayed in the CTO role and had the same influence he had before leaving. He is still on the board of directors. (What that actually means is unclear, though.) Perhaps Tesla’s success and business would still be primarily based on the Model 3 and Model Y, without new transformative products. Or perhaps we would have had a more normal pickup truck from Tesla, an electric van, better batteries, a better market rollout of the Semi, and other products. Perhaps the Cybertruck, an epic product failure, never would have been shown to the public and hyped up unrealistically with specs and figures disconnected from what was going to be possible. Perhaps we’d have a Model C (affordable, compact Tesla) rather than a Cybercab. Perhaps Tesla would be a very different company.

Who knows? We don’t know the ins and outs of JB Straubel and Elon Musk’s relationship, or how much Straubel actually led Tesla in his CTO role. We don’t know how much Straubel tamped down Musk’s worst instincts or guided product development. We still really don’t even know why Straubel left. According to Tesla, it was so that he could focus more time on Redwood Materials, but why wasn’t that business within the umbrella of Tesla in the first place? Vertical integration has long been a key element of the company. Who knows? There’s a lot we don’t know and never will. However, what we do know is that since JB Straubel stepped down from his role as CTO of Tesla, we haven’t seen a significant, successful new product launch at the company. We’ll have to wait a bit longer to see if the Cybercab is one, and whether that sets a new, positive course for the future of the company or not.

#5804. Even California’s Biggest Ports Aren’t Betting On Hydrogen For Ships

California has been one of hydrogen’s most enthusiastic supporters for two decades. The state has subsidized hydrogen vehicles, backed refueling networks, supported fuel-cell buses and trucks, invested in hydrogen production and kept the technology prominent in its zero-emission transportation policies. Los Angeles and Long Beach, the two enormous ports that anchor America’s Pacific container trade, have been part of that enthusiasm. So when these ports produce a comprehensive assessment of future marine fuels and leave hydrogen out of their detailed deep-sea shipping plans, it deserves attention well beyond California.

The Clean Marine Fuel Feasibility Assessment, released in October, is the product of a substantial examination of shipping fleets, carrier preferences, fuel availability and infrastructure costs. The ports recommend developing commercial methanol bunkering demonstrations, continuing existing ship-to-ship LNG bunkering arrangements and avoiding major new fuel terminals until demand becomes credible. Hydrogen, the molecule that California has spent so much money promoting for transportation, did not make the cut for detailed quantitative deep-sea fuel planning.

This isn’t a state government announcing the end of hydrogen subsidies. It is arguably more revealing. Two commercially important institutions have examined what the ships serving them are likely to use, considered the infrastructure they would have to provide and declined to spend the next decade preparing a major hydrogen bunkering business.

California’s hydrogen story has repeatedly confused technical possibility with commercial demand. Fuel-cell passenger cars proved they could operate, but struggled with expensive fuel, unreliable stations and battery-electric competition. Hydrogen buses and trucks have faced many of the same issues, often compounded by the economics of supplying fuel to fleets. Ports have supported hydrogen demonstrations because eliminating diesel exhaust matters enormously to nearby communities, but a successful demonstration does not establish that the resulting system is the best commercial solution.

Deep-sea shipping adds a particularly difficult set of constraints. A container vessel requires immense amounts of energy over long voyages. Hydrogen’s low volumetric energy density, expensive handling and demanding onboard storage make the fuel system difficult to accommodate economically. The problem extends beyond the ship to production, transportation, storage and bunkering equipment. There is little reason for a port authority to build a hydrogen fuel network when shipping lines are not ordering a commercially significant fleet that would use it.

The ports’ survey reinforces the commercial judgment. Of 38 shipping lines contacted, 19 responded. Nine expressed varying degrees of interest or commitment concerning methanol, but only one did so for hydrogen. That is a survey of carrier intentions, rather than binding fuel contracts, and the researchers examined other evidence as well. Still, it is difficult to turn one interested carrier into a compelling investment case for expensive hydrogen infrastructure.

The contrast with methanol is instructive. Methanol is a liquid under ordinary conditions and fits into a mature global chemical-handling industry. Existing storage and bunkering equipment can be adapted instead of requiring an entirely new cryogenic or high-pressure fuel network. Long Beach is already offering a $1 million incentive for an initial commercial-scale methanol bunkering operation, an approach that can test operational feasibility without committing to an enormous permanent facility.

I have a personal interest in that choice because I had to correct my own shipping-fuel forecast. Last year, I published Mea Culpa: Biomethanol Will Be A Major Shipping Fuel. I had previously given too much weight to vegetable-oil-derived fuels for shipping and insufficient weight to aviation’s competition for those feedstocks. I had also underestimated how far cheaper batteries could displace marine fuel demand. My revised assessment favours electricity for inland and short-sea shipping, with sustainable biomethanol becoming a primary liquid fuel for the remaining deep-sea requirement. The ports’ decision is consistent with preparing infrastructure for that possibility, although it does not establish that their future fuel supplies will be renewable.

The distinction is important because methanol is not inherently a climate solution. It can be manufactured from natural gas, coal, biomass or electricity-based production pathways, and those routes have radically different greenhouse gas consequences. California’s ports can build a technically successful methanol bunkering service without achieving substantial shipping decarbonization if the fuel delivered is fossil-based. That question belongs alongside the commercial case, not behind it.

LNG presents another caution. The ports are accommodating existing LNG-capable ships without recommending major new shoreside LNG infrastructure. It is a conservative capital decision, although fossil LNG’s climate case remains weak when methane leakage and engine slip are included. Reductions in sulfur and particulate emissions are useful, but they are not the same as meaningful lifecycle greenhouse gas reductions.

The larger significance of the study is the change in what gets taken seriously. For years, California’s hydrogen ambitions helped keep the molecule at the centre of transportation discussions. At Los Angeles and Long Beach, the planning process has produced a different hierarchy. Existing demand and affordable infrastructure determine the near-term investment choices. Hydrogen enthusiasm has not produced enough evidence to justify a deep-sea bunkering buildout.

There are weaknesses in the ports’ assessment, including the separation between bunkering readiness and verified emissions reductions, and the question of how electrification will change future fuel demand. Those deserve more scrutiny than a short article can provide. But the decision to leave hydrogen outside detailed deep-sea fuel planning is already consequential. California’s most important maritime gateways have applied a commercial filter that much of the state’s transportation hydrogen strategy has resisted.

My full TFIE Strategy Briefing assessment examines the ports’ fuel research, infrastructure costs, commercial demand estimates and emissions evidence, including where the study’s conclusions are sound and where its assumptions need more scrutiny.

#5803. Arizona’s longest-tenured utility commissioner on large loads, affordability and Western markets

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

“We have been talking for years that growth must pay for growth, and I think customers are asking for proof [of that],” Lea Márquez Peterson told Utility Dive on the sidelines of NARUC’s meeting.

Utility Dive sat down with Lea Márquez Peterson, a commissioner on the Arizona Corporation Commission, on Sunday at the National Association of Regulatory Utility Commissioners’ annual meeting in San Juan, Puerto Rico, to hear about her views on the challenges facing the ACC and other state regulators.

Márquez Peterson was appointed to the ACC in 2019 by the governor and won elections for four-year terms in 2020 and 2024 — making her the longest tenured commissioner at the agency today.

This interview has been edited for length and clarity.

UTILITY DIVE: It seems like affordability and data centers emerged as top issues at NARUC’s annual meeting two years ago. To what extent have those issues changed since then?

Lea Márquez Peterson: I think in Arizona, we are growing so rapidly, and we're seeing a lot of large load requests. But that's not just data centers — it's semiconductors. And so we're having conversations, probably a little more in depth and more publicly than we did two years ago, with water use leading the conversation.

The top thing, when you come to Arizona to build anything, is you tell us about your water use, and the public is very sensitive to this. They've seen a lot of requests and development projects come through our state, and not all have been as transparent as they should be.

What about concerns that large loads are causing rates to go up?

We saw that play out in this last election cycle. We have been talking for years that growth must pay for growth, and I think customers are asking for proof [of that]. What are the components of cost? How are you structuring this as a commission, and how do we ensure that growth is really paying for growth? And so you see that playing out across the nation, whether it's large-load tariffs, or, in [Arizona], energy supply agreements.

Has the commission set any rules around those agreements?

They evolved over time. I think they were simpler years ago, and as more and more load comes through … the utilities are getting even more sophisticated in how they're handling it.

Is there any interest in standardizing it with a large-load tariff?

Not statewide. We have our upcoming large rate cases coming up, and I think you'll see some development of their large load activity in those rate cases.

What levers does the commission have on affordability?

It could come to rate design, how that's structured. It's looking at the supply agreements. How much of the cost allocation should a large load be covering? I think we have a lot of different tools. Are the [large loads] going to be partners in some of the new generation?

I've been defined in the media as skeptical. I’d stick with that. We passed it as a policy statement in Arizona, and I thought it was interesting conceptually, but I wanted proof that it really was in the best interest for customers, and so I'm kind of working through that case by case as they come before us. We have [Tuscon Electric Power] before us next week and we have [Arizona Public Service] in mid-December.

On market development in the West, Arizona's big utilities are opting for the Southwest Power Pool’s Markets+. With Bonneville potentially switching to the Enhanced Day-Ahead Market, you asked the utilities to provide updated information about their decision. So how are you thinking about market development and opportunities for the Arizona utilities?

The ACC doesn’t have a choice [on which market the utilities join], but I wanted to better understand how that impacts customers — the exiting of the Energy Imbalance Market — whatever regional markets are decided, and I wanted to understand the [cost/benefit] analysis that was done by [the utilities].

I'm going to follow up with a workshop on Feb. 3 where I want to ask different questions on the components of those questions I asked in the letter, and then have some stakeholders ask questions, too … and just make sure that we're all aware. There’s no reason there can’t be transparency.

I want to just see that the benefits, whatever market they choose, is in the best interest of ratepayers. It makes a difference — whether from a reliability perspective, transmission availability, resource planning, whatever it is. I want to better understand the benefits.

How comfortable are you with Arizona’s generating resource situation?

I supported an “all the above” approach. Reliability is so key. That means you cannot have power outages, especially during the heat of summer.

I'm very focused on nuclear, but it's very expensive. About 30% of the state is served by the Palo Verde nuclear generating station. So we might see new nuclear in the future, but who's going to pay for that? What role do large load users have, perhaps?

Looking ahead to next year, what are the top issues that you and the ACC will be dealing with?

Next year, we will have voted on a number of formula rate cases. We will have annual true-ups, and I think there's a lot to explore and develop with those true-ups. Was there enough analysis done? Was there enough time for the [ACC] staff to do the analysis? How much could stakeholders weigh in?

#5801. A review of ‘Solar Roots: The Pioneers of PV’

By Tor “Solar Fred” Valenza | November 10, 2026

Was today’s modern solar industry inspired by off-grid California-living, marijuana-growing, Grateful Dead-loving hippies? That’s the main question explored in “Solar Roots: The Pioneers of PV,” a new documentary that recently premiered in Grass Valley, California.

The entertaining documentary traces the history, colorful characters, off-grid homeowners, solar educators and the entrepreneurs who embraced solar photovoltaic technology for vastly different reasons.

Shot on a shoestring budget with volunteers, “Solar Roots” may not have the polished production of a Hollywood documentary, but it is an untold story about the people and early solar and technology that led to the modern solar industry.

Since 2009, when I first started in the solar industry, I had heard jokes and anecdotes about the marijuana-growing hippies who sparked the residential solar industry. Until “Solar Roots,” no one had ever set up a camera to talk to those early solar pioneers and document why rural America became a hub of off-grid solar innovation.

Enter Jeff Spies, a longtime solar veteran and the documentary’s executive producer. In 2015, Spies organized a weekend party for the off-grid solar pioneers of Northern California, where he started his career in the mid-2000s. After hearing so many colorful stories about why these rural homesteaders bought $14/watt solar panels and started new solar installation and distribution businesses, Spies enlisted another solar industry veteran, Jason Vetterli, to travel to Southern California, Arizona, Oregon, Colorado and Wisconsin to capture these unique and personal stories on video.

This isn’t the first documentary about the solar industry or its challenges, and to be clear, Vetterli and Spies’ narrative is not about creating “the solar biz.” Truly, it’s about a particular tribe of solar early adopters. Through interviews, we learn about the 1970s, ’80s and ’90s counterculture movement and the people who stumbled into solar businesses out of necessity and good timing.

Listening to these stories, you quickly understand that none of these solar pioneers intended to get into the solar business to make millions or solve climate change. Their real motivations were tied to maintaining their independence from cities while having enough electricity to keep the lights on and the music playing while raising a family. Beyond the home applications, solar became a new water-pumping solution that supported the pioneers’ cash-only “agricultural” businesses.

Vetterli sets the film’s tone and theme with the first scene. Instead of fading in to a utility solar farm or suburban residential roofs, the film opens with a young hippie using a wire to connect a single 35-watt ARCO solar panel to a car battery that powers an old cassette boombox. He then inserts a Grateful Dead tape and presses play.

The Grateful Dead-inspired musical score, composed by Midnight North, a band whose members include another solar veteran, TJ Kanczuzewski, appropriately plays under the rest of the film’s narrative and interviews.

After a brief solar technology history lesson, Spies interviews the solar pioneers about the roots of their solar PV fascination, how they came to realize that solar could grow and impact their lives and their off-grid movement.

This realization wouldn’t have happened without the back-to-the-land movement. Buying land was inexpensive but getting a utility to build a power line to DIY houses in rural California cost millions. While living without electricity, we learn about their lives before and after PV.

Before PV, the pioneers had to jerry-rig their pickup trucks’ batteries to listen to music and use dangerous kerosene lanterns for light. Gasoline generators were loud, costly, required maintenance and were contrary to the quiet, remote lifestyle they wanted. After PV (and lead-acid batteries), the solar pioneers could enjoy light, music, small DC refrigerators for cold beer and fresh food, 12-volt TVs and other DC devices that were designed for camping.

After implementing solar PV into their own homesteads, the pioneers wanted to share their find with their neighbors. It was that desire to share the off-grid solar PV love that led to the early solar distributor businesses, publications and training centers — not a desire to make millions.

As businesses grew, cultures clashed. We hear the story of when corporate solar salespeople traveled to Humboldt County in three-piece suits to meet their best customers who are wearing T-shirts and jeans and carry the scent and cash of a “weedpreneur.”

While the majority of the film is about the people who wanted an off-grid lifestyle, Vetterli and Spies also interview the late 1990s PV code and policy pioneers. These solar people also wore suit, but they also saw grid-tied potential to make solar mainstream.

“Solar Roots” does a great job covering this off-grid to grid-tied transition, but the film only covers a fraction of the stories and people who inspired the multi-gigawatt and global solar industry that exists today, and that’s O.K. A single two-hour documentary could never cover all of the world’s solar pioneers, but we’re fortunate to have met these particular solar people. They may not have started the residential solar industry, but they certainly contributed to it.

Spies and Vetterli plan to release the entire documentary online in the coming months and have set up a mailing list at www.solar-roots.com for people to receive updates.

Tor “Solar Fred” Valenza is a former film and TV writer (Stargate SG-1, Dharma & Greg, The Dead Zone) and current chief solar public relations geek at UnThink Solar, a solar PR and communications firm.

Tor, thank you for this review. As you know, I was at the Grass Valley premiere too, and the film is a delight. Its account of how solar cells, battery charging, and inverters developed is the best I have seen: clear, even-handed, and grounded in the people who made it happen, with phenomenal historical footage.

Having spent those years at ARCO Solar, I smiled at the opening scene with the 35-watt ARCO module, the car battery, and the Grateful Dead tape. The homesteaders in this film put those early modules to work, solved practical field problems, and showed what solar could make possible.

The path runs from the 12-volt appliances of those early days to today’s 800-volt DC data centers, and it is far from finished. I look forward to sharing the film with students when it is released online, so the next generation can see whose work they are building on.

Congratulations to Jeff Spies, Jason Vetterli, TJ Kanczuzewski, and everyone who helped preserve this history.

#5800. Top Selling Electric Vehicles in the World — 32% EV Share in September!

Plugin vehicle registrations were up 3% year over year (YoY) globally in September, ending the month at around 2.16 million units, pretty darn close to the all-time record (2.17 million) set last December. Once again, BEVs (+8% YoY) and PHEVs (-9% YoY) are experiencing opposite dynamics. Pure electrics actually set a record high of 1.54 million registrations.

This means that, while plugin vehicle YTD growth remains low (+4% YoY), that is solely due to the PHEV blues (-10% YoY). BEVs are on their way back to normal (+12%).

Share-wise, September saw BEVs end the month at 23% share, with the plugin tally increasing to 32% if we add in PHEVs. This near-record performance pulled the 2026 plugin share to higher levels. BEVs are now at 18%, while plugin hybrids are at 7%. Therefore, 2026 EV share is now at 25%.

Looking at the best selling models, there’s some news. The leader Tesla Model Y hit a new all-time record (165,000 units), thanks to a 17% YoY growth rate. That larger six-seat version is doing wonders here.

The runner-up spot went to the Tesla Model 3. Despite being down 4% YoY, it picked up enough deliveries (65,000) to surpass both the small Geely Xingyuan, also known as the E2 or EX2 in export markets, and the BYD Song.

Regarding Geely’s hatchback, the export highlights were Brazil (2,724 units), Australia (1,211), Indonesia (1,890), and Thailand (1,759).

Despite being relegated to 4th, the BYD Song was actually the fastest grower among the top 5, rising 19% YoY and reaching 54,519 registrations. This was due to an export push (the highlights being Brazil — 5,892 units, Italy — 2,134 units, and Germany — 2,028 units) and the new-generation Ultra body. Flash charging capabilities promise to recharge the Song’s sales and make it a serious candidate, not only for the silver medal in 2026, but also as strong competition to the Tesla Model Y in off-peak months.

In a near-record month, records were plenty, with six models in the top 20 reaching record highs. Besides the aforementioned Tesla Model Y, all the other five record scorers were compact models — a coincidence or an indication of where the market is going?

The five record performers of September were:

Finally, a mention goes out to the surprise appearance of the Geely Starray / Galaxy 7, in 19th, thanks to 15,761 registrations, its best score in 20 months. With the compact PHEV crossover currently having a rather discreet career in China, export markets are giving it a new life, with the strongest markets being Australia (1,254 units in September), the UK (3,100), and … Russia (900).

Outside the top 20, there are a few models that deserve a mention. The big highlight was the landing with a bang of the MG 07, a fastback that kind of reminds me of the Porsche Taycan and Xiaomi SU7, but sits one category below, looking to compete with the Tesla Model 3 and the BYD Seal 06. It is using its sleeker silhouette to distance itself from those options. And with over 14,000 units in its landing month, one can say that MG’s new baby could become top 20 material soon.

Other Chinese EVs are also ramping up, like the Chery QQ3 (13,000 units), Xpeng L03 (12,000), BYD’s new Great Tang flagship SUV (11,000), and the Leapmotor A05 (B03 in export markets — 12,000 units). With Leapmotor on a roll, having placed two models in the top 20 in September, of all these models ramping up, I am betting that the small Leapmotor will be the one that will sit higher on the table.

Year to date, the leader Tesla Model Y is really in its own league, selling twice as many units as the runner-up BYD Song. The Model Y is so ubiquitous now that when regular people imagine what an electric car is, they think of it. Tesla’s crossover is the gold standard of EVs.

In the remaining podium positions, the #2 BYD Song and #3 Geely Xinguan held their positions, following the September high tide month of the Model 3. This is good news for both Chinese EVs. It seems the Model 3’s sales erosion continues (down 7% this year) despite the current EV sales rush caused by the future 2026 EV Salesperson of the Year, DJT.

Having said that, with 16,000 units separating Tesla’s Model 3 from the silver medal, not all is set in stone. But if I had to bet, I would say that BYD’s bread and butter model will stay in 2nd, closely followed by the Geely hatchback, and then the Model 3 ending in 4th.

(Adding context to this race, the Tesla Model 3 has been on every podium since 2018, while the Geely Xingyuan still hasn’t won any medal.)

Elsewhere, there were five changes in the table, with the first being the premium arm of BYD, Fang Cheng Bao, pulling its Land Rover Defender fighter (25,000 euros cheaper — this thing could be a huge success in Europe) into the 8th position. Below it, the Leapmotor A10/B03X continued its rise, with the crossover climbing to 12th.

Below the small Leapmotor, three compact crossovers were also on the rise. The BYD Yuan Plus/Atto 3 jumped two positions, to 14th, while the Nevo Q05 was up to 16th and its premium cousin, the Deepal S05, joined the table in 19th.

Compact crossovers are hot right now, so lucky for Changan to have two of the most competitive examples in its lineup. True, BYD also has two representatives in that category (Sealion 05 and Yuan Plus), but then again, that would have been expected — the depth of options that the Shenzhen make has is so big that the difficulty is finding a hole in BYD’s lineup. (I have a suggestion: affordable sports car? Like a Tesla Cybercab, but with pedals and a steering wheel?)

One of the biggest highlights was #2 Tesla having a record month (232,000 units, +9% YoY), but in a near record month, it wasn’t the only one to post strong results.

In fact, there were eight makes posting record results and four others performing year-best results.

#3 Geely also had something to celebrate in September, with its 130,000 registrations representing its best score of the year, mostly thanks to the fact that the Geely Xingyuan had some siblings to share the burden with this time (the Starray — 16,000 units; and the EX5 — 14,000 units). With B and C segments now covered, all they need is a strong seller in the D-segment.

Leapmotor continued on its record streak, with strong sellers in the B-segment (A10 and A05), C-segment (B10), and D-segment (C10). If there is anything currently holding back the startup, it is that the company lacks the dealership scale of BYD or Geely. But give it time….

Below the top four, we have something big happening — the rise and rise of Toyota (61,332 registrations, its fourth record in a row), which allowed it to end ahead of Volkswagen. Again. So, the Japanese globetrotter finished the month as the best selling legacy make. Again. All this despite Volkswagen placing a year-best result of 58,000 units.

Here, also, the establishment is being challenged, with the longtime leader of legacy brands being surpassed by a player that many still consider a sleeping giant.

And it’s not only Toyota’s RAV4 PHEV and BZ4X contributing to the volumes, as its Chinese operations are also delivering (the BZ3X compact crossover was the company’s third model to deliver over 10,000 units in September).

Another surprise was MG’s strong result, ending the month in 7th thanks to a record 57,000 registrations. Building on the MG 4 hachback’s success, if the MG 7 becomes the roaring success that its order list (over 50,000 units) and landing month point to, then Volkswagen will have yet another brand to worry about….

The #9 Kia is also on a record streak, having ended the month in 9th with some 50,000 registrations. With a freshly developed BEV lineup, the Korean is now sowing the seeds of an early investment in dedicated BEV platforms.

Still in Legacy Land, Mercedes has a similar story to Kia. With an ambitious BEV development plan, the German make is finally pushing its sales into record territory (40,000 units), and with new models coming in (GLA EV, C-Class EV, VLE, etc.), expect the Stuttgart brand to start getting in the rearview mirror of arch rival BMW. Maybe that pushes the Bavarian into more quickly deploying the next generation of BEVs (i4, iX1, iX2, i1, i2…).

But it seems BMW is in no hurry. I mean, even Audi had a year best result in September (34,000 units). BMW? Not even that.

The remaining highlights belonged to Chinese makes, with #15 Zeekr and #18 Chery posting record highs while #16 Fang Cheng Bao delivered year-best results. The highlight was Chery. Once known for being an ICE-heavy brand, it is now benefitting from the success of its new QQ3 EV (13,000 units) and joining the EV best seller rankings.

As for the year-to-date table, there was no major news in the top four positions, which already seem to be secured through the end of the year. However, below it, things are changing.

Rising Toyota surpassed Wuling and is now 6th. The Japanese make is some 20,000 units behind #5 Volkswagen, and while that might sound like a lot, there are still three months to go. With Toyota breaking records every passing month, it seems possible to shorten the distance by some 7,000 units every month and end ahead of Volkswagen by the end of December. To be continued…

Two more Chinese brands went up: SAIC’s MG jumped two positions, to #10, while Changan’s Qiyuan/Nevo mainstream brand joined the table at #20, much thanks to the success of the Q05 crossover.

Looking at OEMs, BYD (20.2%, down 0.1%) is stable in the lead, while runner-up Geely (9.8%, down from 9.9% in August) is holding a good distance over 3rd placed Tesla (8.7%, up 0.4% from August).

#4 Volkswagen Group (7%) is stable in 4th, just like SAIC (6.2%) is in 5th.

Behind the top 5, though, things are shaking up. Rising #6 Leapmotor (4.4%, up from 4.3% in August) is safe, but behind it, #7 Hyundai–Kia (3.9%, down 0.1%) is now being closely followed by #8 Chery (3.9%, up 0.1%). With just 3,000 units separating the two, we might see the Chinese OEM surpass the Korean in Q4.

Another OEM on the rise is Toyota (3%, up 0.1%), which joined the top 10 at the expense of BMW (2.9%, down 0.1%).

Looking just at BEVs, there were about 10.8 million registrations so far this year, or 71% of total plugin sales. A year ago, BEVs represented just 66% of plugin sales, so the PHEV slowdown is quite visible.

BYD (15.8%, up 0.3%) is at the top, benefitting from the OEM’s recent shift into BEVs. It is followed by Tesla (12.2%, up 0.6%). In 3rd place, we have Geely (8.8%), which is also safe in the last place of the podium, so the medal positions should be decided by now.

Meanwhile, there was a position change below the podium, with SAIC (6.3%) surpassing Volkswagen Group (6.3%, down 0.1%), so the Shanghai OEM is the new 4th placed. But with fewer than 4,000 units separating the two, anything can still happen.

Outside the top 5, Leapmotor (4.6%, up from 4.5% in August) climbed into the 6th position, at the expense of Hyundai–Kia (4.4% share, down 0.1%).

As an Easter Halloween Egg, here are the top sellers in the pickup truck, MPV, and sports car categories:

With the Ford F-150 EV now part of the past, a new pickup king is among us, and it is part of BYD’s stable. With the following two models selling roughly half of its volume, and a China market entry said to be coming soon, expect BYD’s Shark model to rule this category in the coming years.

Having said that, a certain Toyota Hilux BEV is ramping up, having delivered around the same number of units in September as the Cybertruck and the Ranger, so expect the 2027 race for the podium to be more entertaining, especially if a certain Slate ramps up production without major hiccups.

Now, this was a surprise. Media-friendly VW ID.Buzz? Way behind, at roughly half of the current winner. Giant Chinese minivans? The best one is only 3rd, closely followed by the Voyah Dreamer. MPVs are mostly surviving extinction because of Asian markets, and it shows. The category leader is a midsize MPV, and while its main market is its domestic China, others, like Thailand and Indonesia, where it’s known as Darion, also provide significant volumes to Wuling’s MPV.

But an even bigger surprise is the 2nd placed Vinfast MPV 7 (Limo Green in ride hailing/taxi spec). The compact seven seater, which is a sort of MPV disguised as a crossover (not unlike certain NIO designs), has most of its sales coming from native Vietnam, but some export markets are also contributing to the volume, like India, Indonesia, and the Philippines.

This is a category in crisis. With no new players; the 2026 winner, the Dodge Charger, experiencing crashing sales (down 89% YoY); and the Ferrari 296 also suffering from shrinking sales, the only highlight is the Mercedes CLE, which went from 3rd last year to it current uncontested leadership position.

Still, expect this category to receive some much needed fresh metal in 2027, like the Denza Z (of course BYD had to get into the action…), the Porsche 718 twins (Boxster and Cayman), Audi’s take on the Porsche 718 (Audi TT?), and the next-generation Alpine A110.

Always interested in the auto industry, particularly in electric cars, Jose has pioneered on documenting the plug-in sales evolution through the EV Sales blog, allowing him to gain an expert view on where EVs are right now and where they are headed in the future. Extending that work and expertise, Jose was co-founder of EV-Volumes and currently works with the European Alternative Fuels Observatory on EV sales matters.

#5798. Utilities should consider ‘taking a breather’ from rate cases: Minnesota PUC vice chair

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

“Filing rate cases right now is really painful,” Joseph Sullivan told Utility Dive in an interview. He also discussed his concerns with overly complex data center cost allocation.

Utility Dive met with Minnesota Public Utilities Commission Vice Chair Joseph Sullivan on Monday at the National Association of Regulatory Utility Commissioners’ annual meeting in San Juan, Puerto Rico.

We wanted to hear his perspective on the top challenges facing regulators in Minnesota, home to utilities like Minnesota Power, Otter Tail Power and Xcel Energy’s Northern States Power-Minnesota.

Sullivan joined the PUC in April 2020, and was reappointed by Gov. Tim Walz in January to a second six-year term.

This interview has been edited for length and clarity.

UTILITY DIVE: What are the top challenges you're facing at the commission?

Joseph Sullivan: Certainly affordability. In recent rate cases, it's abundantly clear that many people are struggling to pay their bills, and they're going to struggle to pay a new higher bill.

On top of that, you have the compounding impact of the system that's changing. We have a system that is getting older. We have older power plants that need to be replaced. We have clean energy targets and requirements in our state.

And then you put on top of that new large loads that are coming, so we have transmission lines that are coming in. Many people are looking at these transmission lines, and they say, "Why is this transmission line going in? Is it going to increase the bills that I pay?”

In addition, people don't trust the institutions. That's becoming more and more evident and more difficult. You can see that in our hearings, which are becoming more contentious. What we receive in the dockets and the record is becoming more and more contentious, and in some cases, very worrisome.

How important is transparency in terms of rate cases, large loads?

Transparency has certainly been an issue with some of the data centers that have been proposed. People are concerned about what is happening within their community.

I would say, though, it's not just transparency that's an issue. It's context. People oftentimes just don't understand how the system works, and why would they?

But when you add that lack of transparency, plus the lack of contextual knowledge about the system, plus a degradation of faith in the institutions — it ends up being a really foul mix.

On the affordability issue, what can the commission do?

The biggest affordability program that our state has are our demand side management programs. They are taking millions of megawatt hours out of the system every year, and they're making us more productive. If you put those megawatt hours back in the system, our system would be about 20% larger. So that is a very real savings to people. All ratepayers benefit because we have to build fewer power plants and transmission lines. So, that's one of our main go-tos.

We have put a lot of work into setting up programs that our gas utilities run and our electric utilities run to help people avoid being disconnected, to help them with the arrearages.

When our legislature passed data center legislation last year, they put in a component that the data centers would have to pay into the state's weatherization fund.

What about things within a rate case: return on equity, debt to equity ratios?

Why that didn't spring immediately to mind is because that's what we do. Every rate case is about prudency. Is this expenditure just and reasonable, and therefore prudent? We focus like a laser beam on that.

Under our state statutes and under my thinking of how our law is, you don't include affordability in that ROE determination.

Is there anything that the utilities could be doing better to meet some of these challenges?

This is going to be really hard for them, but filing rate cases right now is really painful. I recognize that they have capital needs. But taking a breather from rate cases could be very helpful. We've had a lot of rate cases in the last year. They come at a — politically — really not great time.

Things that they could do also would be to be supportive of affordability programs that are going to be helpful to keeping people connected or paying down arrearages. I want the utilities to be creative in how they are addressing affordability.

The utilities also have to really clearly articulate that they are primarily investing in resources without fuel clauses, that don't have a fuel impact. Like, “We're investing in resources that are less expensive in other parts of your bill.”

And keeping rate increases to below the rate of inflation.

What effect do you think the midterms may have on state energy policy (In Minnesota, Democrats maintained control of the governor’s seat and Senate and took full control of the House in the midterm elections)?

For six of the last eight years, we've basically been on Democratic control. We already have a 100% carbon-free statute. We have our new energy efficiency framework. A lot of these big pieces are already set in stone, and we're in implementation mode on them.

The discussion in Minnesota will certainly be on data centers. Data center moratorium legislation was offered last year, and I think it'll be offered again this year. The politics has definitely changed, and some of those initiatives may have more momentum.

For me, the interesting question will be do the advocates who are more interested in small solar, are they on the ascendancy? Or are the people who are supportive of the vertically integrated model, are they on the ascendancy? Restructuring versus continuing the regulated approach — where does that land?

How are things going with the Midcontinent Independent System Operator?

In general, MISO is doing a good job with juggling between a lot of stakeholders.

We've continued on the pathway with the [Long-Range Transmission Planning] Tranche One [transmission projects]. Minnesota is done with our permitting for Tranche One. We're deep into our permitting for Tranche 2.1. So MISO has been moving forward — not as fast as I'd like — approving new transmission.

What I am worried about with MISO right now is the cost allocation for large loads, and in particular, I'm concerned that MISO has developed, along with the transmission owners, a very complicated cost allocation framework. We don't understand how it is consistent with not just Minnesota state law, but with what the Trump administration has said [on ratepayer protection], with where it appears that Congress is going, with making sure that data centers are paying both their incremental and their embedded costs.

Major work by regional planners is focused on flexible access to existing transmission. Some say greater emphasis on advanced transmission technologies can quickly and cost-effectively create room for new large loads.

#5795. Most generators prepared for extreme cold, but risks remain: NERC

Let Utility Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

Despite improvements to cold-weather operations, the grid “continues to face serious reliability risks during the winter period,” NERC President and CEO Jim Robb said.

Almost all U.S. power generators are capable of operating in extreme cold, with a relative few facing known constraints that could keep them from reinforcing their ability to produce electricity during winter storms, the North American Electric Reliability Corp. said Thursday.

“Industry is rising to the challenge of protecting the grid during periods of extreme cold,” NERC President and CEO Jim Robb said in a statement.

The reliability watchdog last month filed its “Cold Weather Data Annual Report” with the Federal Energy Regulatory Commission, tallying the industry’s progress in implementing new winter weatherization requirements. About 94% of the bulk power system’s total capacity could be called on to operate below 32 degrees, thus NERC requires those units to implement protective measures. Relatively few units experienced reliability issues requiring corrective action plans, NERC said.

Units constituting 4.3% of capacity are implementing corrective action plans to address reliability issues, and 60% of those plans will be completed before this upcoming winter, NERC said. “A small percentage of units,” about 7.4% of the total capacity, “declared a constraint that precluded them from implementing corrective actions.”

The findings “highlight measurable improvements in the electric industry’s cold weather preparedness, while underscoring the need for continued vigilance to address winter reliability risks,” NERC said.

The risk from extended corrective action plans and cold weather constraints “is limited,” NERC said. “Based on the data reported this year, 99% of the total net winter capacity in the continental U.S. responded that they were able to operate” at calculated extreme cold weather temperatures.

Generator owners reported a total net winter capacity of 1,154,231 MW in the continental United States. That’s a 7% increase over the winter 2024-2025 reporting period, NERC said. Owners reported cold weather constraints for 633 units comprising 85,691 MW, with known limitations of wind accounting for 71% of units reporting a constraint.

“Coupled with our recent joint report with FERC on winter system performance, it’s clear that improved preparation is making a difference with no significant system disruptions over the past winter,” Robb said. “While that is good news, the bulk power system continues to face serious reliability risks during the winter period.”

Milder temperatures in some regions could provide relief this winter as well. The El Niño weather pattern is likely to result in warmer-than-average temperatures in the northern U.S., according to the National Oceanic and Atmospheric Administration. Wetter-than-average conditions are likely across the southern U.S., from California to Florida, and along the East Coast, NOAA said.

Cold weather grid operating standards have been a priority for FERC and NERC since Winter Storm Uri in 2021, when about 4.5 million Texans lost power because power plants were unable to run.

PJM Interconnection, the grid operator for 13 states and the District of Columbia, on Thursday said it expects to have 182,426 MW of operational capacity to serve a forecasted peak electricity demand of approximately 147,500 MW, plus a forecast average of approximately 3,200 MW of electricity exports to neighboring systems.

That peak, driven by demand growth in the region, would set a new winter record if reached, the grid operator said. PJM’s ability to reliably serve winter demand “will depend on continued preparation and strong generator performance,” the grid operator said. “This is especially important in areas where transmission constraints limit the ability to deliver electricity from other parts of the system,” particularly including some zones in Maryland, Virginia and the District of Columbia.

ReliabilityFirst, the regional entity which monitors reliability along most of the East Coast, including the PJM grid, has about 13% of its total net winter generating capacity under a cold weather constraint, NERC noted. About 12% of the total net winter capacity in the Midwest Reliability Organization region reported a generator cold weather constraint, according to the report. In the Northeast Power Coordinating Council territory, about 4% of the total net winter capacity reported a generator cold weather constraint.

"Our goal is to coordinate operations with the generation and transmission owners and proactively manage the system to perform at high levels when consumers need energy the most," Michael Bryson, PJM senior vice president of operations, said in a statement.
